--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page follows the title leaf. The signature mark "):(" indicates it is part of the introductory front matter. The catchword "suspicio-" at the bottom suggests the next page begins with a word relating to "suspicion" or "doubt," likely "suspicionem."</meta>

### TO THE
## MOST AUGUST 
## EMPEROR
# RUDOLPH THE SECOND
## THE PREFACE OF
### *TYCHO BRAHE.*

**A**STRONOMY IS THE MOST ANCIENT SCIENCE, granted by God to the human race from the time of Adam the First-Formed <note>original: "Protoplasto"</note>, and by far the most excellent, just as celestial and sublime things surpass these earthly and inferior ones. This Divine Astronomy, I say, draws its origin from the very senses of the eyes—which observe the many-wandering successions of the stars—as far as external matters are concerned. From the very beginning of the world, it has occupied the spirits and the intellects of the most excellent men. So great indeed is the majesty of God, the Best and Thrice-Greatest, that the wisdom of His works can be exhausted by no creature. However, since the intuition of the eye alone could not grasp all those mysteries of the wondrous celestial theater, nor the intricate apparent variations, with the necessary subtlety and accuracy, various craftsmen in every age devised means and instruments by which the sight might be aided in perceiving the hidden motions of the stars. 

Hence come those pillars which Josephus, the writer of Jewish history, records that the descendants of Adam erected in Syria, and upon which they inscribed their discoveries for the memory of posterity. To this also belong the very high and costly Pyramids of the Egyptians and other nations, and many other machines constructed for this use by ancient kings, such as those that were once seen in India, Syria, Arabia, Chaldea, Ethiopia, and Egypt—especially there in the Alexandrian portico—as well as elsewhere in the surrounding regions where men lived who were diligently intent upon the science of the stars. For this is the first requirement of all: that in astronomical matters, many long-term observations be taken with suitable instruments that are not subject to error. These are afterward digested through Geometry, by devising convenient hypotheses, into continuous quantities and circular, uniform motion (which celestial things naturally and without intermission seek and practice); then, through Arithmetic, they are digested into discrete quantities, so that the circuits and locations of the celestial bodies may be established for any given time. 

Among all those who have labored strenuously in this matter, there have reached us at least the records of those things set down by <term>Timocharis<gloss>An early 3rd-century BCE Greek astronomer who made some of the earliest recorded observations.</gloss></term>, Hipparchus, Ptolemy, Albategnius <note>original: "Albategnio," referring to the 9th-century Arab astronomer Al-Battani.</note>, King Alfonso, and in the previous age, Copernicus; although the traditions of the two preceding these survive only through some account by Ptolemy. Which instruments these men chiefly used in measuring the phenomena of the stars is somewhat clear from their writings. Among these, I find three principal ones: <term>Parallactic Rules<gloss>Also known as the triquetrum, used for determining the zenith distances and altitudes of stars.</gloss></term>, <term>Zodiacal Armillaries<gloss>A series of nested metal rings used to determine the coordinates of celestial bodies relative to the ecliptic.</gloss></term>, and the <term>Torquetum<gloss>A medieval instrument used to transform coordinates between the horizon, equatorial, and ecliptic systems.</gloss></term>, which was used more by the Arabs (as were <term>plane astrolabes<gloss>Handheld devices used for telling time and solving problems related to the position of the Sun and stars.</gloss></term>); the others are of less importance. 

Yet perhaps there were many other things which, not being committed to writing, did not reach us; these could easily have perished (which is to be deplored) in such great and frequent confusion and change of the world's stage, with so many wars and devastations creeping in from time to time. More recent men have added the <term>Quadrant<gloss>A 90-degree arc used to measure the altitude of celestial objects.</gloss></term>, the <term>Radius<gloss>Often called the Cross-staff or Jacob's staff, used for measuring angles between stars.</gloss></term>, and the <term>Astronomical Ring<gloss>A portable sundial or observational tool consisting of several rings.</gloss></term>, as well as others of even less esteem. However, since the motions of the stars, when accurately considered in our age, do not at all show themselves to be as the calculation derived from the observations of any craftsmen (whether ancient or recent) suggests, it is not without reason—

<summary>This page serves as the opening of Tycho Brahe's dedication to Emperor Rudolph II, tracing the divine and ancient origins of astronomy from Adam through the great civilizations of antiquity. Tycho argues that the inaccuracies of previous astronomical tables necessitate the creation of new, more precise instruments to properly observe the "celestial theater."</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Rudolph II, history of astronomy, astronomical instruments, Ptolemy, Copernicus, observations, scientific methodology</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe's preface to his description of astronomical instruments. It follows the previous page's discussion on the history of astronomical observation, picking up from the catchword "suspicion" regarding the accuracy of older records.</meta>

->PREFACE<-

# PREFACE

stirs the suspicion that the means and instruments which they used during their observations were not entirely free from every fault. This is sufficiently proven by the very positions of the fixed stars, which are arranged differently in relation to one another than is indicated by the <term>Hipparchian</term> <note>referring to Hipparchus, the Greek astronomer who produced the first known star catalog</note> star-list revised by <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, whose second-century work the Almagest was the standard astronomical text for over a millennium</note> and passed down to us from that source, even if other matters are ignored. This was true even for the ancients, whose Emperors and Kings liberally supplied the funds for making such instruments, so that they might be magnificently constructed from solid metal and held to a proper, convenient size. It is even more true of those instruments which <term>Copernicus</term> <note>Nicolaus Copernicus, the astronomer who proposed the Sun-centered model of the universe</note> prepared in the previous age at his own private expense; that they were sufficient neither in the solidity of their material nor in the size required for their intended uses is clear partly from his work *On the <term>Revolutions</term>*, and partly from those instruments of his which still survive. One of these, namely a <term>Parallactic</term> instrument <note>original: "Parallaticum"; also known as a triquetrum, used for measuring the altitudes of stars</note> made entirely of wood and not sufficiently fit for observations, is in my possession.

Therefore, having thought very anxiously since my youth whether this inconvenience could be remedied, I subsequently saw to it that various astronomical instruments were constructed in succession, with incredible diligence and expense. By these, it would be possible to scrutinize the appearances of the stars more exactly than was practiced by our predecessors (may no offense be taken at the word). Although those instruments we made in our youth—when neither our knowledge nor our judgment was sufficiently mature—did not reach the intended mark with great exactness, yet as a more serious age followed, along with more frequent experience, I finally invented other larger and more excellent instruments. I saw to it that these were skillfully crafted, such as perhaps hardly any previous age has seen, and which perform their intended task with the highest precision and certainty.

I have, however, constructed various and multiple types, and for obvious reasons. First, so that by inquiring into the same thing in different ways, I might prove the observation to be free from all error; and also, so that if any one of them should develop a hidden flaw (as <term>Ptolemy</term> seems to complain about certain instruments made of solid metal that once stood in the <term>Alexandrian</term> portico), others would be ready at hand to correct it and prove the exact certainty. Furthermore, this allows the keenness of the observers in sighting and their diligence in counting—of whom there ought to be at least six or eight—to be examined by comparing them with one another, without any of them knowing what the other has found. Not to mention that the variety itself makes the observers more diligent and eager, and is a delight in itself. Added to this is the fact that among the things to be observed, some are better investigated by certain machines rather than others: some require only known <term>Altitudes</term> <note>the height of a star above the horizon</note>, some these together with <term>Azimuths</term> <note>the horizontal angle of a star from the North or South</note>, some require only distances, some <term>Declinations</term> <note>the celestial equivalent of latitude</note>, some <term>Equatorial Distances</term>, others are suitable for obtaining longitudes and latitudes, and others for obtaining <term>Right Ascensions</term> <note>the celestial equivalent of longitude</note> and <term>Declinations</term> simultaneously, and so on for the rest, if there are any more that serve <term>Celestial Observation</term> in any way. Thus, for this reason alone, a large number and variety of instruments are entirely necessary.

But since those things which are devised and crafted by men for the use of the Arts ought to be shared, for the sake of the kind fellowship of the human race and the propagation of the Arts to posterity, I did not think such sublime and useful inventions should be reserved for myself alone. Rather, they should be shared with others, if any may be found anywhere (as I hope) who are touched by such an arduous concern. Therefore, I wished for these instruments, which I have used until now, to be sketched and represented with their own images (as far as could be provided so quickly) and sent to the press to be published, with a brief explanation added to each, as will appear in the following pages.

In arranging them, I have followed this method above all: first, I place those that examine the <term>Altitudes</term> and <term>Azimuths</term> of the stars, of which some show only <term>Altitudes</term>, and some show both these and <term>Azimuths</term>; here also the smaller and less important ones come first. The others, which are more specialized and provide exact precision, follow; and with these, the first eight instruments are completed. However, at the end, I will also show and commemorate a certain very large <term>Quadrant</term> <note>a quarter-circle instrument for measuring angles</note> intended only for Altitudes, which I once used in my youth at <term>Augsburg</term> <note>original: "Augustæ Vindelicorum"</note>. The reason why this was done will be stated later. Then follow the <term>Armillary</term> <note>from the Latin 'armilla' meaning bracelet; instruments made of nested rings</note> instruments. Among these, the first has <term>Zodiacal</term> rings, showing the longitudes and latitudes of the stars with greater speed and certainty than was perhaps practiced by the ancients. But since these, for certain reasons explained in that place, do not "hit the mark" (as they say), three other <term>Equinoctial Armillaries</term> follow. The first of these consists of three circles and an axis; the second requires four along with the axis. But the third and largest, containing a circle-and-a-half and a smooth axis, manages the whole business as in the others; it reveals both <term>Declinations</term> and <term>Right Ascensions</term>, and the distance from the Meridian, with the greatest subtlety. In the third place, I have arranged those instruments that serve for measuring the gaps between the stars, both for...

<summary>Tycho Brahe critiques the imprecise instruments of his predecessors, including Ptolemy and Copernicus, and explains his decision to build a vast collection of high-precision tools. He details his methodology, emphasizing that using multiple specialized instruments and several independent observers ensures the highest level of astronomical accuracy.</summary>

<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Copernicus, astronomical instruments, precision, quadrants, armillary spheres, Ptolemy, Augsburg, observation methodology</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe's preface to his "Astronomiae Instauratae Mechanica" (Instruments for the Restoration of Astronomy). On the previous page, Brahe discussed the shortcomings of historical instruments, including those of Copernicus. Here, he shifts to describing the specific instruments detailed in this volume and formalizes his dedication to Emperor Rudolf II.</meta>

TO THE EMPEROR.

## TO THE EMPEROR.

...intended for obtaining greater and smaller distances, provided they do not exceed the sixth part of a circle. There are four of these instruments used for distances, although I have more ready at hand which I could not have depicted and engraved so quickly; among these is one which measures the distances of the stars even when they are far greater than a sixth part of the circle, extending even to a full semicircle. Finally, a certain instrument is included after the previous group which I used for that most wonderful New Star of the year 1572 <note>This refers to the supernova in the constellation Cassiopeia which Tycho observed, proving that the heavens were not unchangeable as Aristotle had claimed</note>, specifically for recording its altitudes when it was in a more inclined position. I have also included that huge Quadrant, which I mentioned before, which we oversaw the construction of many years ago near <term>Augsburg</term> <note>original: "Augustam Vindelicorum"</note> in the garden of the Consul **Paul Hainzel** <note>A mayor of Augsburg and a patron of astronomy who funded the construction of Tycho's massive 19-foot quadrant</note>, a man most fond of these arts during his lifetime. Since that instrument no longer exists there, having lasted only five years, I did not wish to grant it a place among the other instruments for measuring altitudes, but instead placed it at the end for that reason. After this, I have included a certain Steel Quadrant, which I discussed in the earlier sections, though it is arranged differently there. Once these are finished, a brief description of others among my instruments is added—those which I have ready but have not yet been drawn or displayed—where I also add some indication of those things which I still intend to refine with wonderful brevity. I shall provide a fuller explanation of each of these, both those ready at hand and others to be refined in due time as opportunity allows; for now, striving for brevity, and since the illustrations of them were not ready, I wished to touch upon at least a few points concerning this business. Finally, as a finishing touch, we describe that great <term>Brass Globe</term> <note>original: "Globum Orichalcicum"; Tycho's famous globe, over 1.5 meters in diameter, on which he engraved the positions of 1,000 stars</note>, which encompasses a thousand fixed stars, restored by us with the greatest diligence and precision over many years; this is explained insofar as such a laborious object, serving many uses, can be elucidated in few words. And so we bring the description of the instruments to an end. To all of these, we have annexed a certain record of those things which have been completed by us so far, and those which remain to be finished in the future; with the addition of a few letters from Famous Men, commending the work we are undertaking. Furthermore, in the place of a certain appendix, it seemed good to add representations and explanations of the architectural works, which reveal to the eyes the structures we ordered and completed for these uses in previous years <note>referring to his observatories, Uraniborg and Stjerneborg, on the island of Hven</note>. Although this might seem unnecessary to some at this point—since those who wish to apply serious care to these things and are capable of managing the expenses will easily find for themselves the plans for suitable buildings, especially by taking advantage of the location—nevertheless, so that I might show some idea according to what has been continually refined by us regarding these matters, I judged it not entirely irrelevant to include these astronomical buildings represented together. And these are nearly all the things by which this book is completed, as will be clear to the reader upon looking inside. If any other things are mixed in or added, the prudent reader will easily perceive them for himself without needing me as a guide.

To YOU, however, most August Emperor, **RUDOLF THE SECOND**, most Merciful Lord, I offer, dedicate, and consecrate this and our other astronomical labors with a humble spirit and an intention studious of the public good. For just as Your Imperial Majesty, in your supreme preeminence, far excels other inferior men, so you are not unaware that it is the duty of your dignity to emulate the Heavens and Celestial things, which immensely surpass earthly and common things: therefore these sublime studies should be held in high value and fostered. That ancient poet **Manilius** <note>Marcus Manilius, a 1st-century AD Roman poet who wrote "Astronomica"</note> seems to have looked to this, when writing to the second Monarch of the Roman Empire <note>Emperor Augustus</note> about this Celestial art, he decorated it not undeservedly with this praise:

->*Royal minds were the first it deigned to move,*<-
->*Touching the summits of things nearest to Heaven.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Regales animos primùm dignata movere est / Proxima tangentes Cælo fastigia rerum."</note>

To YOU, therefore, most August Emperor, I trust that these things—which for many years now, almost from my youth, I have performed with untiring labor and incredible expense, so that this divine and most Royal science of Astronomy, which holds the first place above all others, might at last be restored to its integrity and handed down to Posterity more corrected than ever before—will be graciously accepted, and that both myself and these noble studies I treat will be most highly commended to you in the future: so that the honor and Majesty of GOD the Best and Greatest, which shines forth in Celestial things above all other visible things of this most ample theater of the World, might be more rightly known, and might be further magnified and respected among the inhabitants of the Earth; and moreover that the memory and fame of Your Imperial Majesty might be preserved, because you supported these things so excellent and almost chief among worldly 

<meta>catchword: matters (cipua)</meta>

<summary>Tycho Brahe concludes the description of his instruments and architectural works, then formally dedicates the entire volume to Emperor Rudolf II, emphasizing that astronomy is a pursuit fit for the highest royalty.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page concludes Tycho Brahe’s dedication to Emperor Rudolf II. It transitions from a description of his instruments to a philosophical justification for studying the stars—arguing that celestial study offers a path to eternal fame—and concludes with a formal New Year’s greeting and the date and location of the writing: December 31, 1597 (for the year 1598), at Wandsbek.</meta>

# PREFACE TO THE EMPEROR

...the most important things, and that you do not feel it a burden to preserve, protect, and promote them, so that to all Posterity, as long as the Sun and the Stars shall last, your name may shine and endure more illustriously. Since it is only from these things which are perpetual and constant, such as the Celestial things, that one is granted the ability to acquire a perpetual and constant name and glory. Other sublunary <note>Referring to the region "below the moon," which in Aristotelian physics was the realm of change, decay, and imperfection, as opposed to the perfect and unchanging heavens</note> and earthly things, just as they are subject to manifold changes, so too whatever glory is prepared from them is fleeting, wandering, and inconsistent; it is no rival to eternity, unlike these Celestial things of which I speak. 

May that supreme and unfathomable <note>original: "incompræhensibilis"</note> author of these things deign to grant Your <term>Imperial Majesty</term> <note>original: "Majestati tuæ Cæsareæ"</note> a good mind, bodily health, peace, and an abundance of the goods of this World, along with a tranquil and happy empire, especially for His own glory and the illumination of the things created by Him, and for the benefit of your subjects—which I humbly and from my heart pray and vow. May Your Imperial Majesty fare well, and may you graciously accept this small New Year's gift <note>original: "strenulam"</note>, which I present with all the strength and humble spirit and reverence I can muster.

->Given from the Rantzau Castle at **Wandsbek** <note>The estate of Tycho's friend and patron Heinrich Rantzau, where Tycho lived after leaving Denmark</note>, which is near<-
->**Hamburg**, on the borders of Germany and the **Cimbrian<-
->Peninsula** <note>original: "Cimbricæ Chersonesi," the Jutland peninsula comprising modern mainland Denmark and Schleswig-Holstein</note>, on the day before the Kalends of January, in the YEAR<-
->of the Common Christian Era,<-
->1598.<- <note>original: "CIƆ. IƆ. IIC." Using an older form of Roman numerals where CIƆ represents 1000, IƆ represents 500, and IIC represents 98 (100 minus 2)</note>

<summary>Tycho Brahe concludes his dedication to Emperor Rudolf II by arguing that celestial science brings eternal glory, unlike earthly pursuits, and offers the book as a New Year's gift from his exile in Wandsbek.</summary>
<keywords>Rudolf II, immortality, sublunary, Wandsbek, Hamburg, Cimbrian Peninsula, 1598, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page follows the conclusion of Tycho Brahe’s dedication to Emperor Rudolf II. The previous page ended with a New Year’s greeting from Wandsbek in 1597. This page begins a dedicatory poem by Tycho’s relative, Holger Rosenkrantz, defending Tycho against his critics as he presents his astronomical achievements.</meta>

<meta>catchword: WHICH</meta>

### TO THE
### NOBLE MAN
# TYCHO BRAHE
### OF KNUDSTRUP, HIS MOST
### BELOVED KINSMAN AND 
### RELATIVE;

### AGAINST
## THE ZOILI,
<note>A "Zoilus" (plural Zoili) refers to a harsh, envious, or pedantic critic, named after the ancient Greek grammarian known for his critiques of Homer.</note>
### THE MALEVOLENT AND THE IGNORANT.

**U** *rania* <note>The Muse of Astronomy in Greek mythology.</note> *allows you, Tycho, to know the hidden recesses—though they be neglected—where the great Gods are granted to dwell in the high Ether* <note>The "Aether" was believed to be the pure, upper air or the substance that filled the celestial spheres.</note>*, and the unknown paths of the Sky and their eternal motions. As she reveals these sacred mysteries, around which she sees the high axes of the world revolve, the ignorant and envious crowd, pausing for nothing, receives them with jeers. With a dull-witted amazement, they pluck at and harass what they do not yet understand; they meditate on frauds and cunning.*

*They devise unjust schemes and hurl unspeakable weapons, which nevertheless shatter against a solid shield, though they might try by chance to disturb your illustrious labors. But virtue is always sufficient unto itself from every side, and it does not dwell upon the trifles and empty customs of men. Behold, now (marvelously) you produce instruments* <note>original: "Organa"</note> *conforming to Divine rites, by which you are able, through your own genius, to bring the high Heaven under your command. Be gone, you profane ones!*

*These are the works of virtue; they are rivals to the face of the Gorgon* <note>In mythology, the gaze of the Gorgon Medusa turned onlookers to stone; Rosenkrantz suggests Tycho’s instruments strike critics with a similar paralyzing awe.</note> *; they are even greater. For envy and hatred, poorly conscious of their own wicked minds, care nothing for things that are luminous, nor for the things by which they are spurred. At last, shaken by the mere sight of these things, they stand stunned, silent, and stiff as rocks. Indeed, who could deny that the ignorant—to whom these divinely inspired* <note>original: "Enthea"</note> *things seem worthless—are but trunks of trees, or beasts in a human body?*

*But you, Tycho, bring yourself and your deeds into the innermost shrines of the gods. Laugh from on high at the delusions of the filthy world, its lowly clamor, and the hearts ignorant of the fate which the Gods dispense by a just path. Standing on the shallow shore, despise the waves, the rains, and the blasts of wind.*

->HOLGER ROSENKRANTZ<-
->Son of G. F., of Rosenholm<-
<note>Holger Rosenkrantz (1574–1642) was a Danish nobleman, theologian, and a close younger relative of Tycho Brahe. He was a prominent scholar who studied at Tycho's observatory, Uraniborg.</note>

# THE SMALL QUADRANT
## OF GILDED BRASS.

---

<note>This heading introduces one of the portable precision instruments designed by Tycho Brahe. While many of his instruments were massive and fixed in masonry to ensure stability, this "Small Quadrant" was crafted for flexibility and ease of use without sacrificing accuracy.</note>

<term>Quadrant<gloss>An astronomical instrument used to measure the altitudes of celestial bodies, consisting of a graduated arc of 90 degrees (a quarter-circle) and a sighting mechanism.</gloss></term>

<term>Orichalcum<gloss>A golden-colored alloy, typically brass, which was highly valued in the Renaissance for the construction of scientific instruments due to its durability and resistance to corrosion.</gloss></term>

<note>original: "Quadrans Minor Orichalcicus Inauratus" — Small Gilded Brass Quadrant. The term "inauratus" indicates that the brass was gilded, or plated in gold, both for aesthetic beauty and to protect the metal from the elements during observations.</note>

<meta>Catchword: EXPLANATION</meta> <note>original: "EXPLI", likely an abbreviation for "Explicatio" or "Explanation," indicating that the following page provides the detailed technical breakdown and instructions for using the instrument.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the formal title and introductory heading for the Small Gilded Brass Quadrant, marking the shift from the book's introductory material to the technical descriptions of Tycho Brahe’s astronomical instruments.</summary>

<keywords>Small Quadrant, Orichalcum, Brass, Gilded Instruments, Tycho Brahe, Astronomy, Renaissance Science</keywords>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

A quadrant of modest size was constructed by us many years ago in the form shown here. It consists of a solid plate of <term>Orichalcum<gloss>A golden-colored brass alloy highly prized for its durability and beauty in scientific instruments.</gloss></term> of considerable thickness, measuring one cubit from the Center **A** to the circumference **B D C**. Every part of it is gilded by means of gold, mercury, and fire <note>original: "Auri hydrargyri & ignis beneficio" — This describes the process of fire-gilding, where a gold-mercury amalgam is applied and heated to create a permanent, lustrous finish.</note>, so that it remains more beautiful and clean, and is not subject to the tarnish that brass otherwise gradually collects unless it is frequently cleaned. Its outer limb **B D C** is subdivided in the common manner into 90 parts <note>degrees</note>, and each of these again into 12, so that this subdivision shows intervals of five minutes each. Within this is another distribution which the most famous Spanish mathematician, Pedro Nunes <note>original: "Petrus Nonnius"</note>, described in his learned book *On Twilight* <note>original: "de Crepusculis"</note>, believing it to have been used by Ptolemy in the past. This, however, is hardly worthy of belief. It is designed such that 44 smaller arcs are drawn within the outermost quadrant, following one another in succession. The outermost of these is divided into 89 parts; the following into 88; the third into 87; and so on, until the last and innermost is reached, which has 46 portions. These are represented here in the illustration. Its sighting rule **D A** has sights <note>original: "pinnacidia"</note> at **A** and **D** prepared in the usual way with holes; though at the lower sight at **A**, parallel slits can sometimes be applied to the upper one, according to our custom, so that the alignment becomes easier and more exact. A plumb line **E F G** is hung from the back, so that by its help one may know when the quadrant is correctly positioned; so that its lower side **A B** exactly reflects the equilibrium of the Horizon, and the other side **A C** the zenith above our heads; and the entire plane of the quadrant matches the plane of some vertical circle. That the quadrant may be so correctly directed is achieved partly by the screws it has near the lower arrangement of the pedestal, marked with the letters **S**, and partly by the screw at **O M**, which raises and lowers the quadrant itself until the plumb line shows that it is properly ordered. For it stands on a pedestal **N M O P Q R S T**, which is made entirely of pure iron along with the other requirements, where it is attached at the back near **H**. This support is somewhat movable near **N**, so that the quadrant can be moved up and down by the screw **O M**. For the iron rules **M N** and **O N** can be tightened or widened by means of that same screw **O M**; thus the quadrant can be raised or lowered slightly as needed. It can also be turned around in a concave channel **N P** toward any quarter of the sky desired, while the entire lower part of the pedestal, marked by the letters **R S Q T**, remains firm. This lower portion provides supports in the form you see, so that the quadrant stands firmly on its four screws indicated by the letters **S T**. These screws, as I said, direct the whole instrument into its proper position. These screws rest on small iron plates placed underneath near **T**; while these remain stationary on a table **V X Y Z**, the screws may be turned as desired. Let these general points regarding the quadrant and its pedestal suffice.

However, the picture **L K** which is somewhat visible within the center and the smallest quadrant, contained in a circular form, was engraved for the sake of ornament and also so that it might teach something through its symbolic meaning <note>original: "Hieroglyphicâ fignificatione"</note>, since otherwise that space would be empty. In this illustration (to mention this in passing), next to a tree that is green and leafy on one side sits a young man crowned with laurel upon a square stone. He holds a celestial globe in one hand and a book in the other, extending his feet upon the green grass and herbs covering the roots of the tree. On the other side, namely the left side of the tree, the root is withered and the branches are sterile and lacking leaves. Among these is a table, holding something of almost everything that is held in high price by mortals on this earthly stage, such as: a small chest full of coins, scepters, crowns, family coats of arms, golden necklaces, gems, women's finery, cups, papers, dice, and similar things. All these a certain skeleton, representing death, surrounds with its hands and feet, as if snatching them for itself. Over the whole figure is written a half-verse of poetry which explains the allegorical meaning: **WE LIVE BY OUR GENIUS; THE REST SHALL BELONG TO DEATH** <note>original: "VIVIMUS INGENIO COETERA MORTIS ERUNT"</note>. Thus, the first part of the half-verse hangs from the branches on the right and green side of the tree where that philosophizing youth sits, as if written on a scroll; the other portion follows on the other side. By these we wished to suggest that solid learning—especially the sublime knowledge of heavenly things—grants a perpetuity of life and memory in these lands; all other things are frivolous and fleeting, and perish along with the human body. However, when we look toward the eternity of the Kingdom of Heaven, then these very things which are now on the green side of the tree pass to the back, for there is no proportion between the finite and the infinite. Then the inscribed half-verse is changed differently, namely thus: **WE LIVE IN CHRIST; THE REST SHALL BELONG TO DEATH** <note>original: "VIVIMUS IN CHRISTO, COETERA MORTIS ERUNT"</note>; so that the first part is adapted to the green tree, the other to the sterile one. Within the green side of the tree, there is also a certain symbolic indication of the life and doctrine of Christ. The rest remains as before, except that human doctrines and inventions also pass to the left and back— <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, leading to the next page.</note>

<summary>Brahe describes the technical specifications of his gilded brass quadrant, including a complex scaling method by Pedro Nunes, and explains the vanitas-style allegory engraved on the instrument's face.</summary>

<keywords>Small Quadrant, brass instrument, fire-gilding, Pedro Nunes, Nonius scale, allegory, Vivimus Ingenio, Vanitas, astronomical precision, leveling screws</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical and symbolic description of the "Small Gilded Brass Quadrant." The previous page ended mid-sentence describing the sighting rule; this page begins by concluding a discussion on the allegorical artwork (a tree) engraved on the instrument before returning to the mathematical scales and practical operation.</meta>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...toward the [upper] part of the tree, which is the sterile part. By this we imply that nothing can make a person truly blessed or grant eternal immortality except the merit of the Son of God, Christ the Redeemer, and the observance of His doctrine and life. I chose to explain these individual points here—though perhaps they are a bit beside the main point—because of the accompanying illustration. On the other side of the Quadrant, a <term>Canon<gloss>A mathematical table or set of rules used for calculation.</gloss></term> has been engraved with ciphers <note>numbers</note>, which shows with very little effort what the individual points of the <term>Nonnian Division<gloss>A system devised by Pedro Nunes (Petrus Nonnius) using concentric arcs to measure fractions of a degree; it was a precursor to the modern Vernier scale.</gloss></term> (which I mentioned earlier) represent in terms of the altitudes being sought.

The use of this Quadrant is for taking the <term>Altitudes<gloss>The angular height of a celestial body above the horizon.</gloss></term> of the stars and the "Luminaries" <note>the Sun and Moon</note>; this is done when the highest precision is not required, such that knowing the measurement within two or three minutes of a degree is sufficient. One should then direct the plane of the Quadrant toward the star to be observed and properly adjust it by its screws, as was said before, until the <term>plumb line<gloss>A weight suspended from a string used to ensure the instrument is perfectly vertical.</gloss></term> shows that everything is correctly positioned. Then, the <term>Rule<gloss>The alidade or sighting arm, labeled A D in the diagram.</gloss></term> **A D** is raised or lowered until the star is seen through the holes or slits of the <term>dioptra<gloss>The sights mounted on the rule.</gloss></term>, or the light of the Sun is precisely admitted through them. Let the rule then be fixed on the other side near **D**, where it has a small hidden screw. In this way, the end of the Rule will show the sought Altitude on the outer limb (where the common divisions are) within five minutes; a half or even a third of that <note>roughly 1.5 to 2.5 minutes of arc</note> can be achieved by careful inspection.

But if one wishes to use the Nonnian division at the same time, one must see where the Rule passes some point on those arcs, wherever it may be. One must count which point it is, and how many such points that specific arc contains. With these two numbers, one enters the Table engraved on the other side of the Quadrant laterally; where the two numbers meet in the main area of the table, the Degrees and Minutes revealing the measured Altitude are found. The same thing can be achieved without a Table by using the "Rule of Proportions" <note>the Rule of Three</note> with not much labor: first, take the total number of divisions in the arc where the Rule falls; second, the number 90 <note>for the 90 degrees of a quadrant</note>; and third, the number of the specific point where the Rule rests, counting from the start. Once the usual calculation is performed, the fourth number will show the result sought. In truth, however, this Nonnian method is not sufficient in practice; it does not actually deliver in the result what it promises in theory, as will be clear to anyone who tries it.

<summary>This page concludes the religious symbolism of the quadrant's decoration and provides instructions for its use, including a critique of Pedro Nunes's scale system.</summary>
<keywords>quadrant, Nonnian division, Pedro Nunes, altitude, alidade, astronomical instruments, mathematical tables, Rule of Proportion</keywords>

# MEDIUM-SIZED QUADRANT
## OF AZIMUTHAL BRASS

<note>original: "Quadrans Mediocris Orichalcicus Azimuthalis"</note>

...toward the upper part of the tree, which is the sterile part. By this we imply that nothing can make a person truly blessed or grant eternal immortality except the merit of the Son of God, Christ the Redeemer, and the observance of His doctrine and life. I chose to explain these individual points here—though perhaps they are a bit beside the main point—because of the accompanying illustration. On the other side of the Quadrant, a <term>Canon<gloss>A mathematical table or set of rules used for calculation.</gloss></term> has been engraved with ciphers <note>numbers</note>, which shows with very little effort what the individual points of the <term>Nonnian Division<gloss>A system devised by the Portuguese mathematician Pedro Nunes (Petrus Nonnius) using concentric arcs to measure fractions of a degree; it was a precursor to the modern Vernier scale.</gloss></term> (which I mentioned earlier) represent in terms of the altitudes being sought.

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

This Quadrant, marked A B C, consists of a solid plate of <term>Orichalcum<gloss>A high-quality brass alloy, often used in the Renaissance for precision instruments because of its durability and gold-like appearance.</gloss></term>. From the center to the circumference (A B), it measures a little more than a cubit and a half <note>approximately 2.5 to 3 feet</note>. Its thickness is roughly half the width of a finger. Within the circumference, it contains not only the division common to us—using transversal lines and points (which I have discussed elsewhere)—but also that <term>Nonnian<gloss>A method devised by Pedro Nunes (Petrus Nonnius) to measure fractions of a degree using 45 concentric arcs; it was a precursor to the modern Vernier scale.</gloss></term> system published by the most famous Spanish mathematician in his learned book *On Twilights* <note>original: "de Crepusculis"</note>. I mentioned this system previously, though here it is expanded in a different and more convenient way so that it contains more subdivisions, especially of those numbers that can be easily divided into several whole parts. In this regard, the Nonnian invention used to present no small difficulty: for by itself, it is not sufficient for all the minutes of the entire Quadrant, much less to show the seconds of these minutes, as he [Nunes] intended. Such precision has a place in calculation rather than in practice; as often happens, beginners in this art are frequently deceived in such matters. A Table is also added, carved into the other surface of the Quadrant, which explains the Divisions and makes them very easy to use without further calculation.

The rule <note>the alidade or sighting arm</note>, marked D E, is made entirely of steel so that it may always remain perfectly straight. However, it is also gilded to prevent it from being corrupted by rust. It has two brass <term>dioptra<gloss>Sighting vanes or plates used to align the instrument with a celestial object.</gloss></term> around D and E. At the lower end E, it features small slits parallel to the primary sighting vane at D, where the observer’s eye is placed. This allows one to align the stars around the first sighting vane more accurately and centrally from all sides, without any <term>optical error<gloss>Brahe uses "hallucinatione" to mean visual distortion or misalignment.</gloss></term>. This is the method we use in almost all our instruments. The Rule itself was intentionally made longer than the radius of the Quadrant at A E; partly so that at the lower end E, it can be easily raised or lowered with the fingers during sighting until the precise altitude of the observed star is revealed, along with its <term>Azimuth<gloss>The horizontal direction of a star, measured as an angle from a reference point on the horizon.</gloss></term>. To ensure it remains steady wherever it is moved, a thin steel "feather" (as they call it) <note>a leaf spring</note> on the other side of the Quadrant near D holds and monitors it, making the observation more exact and convenient. It functions almost as if the Quadrant had a radius the length of D E; furthermore, because the lower supports do not get in the way, it is easier to handle. Let these points suffice for the explanation of the Quadrant itself.

The supports of which I speak are arranged as follows: First, the Quadrant itself rotates, attached to a certain smooth, oblong, sturdy iron rod, around H by a small screw. Thus, it can be removed whenever desired from a certain channel which has those visible rotations on the outside for the sake of stability and ornament. The channel itself carries another rule further down, represented near K and M; together with the plane of the Quadrant, it rotates conveniently toward any face of the sky above the steel <term>Azimuthal Horizon<gloss>The base of the instrument that represents the horizontal plane, used to measure the compass direction of stars.</gloss></term> N O P. At the same time, beneath the index K, it reveals the Azimuth of the observed star along with its Altitude in a single operation, so that the Altitudes are counted above on the Quadrant (as shown), while the Azimuths are counted on the said Azimuthal Circle. The three screws seen near the mentioned letters, as well as a fourth hidden on the other side, ensure that the steel Horizon can be positioned exactly in the plane of the true Horizon. Thus, the Quadrant resting above it is properly directed both according to the equilibrium of the Horizon and toward the <term>Zenith<gloss>The point in the sky directly above the observer.</gloss></term> on both its sides. There is also another screw near I which corrects the instrument in any very small differences (wherever necessary), so that it represents the plane of any vertical circle perfectly in whatever position. Furthermore, these steel screws, which carry the whole instrument, stand upon four marble columns; three of these are visible and indicated by the letters X, Y, and Z. The fourth, being identical and at the back, is not clearly seen. Finally, within these four columns is a bench or pedestal marked Q R S; this is climbed and stood upon after an observation is made so that the altitude observed on the circumference of the Quadrant can be counted conveniently and accurately. Let it suffice to have pointed out these things regarding the construction of the instrument. Any remaining small details can be easily understood by looking at the figure itself and paying diligent attention.

Its use is very broad, wherever anything involving the Altitudes and Azimuths of both the planets and the fixed stars can be adapted to astronomical observations, reduced to numbers through the science of triangles <note>trigonometry</note>, and serve the same. On this matter, one may consult the books of the experts, as well as those things we have commented on here and there in our own works as the subject required—especially in the second volume of my *Astronomical Exercises* <note>original: "Progymnasmatum Astronomicorum"</note>, which deals extensively with the Comet of the year [15]77. Indeed, in the observations set forth there and adapted for demonstration, the primary use of this Quadrant was established, since at that time I did not have at hand the other larger instruments built in a different form. Nevertheless, this one was sufficient, as it shows individual minutes quite distinctly in both Altitudes and Azimuths. It is also explained in the said book near the end, specifically on page 461.

Brahe notes that this specific instrument was essential for his observations of the Great Comet of 1577 before his larger instruments were completed.</summary>

### EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

And in the three pages following, this same Quadrant is explained a bit more fully in its various details; at the same time, the method for dividing the circumference using transversal points—the most suitable and exact method, which we were the first to apply to circular instruments—is explained at greater length and illustrated with an exquisite engraving. Indeed, its <term>dioptra<gloss>An alidade or sighting ruler used for measuring directions or angles.</gloss></term> is also depicted and explained there on page 462, so that the design of the <term>pinnacidia<gloss>The sights or 'vane-sights' located on the ends of the dioptra, used to align the instrument with a star.</gloss></term>, the arrangement of the small slits (which I mention from time to time), and other relevant matters may be clearly shown. Therefore, I refer the reader who is eager for such things to that section, where they will find more concerning these mechanical matters, both regarding this instrument and the design of others; space does not permit me to record all of it here. And what is set forth there concerning the placement of the divisions and sights, I wish to be understood for all other instruments to which these features must be adapted, so that it is not necessary to repeat them here or elsewhere to the point of boredom.

I will, however, give this warning here—both for this and for other instruments—that they must be as perfect as possible in every respect. Therefore, skilled mechanical craftsmen should be employed, who either know how to work such things skillfully or are willing to learn. And if they do not hit the mark (as the saying goes) <note>original: "acu... attigerint," literally "to touch with a needle," meaning to hit the nail on the head or achieve perfect precision.</note> on the first attempt, the builder should not be reluctant to repeat the work again and to correct in every way what is lacking, until the instruments are free from any defect. This is revealed clearly enough to an intelligent observer through various trials. Hence it happened that we took care to have most of the instruments shown in this book renovated not once, but twice or three times—and at great expense—or rather, to have them constructed almost from scratch. We even constructed mills <note>Tycho Brahe famously built his own workshops and mills on the island of Hven to manufacture his instruments and paper.</note> driven by horses as well as by water for the purpose of turning the parts <note>likely referring to the use of a lathe for precision rounding</note>, although it was almost in vain.

->And many other expenses were incurred here, which cannot<-
->be easily estimated from the instruments themselves.<-

# ANOTHER BRASS
## QUADRANT, ALSO AZIMUTHAL.

### EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of Tycho Brahe’s astronomical instruments, moving from the general theory of the "Another Brass Quadrant" to the specific mechanical details of its construction, the leveling mechanisms of its azimuthal base, and its unique design for portability.</meta>

<sig>A 4</sig>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

This Quadrant, marked *A B C*, is also made of solid brass <note>original: *Orichalco*</note>; it is arranged as shown in the figure, having beneath it a Horizon *G H I K* containing the <term>Azimuths<gloss>The horizontal angle or direction of a celestial body, measured around the horizon from the north or south.</gloss></term>, which is also cast from solid brass. The individual parts of the entire instrument are explained as follows: from the Center *A* to *B* or *C* is a length of one cubit and a half <note>Roughly 2.2 to 2.7 feet (68-83 cm), depending on the specific cubit used.</note>. From this, the circumference *B C* is visible, which is subdivided into individual minutes of the degrees by transversal points in our usual manner. It employs no other method of distinction, unlike the previous instrument, because the limb is not wide enough to comfortably accommodate more. It nevertheless has a width of about two fingers and is capable of expressing individual minutes transversally, as I have said. 

The square that is seen in the area of the Quadrant *A D E F*, together with the cross-bracing supports meeting it, are fitted there primarily for the sake of support and greater firmness. However, that Square can also be used for <term>geometric dimensions<gloss>Terrestrial surveying, such as measuring the height of a tower or the width of a valley.</gloss></term> regarding the height and width of things seen on the earth that have been built by human labor. This is the same way that the instrument which <term>Peurbachius<gloss>Georg von Peurbach (1423–1461), a hugely influential Austrian astronomer and instrument maker.</gloss></term> called the Geometric Square provides these and similar measurements, as explained and demonstrated by that eminent man in a specific book. Elsewhere, this is indicated with less effort (though not as exactly) by those who explain the <term>Astrolabe<gloss>A portable medieval instrument used for timekeeping and surveying.</gloss></term>, particularly regarding what they call its "back." 

Another fitting seen around these parts, which is understood to be on the back of the Quadrant, represents the shape of the letter *Y* and is marked with these three letters *V V X Y*. It is present so that the plane of the Quadrant itself may stand at a right angle to the Horizon in which it is encased, no matter how it is rotated. At the part where it joins the Horizon near *Y*, it is fixed by a small screw when necessary, so that by remaining unmoved in its place, it may conveniently show the Azimuthal degrees. There is also a certain Rule <note>A support bar.</note> drawn from the Center *L* toward the screw *Y* that joins the end of the support with the lower and middle part of the Quadrant, so that everything may hold together more firmly and be rotated as one. A small lead weight <note>original: *plumeolâ*</note> is also added below at the screw so that the Quadrant may stand still in any place without any wobbling. 

The fact that this Quadrant is not solid across its entire surface, but has those openings (mostly four-sided) seen within it, offers the advantage of being lighter in weight. Because of this, it is not only easier to handle but can also be moved from one place to another (for which reason it is built from several parts), as we will indicate more fully later. 

Regarding its rule carrying the <term>dioptra<gloss>The sighting bar or alidade used to align with stars.</term></gloss>, we will speak of it shortly, after we have explained a few things about the Azimuthal Horizon. Indeed, those sights serve equally for the observation of both Altitudes and Azimuths. Therefore, the Rule itself, with its longer and wider plane, lies flat against the plane of the Quadrant everywhere; so much so that even at its lower part where it projects at *A S*, it precisely faces the same flat surface, and the sights with their <term>pinnacidia<gloss>The vane-sights or small plates with slits used for precise aiming.</gloss></term> are equidistant from this and stand at right angles to it. Otherwise, an accurate investigation of the Azimuths (to say nothing of the Altitudes) could not be made. 

The Azimuthal Horizon (which I mentioned) located below the Quadrant and supporting it is expressed by *G H I K*. It has cross-supports at right angles so that it remains firmer, and the Quadrant rotates entirely within a channel fitted there around the common angles of these supports, namely in the center of that same Horizontal circle. Furthermore, it has four screws *M N O P* near the ends of these supports, by which the entire horizon (and thus the Quadrant standing upon it) may be directed so that the plane of any vertical circle is perfectly imitated. This ensures the equilibrium of the Horizon, so that the use of both regarding Altitudes and Azimuths is correctly established. 

The Rule *S V T* applied to the Quadrant (which I mentioned slightly before) functions in the same way with its sights and vane-sights as indicated in the previous description, except that below, near the sight *S*, it has two handles <note>original: *ansas*</note> by which it can be conveniently raised and lowered by hand as the altitude to be observed requires. This also allows its own weight to hold the other part of the Rule *T* as if in balance. 

Moreover, the whole instrument stands upon a square stone, hollowed out somewhat on the sides for more convenient use, which is expressed by the letter *Q*. This stone rests firmly upon a stone column *R*, as this figure sufficiently shows. In its construction, this Quadrant has an advantage over the previous one: both it and the Horizon on which it stands, and all other things belonging to it, can easily be taken apart and put back together again. It is suitable for being carried from one place to another in a convenient case, as it does not consist of as many parts as the previous one. These parts, through screws arranged here and there, allow not only for disassembly but also for easy reassembly into the exact same plane as before. Therefore, I am accustomed to calling this the **Portable Azimuthal Quadrant.**

<summary>This page describes a "Portable Azimuthal Quadrant" made of brass, highlighting its lightweight latticed design, its dual-use for astronomy and terrestrial surveying (via a Geometric Square), and its precision leveling system using a horizontal base and four screws. Tycho emphasizes the instrument's portability and ease of disassembly for transport.</summary>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

Its use is uniform with the previous instrument in all those matters which are to be observed through Altitudes and <term>Azimuths<gloss>The horizontal direction of a celestial body, measured as an angle around the horizon.</gloss></term> and demonstrated geometrically, and from there resolved into numbers. It should be known, nevertheless, that these two Quadrants are not to be trusted too much when an extremely exact observation down to a small fraction of a minute is required. Such observations are those which serve the restoration of the Sun’s course <note>original: *Solaris curriculi*</note>, where the matter concerns the smallest details and requires a precision of a sixth, or at the very least a third part of one minute; this, such small instruments—which span only one or one and a half cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger.</note>—cannot provide. Therefore, let us now turn to larger instruments more suitable for such tasks. These will facilitate not only this subtlety required regarding the Sun, but also the investigation of the tiniest <term>Parallaxes<gloss>The apparent displacement of an object when viewed from different points; used by astronomers to determine the distance of celestial bodies.</gloss></term> and their differences (if there be any) in any celestial phenomenon. Furthermore, they will reveal many other things most scrupulously in observations of both the wandering stars <note>Planets</note> and the fixed stars. 

Those eager for such matters will find examples of these parallaxes in the second volume of our *Introductory Exercises for the Restoration of Astronomy* <note>original: *Progymnasmatum nostrorum Astronomiæ instaurandæ*</note>. There, in both the first and second parts, I deal diligently with seven comets observed within the past twenty years, and I scrutinize their parallaxes as subtly as they could be obtained. I demonstrate geometrically and infallibly that they all existed in the Aethereal Region far above the Moon; even though there is no shortage of those among them who, 

->having emerged from the school of Aristotle,<- 
->(as is also otherwise customary in various human opinions)<- 
->refuse to acquiesce to the truth which has been sufficiently and more than sufficiently revealed.<- 
->But the truth must nonetheless be<- 
->shown. Let those who acknowledge<- 
->and wish to, believe it.<-

<summary>Tycho Brahe discusses the precision limits of smaller quadrants, noting they are insufficient for solar measurements, and argues that his observations of seven comets prove they exist in the heavens beyond the moon, contrary to Aristotelian theory.</summary>
<keywords>astronomical precision, solar orbit, parallaxes, comets, Aethereal Region, Aristotle, observational limits, geometric demonstration</keywords>

# THE ASTRONOMICAL SEXTANT,
## AS IT SERVES FOR MEASURING ALTITUDES.

->[An intricate engraving of an astronomical sextant designed for measuring celestial altitudes. The instrument consists of a triangular frame with a graduated arc, a movable sighting arm, and a plumb line, all mounted on a sturdy decorative pedestal with leveling feet. Various components are labeled A through N for technical reference.]<-

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

Its use is uniform with the previous instrument in all those matters which are to be observed through Altitudes and <term>Azimuths<gloss>The horizontal direction of a celestial body, measured as an angle around the horizon, usually from the North point.</gloss></term> and demonstrated geometrically, and from there resolved into numbers. It should be known, nevertheless, that these two Quadrants are not to be trusted too much when an extremely exact observation down to a small fraction of a minute is required. Such observations are those which serve the restoration of the Sun’s course <note>original: *Solaris curriculi*</note>, where the matter concerns the smallest details and requires a precision of a sixth <note>Ten seconds of arc</note>, or at the very least a third part <note>Twenty seconds of arc</note> of one minute; this, such small instruments—which span only one or one and a half cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the forearm; here it indicates the radius of the instrument is roughly 18 to 27 inches.</note>—cannot provide. 

Therefore, let us now turn to larger instruments more suitable for such tasks. These will facilitate not only this subtlety required regarding the Sun, but also the investigation of the tiniest <term>Parallaxes<gloss>The apparent shift in a star's position when viewed from different locations; used to measure cosmic distances.</gloss></term> and their differences (if there be any) in any celestial phenomenon. Furthermore, they will reveal many other things most scrupulously in observations of both the wandering stars <note>The planets</note> and the fixed stars...

<summary>This page describes the astronomical sextant configured for altitude measurements and explains that while small instruments are useful, they lack the extreme precision required for advanced solar and planetary observations.</summary>
<keywords>astronomical sextant, celestial altitudes, observational precision, solar course, azimuth, parallax, Tycho Brahe, scientific instruments</keywords>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

I was the first to devise this astronomical instrument, which is exceptionally well-suited for practical use, approximately twenty years ago. I gave it this name because it encompasses a sixth part of a circle's circumference <note>60 degrees</note>. It is entirely sufficient for the types of observations most commonly required, and its size does not make them overly difficult to perform. Although others later claimed this invention for themselves and devised some variations in its form—just as some in Germany and elsewhere do not hesitate to claim many other of my inventions—I have successively constructed three such instruments. These all possess the same exterior frames and <term>sights<gloss>The technical term used here is "pinnacidia," referring to the small plates or vanes used to align the eye with a star.</gloss></term>, though their internal frameworks vary as convenience dictated.

The present instrument shown here has the simplest internal framework among those I mentioned, being composed of fewer parts. I built it this way so it could be easily disassembled and then reassembled in specific places using screws. Placed in a chest along with its support—which can likewise be taken apart—it can be transported to other regions, not only by sea (which would be safer) but also by land in a carriage. In this way, the <term>Altitude of the Pole<gloss>The angle of the North Star above the horizon, which tells a traveler their latitude.</gloss></term> can be accurately explored in various lands. For this instrument serves appropriately not only for measuring the distances between the Sun and the stars but also for determining their altitudes. When the heights of these bodies are to be captured, it must be arranged as shown in the present figure. Having first explained its construction, we will briefly indicate its use.

This sextant, marked **A B C**, is entirely covered in <term>brass<gloss>The original Latin uses "Orichalcum," a golden-colored alloy highly prized for scientific instruments.</gloss></term> plates, joined so skillfully that you would swear it was cast from solid brass, with no visible seams. It measures four <term>cubits<gloss>A cubit is roughly 18 inches; this instrument has a radius of about 6 feet.</gloss></term> from the center to the circumference. At the center is a cylinder, **A**, through which observations are made using the lower sight, **M**, on the rule <note>The movable sighting arm, or alidade</note> **A N M**. That sight has two small slits, spaced exactly as the diameter of the cylinder requires, so that the observation is made in parallel on both sides and the star is marked centrally without any error of perception.

The graduation of the circumference **B C** is done via <term>transversal points<gloss>A system of diagonal dots that allowed astronomers to read fractions of a degree with much higher precision than simple lines.</gloss></term>, as is our custom. Based on our experience, this method is the most suitable of all, the easiest to execute, and subject to no uncertainty. I explained this more fully at the end of my book on the Comet of the year 1577 (as I have mentioned before). Others in Germany later attributed this method to themselves after <insert>Paul</insert> Wittich <note>A German mathematician and astronomer who worked briefly with Tycho.</note> saw it at my home and revealed it—along with the design of the sights—at Kassel <note>The court of Landgrave Wilhelm IV, a major center for astronomy.</note>, perhaps even selling the idea as his own.

It should be noted that when an observation of the Sun’s altitude is made, it is very convenient to attach a certain long, four-sided canal or tube to the rule near its sights. This tube should be made of lightweight material so its weight does not hinder the rule or the sights. It should leave a small empty space like a slit near Cylinder **A**, both above and below, so the solar rays passing through from both sides show the rectangular shadow of the cylinder more clearly on the inner part of the lower sight. For this reason, there is a specific opening in the tube through which this can be viewed clearly and distinctly, just as we are accustomed to facilitating solar observations so that the edges of light and shadow appear more readily.

This instrument is assembled and disassembled using several firm screws, **A B C D L**, as well as in other places not marked with letters here. However, its arc and the connecting bar **B C** are never separated but always remain joined, lest the arc lose any of its perfectly calculated curvature. Furthermore, it has a pedestal to which it is attached when altitudes are to be taken (as seen here), marked **E G H**. It is fixed into a round hole by a small pin near **D**, and after being moved into position, it is finally secured below at screw **G**. A plumb line placed at **L** and hanging down toward **C** (near the end of the graduations) indicates that the side **A B** is perfectly parallel to the horizon. Point **L** is the spot where a thin brass wire is hung, halfway between the center **A** and the end of the circumference **B**. This forms an <term>isosceles triangle<gloss>A triangle with two sides of equal length. Because the sextant covers 60 degrees, the chord (the straight line between the ends of the arc) is equal to the radius, forming an equilateral triangle.</gloss></term> **A B C**. It is a basic rule of geometry that the radius of a circle and the straight line connecting the ends of a sixty-degree arc are equal. Since the line **C L** falls perpendicularly to the line **A B**, and the plumb line is aligned with **C L**, it follows that the plumb line points to the <term>Zenith<gloss>The point directly overhead.</gloss></term> while the other line points to the plane of the horizon. Thus, the instrument can be set up for altitudes just as accurately as if we were using a full Quadrant <note>A 90-degree instrument</note>. Its pedestal or support is made of firm wood in the shape you see, though it is mostly hollow inside so that it does not burden transport with excessive weight. Nevertheless, it has iron fittings where necessary to protect it from damage and bruising.

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes his invention of the portable astronomical sextant, emphasizing its construction from brass-plated wood, its precision using transversal points, and its specialized mounting for measuring celestial altitudes.</summary>
<keywords>Sextant, Tycho Brahe, Paul Wittich, astronomical instruments, transversal points, celestial altitude, portability, brass instruments</keywords>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...is made more firmly: And in addition, there are three oblong iron supports extending from its center all the way to the end of the foot, two of which are indicated by the letters **I** and **H**. The third is hidden on the rear side; these can also be attached and removed by their own screws. Furthermore, there are four steel screws which support the entire instrument, three of which are marked with the letters **H**. The fourth is not visible from the other side. These provide convenience so that the instrument may be correctly and firmly directed, as they are turned upon certain small metal plates placed beneath them—lest they press unevenly into the floor below due to their weight and sharpness—until the observer has found the instrument to be aligned with the plane of a particular vertical circle <note>an imaginary circle passing through the zenith and the horizon</note>, determined by an attached plumb line.

Its use, when managed in this way, is for measuring all Altitudes <note>the height of an object above the horizon</note>, both of the luminaries <note>the Sun and Moon</note> and of the other wandering stars <note>the planets</note> and fixed stars, exactly within a third or fourth part of a minute <note>roughly 15 to 20 seconds of arc</note>; provided they do not exceed a sixth part of the sky <note>60 degrees, the span of a sextant</note>. Indeed, if the quadrant is fixed differently here—namely, so that the line **A C** looks toward the Zenith <note>the point directly overhead</note>—the remaining part of the sky can be measured. However, only Altitudes are detected in this way, and not simultaneously the <term>Azimuths<gloss>The horizontal direction or bearing of a celestial body.</gloss></term>. Such a sextant is very well-suited not only for Altitudes but also for investigating the distances between stars with precision.

Nor does it suggest any difficulty or uncertainty in this practice <note>original Latin: "pragmateia," referring to the systematic work of observation</note>, since it consists of few parts, and those (as I said) are firmly joined together; lest anything in it be pulled out of its proper alignment during observation. It also offers one particular benefit (which we mentioned before): it can be transported to other places without difficulty and without damage or inconvenience. This is something that ought to be provided for in certain instruments, as far as possible, so that we are not slaves to particular places. For it is necessary for an Astronomer, rather than one devoted to 

->other parts of philosophy, to be a <term>citizen of the world<gloss>original Greek: kosmopolitēn</gloss></term>, and to consider his<-
->homeland to be everywhere, as convenience or<-
->necessity requires.<-

<summary>This page describes the leveling screws and iron supports that ensure the sextant's stability and precision. It concludes with a philosophical defense of portable instruments, arguing that astronomers must be "citizens of the world" capable of observing from any location.</summary>

# THE MURAL QUADRANT

## OR THE TYCHONIC QUADRANT.

<insert>
PORTRAIT OF TYCHO BRAHE, SON OF OTTO <note>original: "O. F.", standing for "Ottonis Filius"</note>,
BUILDER OF THE BUILDING <note>This refers to Uraniborg, his famous observatory on the island of Hven</note> AND OF ASTRONOMICAL INSTRUMENTS.
IN THE YEAR OF THE LORD 1587, AT THE AGE OF 40.
</insert>

<note>This page serves as a title page for one of the most famous instruments in the history of astronomy: the Mural Quadrant. Unlike portable instruments, this was a massive brass arc bolted directly onto a north-south wall. By observing stars through a window as they crossed the meridian, Tycho could determine their positions with unprecedented accuracy. The portrait mentioned here is part of the iconic engraving showing Tycho pointing toward the stars while his assistants work in the background.</note>

<meta>This page follows the title page for the Mural Quadrant. The catchword "annos" from the previous layout refers to Tycho's age (40 years), leading into this detailed explanation of the instrument's construction and operation.</meta>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

We have also caused to be constructed the great **MURAL** <note>original: "Muralem"</note> or **TYCHONIC QUADRANT** represented here by the letters **B D E C**—so called from the wall to which it was fixed. It is cast from solid brass <term>orichalcum<gloss>A golden-colored alloy of copper and zinc, similar to modern brass, highly prized for scientific instruments for its durability.</gloss></term> and polished to a high degree. It is five inches wide and two inches thick, and its circumference is so large that it requires a radius or semi-diameter of nearly 5 cubits <note>About 1.9 meters or over 6 feet. This massive size allowed for much greater precision than smaller portable devices.</note>. Therefore, the Degrees are quite large, so much so that each individual minute is further subdivided into six small parts. Thus, it displays every ten seconds distinctly, so that even half of these—namely five seconds—can be discerned without difficulty. All of these are marked very finely by certain transversal points <note>A system of diagonal dots that allowed Tycho to read fractions of a degree with unprecedented accuracy before the invention of the vernier scale.</note> in our usual manner.

This Quadrant is attached to a certain wall **M P Q** which faces the Meridian <note>The North-South line.</note> exactly with its plane. It is fixed with very strong screws, so that no force or strength can move it from its proper position. This position is pre-arranged so that it corresponds exactly to the Quadrant of the Celestial Meridian drawn from the Horizon to the Zenith, though placed on the opposite side. On the other wall **L M N B**, which is perpendicular to the first and faces exactly East and West, there is at the top, where the Center of the Quadrant is located, a certain gilded brass cylinder. It is gilded so that it is not subject to damage from the air or gather any tarnish. This is shown next to the letter **A** in a certain square hole in that same wall, which can be opened and closed from the outside by a convenient little door, so that sightings may be taken around said cylinder from either side when the weather is clear; this is done through the sights <note>original: "pinnacidia"</note> at **D** or **E**. For this Quadrant has two sights, so that one may use whichever one is preferred, as may be more convenient for measuring the Altitude. Each of these is a square on the plane the size of one palm. The diameter of the aforementioned cylinder corresponds exactly to this, so that through parallel slits attached to the sights themselves, a sighting can be made in all four places: if both the altitude and the passage through the Meridian are to be taken at the same time. Otherwise, if only the Altitude is desired, the observer (who is depicted near **F**) completes the task through the upper and lower slits and the corresponding parts of the cylinder, and indicates the recorded altitude to another student (who sits near **G** at a table with a lamp) to be entered into the book of Observations. To ensure that the time of the Observation and the very moment of the crossing of the Meridian are established together, a third person (marked at **H**) attends to the Clocks **I** and **K**. When the observer at **F** gives the signal, that moment of time is also recorded in the book by the person sitting at **G**. The clocks of which I spoke are such that they indicate not only the individual minutes of the hours, but also the seconds with the greatest possible precision, and they emulate the regularity of the celestial revolution. Although this is achieved with difficulty, it can be obtained to some extent with the required diligence, and any error that creeps in can be noticed and corrected. Therefore, there ought to be at least two clocks of this kind, so that one may correct the other wherever necessary. We have four such clocks ready; the largest of these manages the whole business with only three wheels, the largest of which is cast from solid and pure brass and has 1200 teeth. It is two cubits in diameter. The rest may be judged from this. The remaining three clocks are smaller and require more wheels; we shall give the composition of all these elsewhere, God willing.

Whatever paintings are seen within the circumference of the Quadrant were added only for the sake of decoration, so that the space between would not remain uselessly empty. It will not be out of place to briefly explain these, even if it is less relevant to the matter at hand. Below the letter **T** is depicted my own likeness, sitting in a chair placed at a table in a long robe reaching to the ankles, leaning back slightly so that the right hand is stretched out toward the cylinder as if pointing, and the other is placed on the table near a book and some other things, as if I were thus indicating to my students what should be observed and how it should be put to use. Tobias Gemperlin, an excellent artist (whom I had once brought with me from Augsburg <note>original: "Augusta Vindelicorum"</note> to Denmark), expressed this portrait with great skill, and so accurately that a better likeness could hardly be given. It is represented in equal form according to the stature and size of the whole body. Above the head near **X**, a certain gilded brass globe is fashioned, which revolves of its own accord by wheels skillfully fitted inside, emulating the daily motion. It simultaneously displays the opposing courses of the Sun and the Moon from the poles of the Ecliptic, so that it even turns the Moon itself according to its phases and illumination, increasing or decreasing them. The Sun, however, within the 24 hour-circles, in addition to its own motion, is revolved in a daily course around the axes of the Equator, showing the individual hours of the day, as well as the time of its own rising and setting, and its crossing across the meridian on both sides. This ingenious <term>automaton<gloss>A self-moving machine or mechanical device; here, a complex clockwork planetary model.</gloss> <note>original Greek: "αυτόματον"</note></term> was invented by me and constructed at my expense in the year of our Lord 1590, for the most Serene Prince, Lord **CHRISTIAN**, then King-Elect, my most Merciful Lord, when formerly...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the construction and operation of his famous Mural Quadrant, highlighting its massive size, brass construction, and the team of three specialists required to operate it. He also describes the interior decorations of the instrument's wall, including a portrait of himself and a complex mechanical globe.</summary>

<keywords>Mural Quadrant, Tycho Brahe, astronomical instruments, brass, transversal points, meridian, timekeeping, Tobias Gemperlin, Christian IV, automaton</keywords>
