<meta>This page continues the description of the Mural Quadrant, specifically explaining the elaborate mural painting that surrounds the physical instrument and the technical method for its use. The catchword "annos" from the previous page connects to the description of the King's visit during his youth.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...namely for seven years. When he was about 14 years of age, he did not deign to graciously visit me at Uraniborg on the Island of Hven, bringing with him—besides the rest of the court nobility—three primary Senators of the Kingdom who then presided over the government. I humbly gave him a gift, which he still preserves. The most serene King-elect <note>This refers to Christian IV, who visited Tycho in 1592 before his formal coronation.</note> in turn graciously presented me with a gold chain, beautifully crafted with a special skill such as he was then accustomed to wear, and decorated with his own image. Above the globe itself, which we just mentioned, a certain part of our Library is represented at the letter **V**. At **Y** and **Z**, there hang two portraits solidly formed within round frames: one is of the most serene and powerful King of the Danes, Frederick II, of most blessed memory; the other is of the most serene Queen Sophia, his most illustrious consort, who always followed me and my studies with Royal and gracious favor. Furthermore, as for the things seen in the inner painting, first at the top near the numbers **1, 2, 3, 4**, some of my instruments are depicted there. Then, below this floor, the Museum <note>In the 16th century, a "Museum" referred to a place of study or a library, rather than a public gallery.</note> is shown. Where, near **5** and **8**, there are tables at which my astronomical students were accustomed to sit while calculating or performing other tasks related to this field. There were four such tables in the Museum itself. For I was always accustomed to support at least 6 or 8, sometimes 10 or 12 such students, summoned from everywhere; besides boys and younger pupils of that kind. Between these tables, at **6** and **7** behind the column located in the middle of the round Museum, is seen that great brass <term>globe<gloss>The "Great Brass Globe" was one of Tycho's most famous instruments, used for mapping the stars.</gloss></term> six feet in diameter, which we will later show and explain in its own place. Finally, below all these, near the numbers **9, 10, 11**, my chemical laboratory is seen, which was entirely underground and vaulted; in which I had prepared 16 pyrotechnic <note>original: "fornaces Pyronomicas" — furnaces designed for the high-heat processes of alchemy and chemistry.</note> furnaces of various types and forms. For I have been intensely devoted to this study, from my youth no less than to astronomy; and I have cultivated it with great diligence and at no small expense. Lastly, near my feet at number **12**, lies one of our hunting dogs, who was very faithful and sagacious. He is depicted in that form and size in which he was seen; not so much as a symbol of nobility, but as a <term>hieroglyphic<gloss>A symbolic representation or emblem.</gloss></term> of wisdom and fidelity. Thus you have the whole painting briefly explained, as far as it was possible to imitate it in such a small form; three different and excellent artists painted it for me. That painter from Augsburg, whom I mentioned, did my portrait. My architect, **Johannes de Embda Stenwinchel**, fashioned the architectural parts and what is contained within them. But those things depicted above them in the manner of a map of regions and mountains, where even the sun is seen setting, were added by **Johannes of Antwerp**, the Royal Painter of Kronborg. For each of the three said artists excelled beyond the others in that specific work which he had done. Finally, above at **R S**, is the inscription of the portrait and the entire painting, as you see.

The use of this very large Quadrant is in investigating most accurately the heights of the stars within a sixth part of one minute <note>Equivalent to 10 seconds of arc, an incredible precision for the pre-telescopic era.</note>, with the sighting made through one of the <term>pinnacidia<gloss>The sights or vanes of the instrument through which the observer looks.</gloss></term> from the upper and lower slit along both circumferences of the cylinder analogous to these; and by counting the altitude itself on the outer surface of the Quadrant at the position of the same sight. The moment of transit through the Meridian can also be obtained from both sides at once, both from the sights and the cylinder, using the accurate clocks we have mentioned. How, indeed, the position of a star is established from a given altitude and the moment of time in which the star crosses the meridian, applying the motion of the sun, is well known to astronomers. I have relied most of all on this quadrant in restoring the motion of the sun from its meridian altitudes, by allowing the square shadow of the cylinder to enter into the inner part of one of the sights—
->into that same square figure, receiving and<-
->containing that shadow exactly. I have nonetheless also called other large<-
->Quadrants into council, so that a matter so subtle<-
->might be free from all suspicion<-
->of error.<-

<summary>Tycho describes the symbolic mural painting surrounding his great quadrant, including his library, student laboratory, and famous brass globe, then details how the instrument achieves precision within 10 seconds of arc.</summary>
<keywords>Mural Quadrant, Uraniborg, Christian IV, Frederick II, Queen Sophia, chemical laboratory, Johannes de Embda Stenwinchel, astronomical precision, great brass globe, pyrotechnic furnaces</keywords>

# THE REVOLVING QUADRANT
## FOR AZIMUTH.

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...namely for seven years. When he was about 14 years of age, he did not <note>The Latin 'non gravatus est' suggests the King was pleased or did not think it a burden to visit.</note> hesitate to graciously visit me at Uraniborg on the Island of Hven, bringing with him—besides the rest of the court nobility—three primary Senators of the Kingdom who then presided over the government. I humbly gave him a gift, which he still preserves. The most serene King-elect <note>This refers to Christian IV, who visited Tycho in 1592. He was "King-elect" because while he succeeded his father in 1588, he was not formally crowned until 1596.</note> in turn graciously presented me with a gold chain, beautifully crafted with a special skill such as he was then accustomed to wear, and decorated with his own image. 

Above the globe itself, which we just mentioned, a certain part of our Library is represented at the letter **V**. At **Y** and **Z**, there hang two portraits solidly formed within round frames: one is of the most serene and powerful King of the Danes, Frederick II, of most blessed memory; the other is of the most serene Queen Sophia, his most illustrious consort, who always followed me and my studies with Royal and gracious favor. 

Furthermore, as for the things seen in the inner painting, first at the top near the numbers **1, 2, 3, 4**, some of my instruments are depicted there. Then, below this floor, the <term>Museum<gloss>In the 16th century, a 'Museum' referred to a private place of study, a library, or a research room rather than a public gallery.</gloss></term> is shown. Where, near **5** and **8**, there are tables at which my astronomical students were accustomed to sit while calculating or performing other tasks related to this field. There were four such tables in the Museum itself. For I was always accustomed to support at least 6 or 8...

<meta>This page continues the description of Tycho Brahe's astronomical instruments, moving from the artistic details of the Mural Quadrant to the technical specifications and operation of the Revolving Azimuthal Quadrant. The catchword "stanti" connects to the first word of the next page.</meta>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

WE have constructed a revolving QUADRANT, which not only scrutinizes Altitudes with precision (like the previously mentioned Sextant), but also reveals <term>Azimuths<gloss>The horizontal angle of a celestial object, measured clockwise from the North or South around the horizon.</gloss></term> perfectly in the same operation, in such a form as you see here. It has a radius from the center **A** to the circumference **B** or **C** of four <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the forearm; here it likely refers to about 18-20 inches, making the instrument nearly 7 feet in radius.</note> cubits, and the circumference is subdivided so finely by transversal points that it shows a fourth part of one minute to the sharp-eyed observer. This is achieved by placing the eye at the sighting vane **K** and moving the Sighting Rule **A K** until the star to be observed shines equally on both sides of the Cylinder near **A**. Then, the Rule reveals the sought-after altitude where it crosses the divisions near **K**. The Index **Q**, meanwhile, which extends into the azimuthal circumference represented by **R**, simultaneously reveals the moments of the Azimuths. The framework seen within the area of the Quadrant is arranged in various ways for this purpose: to hold and preserve it firmly both in its own plane and in its other required configuration. The Edge, where the divisions are located, is covered on all sides with brass plates; likewise, the remaining parts of the Quadrant are protected with steel or brass encrustations (as was necessary). Moreover, this Quadrant is firmly attached to a strong, four-sided iron column **L M N**, with which it also rotates very easily toward whatever part of the Sky one pleases. For the said iron column, made smooth at the top, is firmly fitted into a hole in a thick iron piece that crosses over the round wall forming the small tower or vault in which the instrument was kept. At the bottom, however, near **G**, this same column ends in a rounded steel point and rotates in a similarly pointed small hole within a steel plate. This plate can be pushed in any necessary direction by four externally placed small screws, until both plumb lines of the Quadrant—beginning near **A** and ending near **S**—so direct both the plane of the Quadrant and its back (however it is rotated) that it is found to exactly represent any vertical circle. Once this is done, and the arrangement of the Quadrant has been carefully provided for and secured with the required screws in various and diverse regions of the World, there will be no need to attend to the plumb lines for a long time to see if the Quadrant has moved from its required position. For this cannot happen unless some great violence occurs unexpectedly; even then, it can be easily restored in the manner previously described. Indeed, it rests so firmly on the round stone **H**, which is solid and stands unmoved deep in the earth on another stone foundation, and the ironwork which stands crosswise above—resting as I said on a stable wall—does not allow the iron column to which the Quadrant is attached to waver even slightly from its hole in the center.

THE round WALL, which exactly shows the periphery of a circle (the diameter of which is about 6 cubits, so that there is sufficient space between the circumference of the Quadrant and the hollow of the vault for the Observer to conveniently move around), has above it a certain Mechanical circle covered with brass plates. In this circle, all the Azimuths of the entire Horizon (to which it corresponds in its equilibrium) are provided, subdivided into minutes. In one place, the Beginning of the Azimuths remains unmoved, which depends on the meridian line. This was sought out with such great care and subtlety that it does not elude the Observer by even a single minute. We found the meridian line by another and far more certain method than has been done by anyone until now, using the <note>original: "stella polari"</note> pole star itself, especially when it causes the maximum angle of horizontal removal on either side of the pole; at that time, it seems to stay for a little while as if in the same point of Azimuth, a method we found to be infallible and efficient. This was a worthwhile effort, since few things in Astronomical Observations can be perfected without the most accurate knowledge of the Meridian Line. The method of handling and observing with this Instrument is such that it may be directed toward any star to be noted in the Sky by rotating the plane of the Quadrant and by raising or lowering its rule with its sights. This is done until, through the slits of the sighting vane near the eye, the star is first seen exactly from the upper slit on the upper edge of the Cylinder; and the same is done immediately through the lower slit, attending to the lower edge. In this way, the Rule reveals the sought-after altitude of the star most exactly where it cuts the divisions near **K**. If, however, one also intends to know the Azimuth by the same labor, the Quadrant is rotated slightly this way and that on its column until the same star is seen simultaneously through both side slits near the surface of both sides of the Cylinder. Then, the index **Q** on the horizontal circumference simultaneously reveals the very Azimuth. Because these two things are not easily discerned at one and the same time due to the continuous and swift motion of the Sky, it is better to fix the index **Q** at a certain Azimuth which the star is approaching, waiting until it is carried down by that universal motion, and at that same instant

...to the standing observer taking the altitude by moving the <term>sighting vane<gloss>Latin: *dioptrâ*; a small device or aperture used to align the eye with a star.</gloss></term> of the Rule up or down; this is managed with almost no effort.

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

The use of this instrument is, like others, to perform operations by means of Altitudes and <term>Azimuths<gloss>The horizontal angle of a celestial object, measured from the North or South along the horizon.</gloss></term>. However, it possesses this advantage over others: that without labor, everything can be achieved through it most exactly and safely, even to within a fourth part of a minute <note>This indicates a precision of 15 arc-seconds, a remarkable feat for the time.</note>. This was denied to earlier instruments, both because of their small size and also because their structure was not so elegant or easy to use.

Therefore, the astronomer should hold this method of construction in the highest regard. To ensure the instrument can be used more conveniently for both observing and calculating, steps (represented by **T**) ascend all around, following almost the same curve as the quadrant itself. Thus, an observer standing on the top step can observe a star located near the horizon, while one sitting near the bottom step can observe those near the <term>zenith<gloss>The point in the sky directly above the observer; the Latin text uses "verticem".</gloss></term>, and likewise for the intermediate positions. Moreover, this instrument, along with its hidden small tower <note>original: "turricula cryptica"; likely referring to the specialized enclosure or underground room used at Tycho's observatory, Stjerneborg.</note>, is covered by a roof skillfully made of polished planks. This roof is attached below to a horizontal wall outside the azimuthal circle, specifically to a round <term>armillary ring<gloss>A structural ring or hoop.</gloss></term> made of strong wood. This ring has rollers at four transverse points within it, by which it can be rotated with minimal effort in any direction around the circumference of the horizon as one pleases. And thus the oblong windows that the roof possesses—also made of planks and positioned opposite one another—can easily be directed toward any star to be observed. Once the observation is finished, these windows are closed so that the instrument remains safe from the damage of the air, rain, and winds. This precaution must be taken with all other machines, in whatever way possible. For these instruments are very expensive <note>original: "magno constant"; literally "they stand at a great price."</note> and are easily rendered subject to damage unless they are kept safe from the violence and corruptions of the elements and weather changes.

# THE GREAT STEEL
## QUADRANT, ALSO ENCLOSED WITHIN A STEEL SQUARE,
### and likewise
### Azimuthal.

<note>original: *Quadrans Magnus Chalibeus*. Steel was a revolutionary material for astronomical instruments in the 16th century; Tycho favored it over wood or brass because it was less prone to warping or expanding with temperature changes, ensuring greater precision.</note>

<note>This instrument is "Azimuthal" because it is mounted on a vertical axis, allowing it to rotate horizontally to measure a star's position along the horizon (azimuth), while the quadrant arc measures its height (altitude).</note>

<summary>This page acts as a title header for the Great Steel Quadrant, a specialized instrument designed by Tycho Brahe that combined a quadrant with a square frame and an azimuthal base for highly precise celestial measurements.</summary>

<keywords>Great Steel Quadrant, Tycho Brahe, astronomical instruments, azimuth, altitude, Uraniborg, Stjerneborg, early modern science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the Great Steel Quadrant, moving from its title to a detailed technical explanation of its construction, materials, and mechanical mounting.</meta>

<sig>B 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: Azimu- <note>This catchword points to "Azimutha" on the following page.</note></meta>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

Furthermore, this Quadrant, consisting entirely of solid steel and additionally enclosed by a steel square, performs the same functions as the preceding one; indeed, it does so even more precisely, as it slightly exceeds it in size. It measures nearly five cubits <note>A "cubit" was a traditional unit of length; Tycho’s cubit was roughly 18 inches (45 cm), making this instrument's radius about 7.5 feet (2.3 meters).</note> from the center to the circumference, not counting the Square, also made of solid steel, which surrounds it. The Quadrant itself, on the side where the divisions are marked, is so skillfully covered with a brass plate <note>original: *Orichalcica lamina*. Brass was used for the scale because it is softer and easier to engrave with fine lines than steel.</note> that one would think it was fused to the iron. This was done because brass holds fine divisions more accurately than steel, even when steel is well-polished, and it is not as prone to rust. Each degree is subdivided not only into individual minutes but each of these into six smaller parts, so that it represents ten seconds; this is achieved by a specific method we commonly use elsewhere. <note>Tycho is referring to his "transversal" method—a system of dots or diagonal lines that allowed him to read fractions of a degree with unprecedented accuracy.</note> The Square that goes around the Quadrant is not covered in brass because it is used less frequently, since the Quadrant itself shows what is sought with sufficient precision. The Square serves as a support and for easier rotation, even if it had no other use. However, even this Square is marked as finely as possible on the steel itself, also using our specific method, so that each of its sides surrounding the circumference of the Quadrant—both the one facing the Horizon (D C) and the one facing the Zenith (B C)—contains the final number of the table of sines with six digits <note>original: *canonis sinuum... 6. Ziphrarum*. This refers to a trigonometric table where the radius is calculated to a precision of six decimal places.</note>. The ruler, led from the center across the whole surface, shows the altitude sought during observation on both the Quadrant and the Square: on the former by the equality of the degrees, and on the latter according to the geometric ratio of sines and triangles; nevertheless, the result returns to one and the same value in both places. By "Quadrant," we mean A G M E. By "Square," A B C D. By "Ruler," A P Q, which also has a sight <term>pinnacidium<gloss>a small plate with a hole or slit used for sighting</gloss></term> and an eyepiece <term>dioptra<gloss>the sighting component of an alidade</gloss></term> at P, as well as a cylinder near the center A, in the same manner as described in the previous instruments. The part of the ruler P Q that extends beyond the Quadrant, reaching the furthest corner of the Square, can also be added or removed as desired.

Furthermore, this instrument has its own supports and framework for the sake of bracing and stability, though fewer than the previous one, since it is made entirely of metal. To prevent it from becoming too heavy, the ribs (marked by the letters G H N O and E K L M), which cross each other diagonally, are made of solid steel and firmly hold the entire Square, together with the enclosed Quadrant, in its proper plane and arrangement. Indeed, this Quadrant and its Square are attached to a very robust iron column at X and Y, with which it can be rotated easily and conveniently toward all parts of the sky. The column turns at the top in a round hole located in the middle of the iron fittings indicated by the letters A, which are firmly set across the wall of the crypt; at the bottom, it turns on an iron plate near Y in the same way as described for the previous instrument, so that by means of four screws, the entire instrument can be properly adjusted for use and held firmly in its rotation so that it does not drift from the required plane. Moreover, the plate I mentioned, with its screws, is fixed to a round stone column indicated by B, which supports the entire machine and is set deep into the earth in another stone base.

This instrument also has, within the smooth surrounding wall of the crypt, its own azimuthal horizon T V V V, entirely covered in brass and placed roughly in the middle of the wall. Thus the observer, standing on the upper step (for this instrument also has its own masonry steps for ascending and descending as the observation requires and its size demands), can count the azimuths <note>Azimuth is the horizontal angle of a star, measured along the horizon.</note> which are indicated by the pointers R and S on both sides. The diameter of this azimuthal horizon is so large that it allows one to pass all the way around the Quadrant and the Square; therefore, its diameter equals 9 cubits. This entire apparatus is so firmly arranged and secured with screws that, once properly adjusted, it does not fear the strength of a man to move it from its place. This concludes the construction and arrangement of this instrument, which is also entirely protected from the damage of the weather in its upper part by a neatly crafted roof. This roof has its own windows that can be opened and can be rotated toward any part of the world by rollers carrying the roof, as the observation requires. At the same time, they can be closed while the instrument is being adjusted, so the wind does not violently move its plumb lines near D from their natural path, and furthermore, when the instrument is not in use, to protect it from the damage of rain and wind. As for the roof, these same features were implemented in the previous quadrant, as we said there.

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

Azimuths <note>Azimuth refers to the horizontal angle or direction of a celestial body, measured clockwise from the north.</note>; and insofar as these serve Astronomy, as was indicated before. However, this Quadrant with its attached Square—since it is not only entirely metallic <note>The Latin *non saltém totus Metallicus sit* suggests that while it is composed of metal, its accuracy comes from more than just its material.</note> and exceeds the previous one in size—reveals both Altitudes and Azimuths even more exactly than that one. It allows these to be investigated even to the sixth part of a minute <note>This equals 10 seconds of arc, an incredible level of precision for the late 16th century.</note>, especially where such great precision is required, as is worthwhile when investigating the Solar motion through its Altitude and Declination <term>Declination|The angular distance of a heavenly body north or south of the celestial equator, similar to latitude on Earth.</term>.

I will also add this as a finishing touch <note>original: *Colophonis loco*. A colophon traditionally refers to the final text in a book, but here Tycho uses it to mean a concluding point or "crowning achievement."</note>: that with these two Quadrants—namely the preceding revolving one and this Steel one, which is also revolving—and by taking into council the Great Brass Mural or "Tychonic" Quadrant (upon which we placed the most trust for investigating the Declinations of the Sun with extreme accuracy, so that from this its position and entire course might be restored without error), we have done the same. We also performed this same check when subtly investigating the Declinations of the Stars when they crossed the Meridian, where there was need for the highest precision. For these three massive Instruments, properly arranged and adapted for use, provided the same Altitude even in the smallest fractions of a minute, and everywhere they
->agreed together as one. To which also the Sextants and other instruments<-
->employed together bore witness.<-

# THE GREAT AZIMUTHAL
## SEMICIRCLE.

-> [Engraving: A magnificent astronomical instrument featuring a large vertical semicircle mounted on a horizontal azimuthal base. The entire structure is supported by five ornate, hexagonal pillars. However, this Quadrant with its attached Square—since it is not only entirely metallic <note>original: *non saltém totus Metallicus sit*. Tycho emphasizes that being constructed of metal (brass and steel) rather than wood ensures the instrument does not warp with humidity, maintaining its accuracy.</note> and exceeds the previous one in size—reveals both Altitudes and Azimuths even more exactly than that one. It allows these to be investigated even to the sixth part of a minute <note>This is equal to 10 seconds of arc, an extraordinary level of precision for the 1590s, achieved through Tycho's advanced transversal scale system.</note>, especially where such great precision is required, as is worthwhile when investigating the Solar motion through its Altitude and Declination <term>Declination|The angular distance of a heavenly body north or south of the celestial equator, equivalent to latitude on a terrestrial map.</term>.

<meta>This page continues the detailed technical description of Tycho Brahe's large-scale astronomical instruments, transitioning from the Great Steel Quadrant to the construction, geometry, and allegorical decoration of the Great Azimuthal Semicircle. The previous page ended with a brief introduction to the device's purpose; this page provides the "Explicatio" (explanation) of its mechanical design and artistic symbolism.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**W**E have also devised another instrument suitable for precisely capturing altitudes along with azimuths <note>Azimuth refers to the horizontal direction of a star, measured as an angle from the North or South.</note>, the form of which is shown in the preceding figure. We chose to use a full Semicircle, **A B C**, here instead of a Quadrant. Nevertheless, we established the center of the divisions at point **A** on the Diameter **A C**. We did this so that each individual degree on the rim of the Semicircle would become twice as large as it would be if the divisions were made from the geometric center. Indeed, it is known from the *Elements of Euclid* that an angle at the center of a circle is twice the size of an angle drawn from the circumference to that same arc, according to the twentieth proposition of the third book <note>original: *propositionem vicesimam libri tertii*. Tycho is using a geometric trick: by placing the "eye" of the angle at the edge of the circle rather than the middle, he doubles the physical space available for each degree mark, allowing for much more precise readings.</note>, and others that lead to this conclusion. 

In the middle of this instrument, from the center **D** up to the highest point **B**, there is an oblong square channel. Inside this is a plumb line, whose lead weight—located in the hole **D** with a portion of brass wire—shows when the instrument is perfectly upright, so that it corresponds exactly to the vertical plane of the semicircle. The arched structures seen on either side are used for stability, firmly holding this vast semicircle in its plane and circular path. It has a diameter of six cubits <note>A "cubit" was roughly 18 inches (45 cm). This instrument was massive—nearly 9 feet across.</note> at **A C**. 

The Rule <term>Rule|Also called an 'alidade,' this is the pivoting bar used to sight the stars.</term> **Y A V** attached to it is as long from **A** to **V** as the diameter of the semicircle. It also has a slightly thicker extension at **A Y** so that the Rule can be more easily raised or lowered in balance; for this purpose, two handles made of solid metal have been added at **Y**. On its other side, near **X**, it has a pointer with a screw where it is held fast by a small Quadrant **N O**. This allows the Rule to remain in a fixed position wherever one pleases until the altitude has been read, no matter which part of the semicircle’s rim it has passed over. 

This semicircular instrument is also covered with brass plates wherever necessary. At the top, it has three figures skillfully and artistically carved in solid wood, arranged on their pedestals as seen in the figure. These are placed both for the sake of beauty and to provide a "hieroglyphic" <note>In the Renaissance, "hieroglyphic" meant a symbolic or allegorical image that conveyed a hidden moral or scientific truth.</note> warning through their representation. 

The highest of all is the symbol for **Urania** herself in Astronomy. She is a graceful and beautiful maiden, with her face lifted toward Heaven gazing at the stars. With her right hand, she raises a Sphere of the celestial revolutions; with her left, she takes what the two figures standing below her, like handmaidens, offer up. The color of her tunic is sky-blue, interwoven with small gold stars and silver borders. 

The figure standing on the left side is the Image of **Geometry**. She holds a triangle in her right hand, which she raises toward Astronomy, and a compass in her other hand. By these, she means to suggest that she serves Astronomy through measurement and mechanical constructions, as well as through the learned science of Triangles. She directs her eyes toward the face of Urania, looking up at her with reverence. The color of her dress is brownish-grey interwoven with green, as she takes her name from earthly things <note>The word 'Geometry' comes from the Greek *geo* (earth) and *metria* (measure).</note>. On her head, she wears a laurel wreath to show that she endures forever and is capable of untainted truth. 

On the other side—the right—stands a maiden representing **Arithmetic**. She also looks up at Astronomy, honoring and serving her. She holds chalk in one hand and a tablet in the other, signifying that she expresses the numbers which Astronomy needs to make knowledge accessible. She resolves into "discrete quantities" <note>Numbers or individual units.</note> those things which Geometry previously handled as "continuous" <note>Lines and shapes.</note>. She also wears a laurel wreath on her head, as she is no less eternal and true than Geometry. The color of her dress is white, like paper or parchment, marked here and there with various ciphers <note>Mathematical symbols or digits.</note>. 

All these things were done both to decorate the instrument and to show that **Astronomy**, as the supreme Queen of all the Liberal Arts, has chosen these two—Geometry and Arithmetic—as her chief ministers (though she does not neglect the others). 

Below this Semicircle is the **Azimuthal Horizon** **E F I G H**, measuring four cubits in diameter. It is made of solid steel and its upper surface is aptly divided into Degrees and their subdivisions. In the middle, it has two strong iron fittings intersecting at the center **D** in a cross-shape; the entire instrument rotates around this point. Iron supports are placed on the other side to keep the instrument perpetually at right angles to the Horizon. 

Below the iron Horizon are five columns. The middle one, marked **Q**, stands in the center; the other four, marked with the letters **P**, support the circumference at four equal distances. All five have certain special screws at the top, called "everlasting screws" <note>original: *cochleas perennes*. These are fine-threaded screws used for delicate leveling adjustments.</note>, marked with the letters **R**. This is to be understood for the others as well. When these are turned by the small handles **S T**, they are pushed up or down until the Azimuthal circle resting on them exactly mimics the level of the Horizon, and the semicircle above it perfectly matches the plane of a vertical line. These screws can raise or lower a very heavy weight with minimal effort, providing great convenience in mechanics.

It explains the roles of the figures of Urania, Geometry, and Arithmetic as symbols of the sciences supporting astronomy, and details the mechanical leveling system using fine-threaded screws.</summary>

<keywords>Urania, Geometry, Arithmetic, Azimuthal Semicircle, Euclid, leveling screws, astronomical instruments, Uraniborg, scientific allegory, precision measurement</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Tycho Brahe's large-scale astronomical instruments. The previous page detailed the geometric theory behind using a semicircle to double the precision of degree markings. This page transitions into the mechanical practicalities of the instrument's mounting and rotation, comparing it to earlier steel designs.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

The use of the instrument is sufficiently clear from its construction: its function is identical to those instruments that elsewhere collect data via **Azimuths** and **Altitudes** <note>Azimuth is the horizontal direction or bearing of a star measured along the horizon; altitude is its height above the horizon.</note>; except that here, on a **Semicircle**, the same thing is achieved and measured as is done elsewhere on **Quadrants** <note>A quadrant is a quarter-circle instrument used for measuring angles up to 90 degrees.</note> or **Azimuthal Rules** <note>original: *Regulis Azimuthalibus*. These were specialized sighting rods or rulers used to determine horizontal positions.</note>, which we will discuss later. I will also point this out: previously, that **steel Quadrant enclosed in a Square** (which we discussed in the explanation of the immediately preceding figure) originally rested with its lower part upon a **Steel Horizon** <note>The "Horizon" here refers to the flat, circular base plate that allows the instrument to rotate horizontally.</note> and was rotated upon it, just as we have taken care to represent in another illustration, though it is not displayed here.

It later seemed more advisable to rotate it in such a way—as the preceding figure shows—that it is removed from this horizontal support. This was because, due to its excessive weight (since it is entirely made of steel, as has been said), it pressed down too heavily and forcefully upon the Horizon. Consequently, it could only be turned with great difficulty without the whole machine being disturbed or shaking. Nevertheless, its management can also be set up in that original way; in that case, no round wall is needed all around to serve as an **Azimuthal Circle**, nor are iron fittings required above and below, as in the preceding instrument. Perhaps we will at some point provide an illustration <note>original: *Icona*</note> of this alternative arrangement along with its explanation, although the method described previously remains far more convenient and reliable.

# THE PARALLACTIC IN-
## STRUMENT OR THE
### Instrument of Rules.

<note>The Parallactic Instrument, also known as a **Triquetrum** or "Ptolemaic Rules," was a device used to measure the zenith distance of stars (how far a star is from the point directly overhead). It was favored by ancient astronomers like Ptolemy because it was easier to construct with precision than a large circular arc.</note>

<margin>Illustration of the Parallactic Instrument</margin>

<note>The accompanying engraving shows the instrument's design: a vertical pillar (marked B-C) supports two hinged arms. The top arm (D-E) is used for sighting the star, while the lower arm (F-G) acts as a scale to measure the angle. The entire device sits on a sturdy tripod base (A, L) with leveling screws, allowing for precise adjustment.</note>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

I also decided to construct this Parallactic instrument—which others call the **Ptolemaic Rules**—since it succinctly measures the distance of stars from the **zenith** <note>The point in the sky directly above an observer.</note>, and thus also their altitudes, using only three required rules. I actually received an instrument of this kind made entirely of wood, which had once belonged to the incomparable **Copernicus**; it was, as they say, crafted by his own hands. This was sent to me as a gift by Master Johannes Hannovius, a Canon of Warmia <note>A region in Poland where Copernicus lived and worked.</note>, where Copernicus had previously lived. This occurred in the year 1584, when I had sent one of my students, who was assisting me in my astronomical exercises, to that place with a **Sextant** (similar to the one explained in the third section above). My purpose was to have him measure the **Elevation of the Pole** <note>The latitude of a specific location.</note> at Warmia with the utmost exactness using that intermediary device.

I suspected that nearly three minutes were missing in Copernicus's observation of this latitude; I was warned of this by the motion of the Sun and the maximum **obliquity of the Ecliptic** <note>The angle between the Earth's equator and its orbital path.</note>, which behaved differently than his numbers suggested. Experience itself proved this to be the case. Through many observations of both the fixed stars and the Sun made with my own instrument, the height of the Pole at that location was found to be 54 degrees and 22 1/4 minutes. This has been demonstrated more extensively by us in the first volume of our *Astronomical Exercises* <note>original: "Progymnasmatum Astronomicorum"</note>, on page 34 and following; in that place, I show the causes of Copernicus’s error in the motion of the Sun, which he had not correctly recalculated. Copernicus had presupposed the latitude of that place to be 54 degrees and 19 1/2 minutes based on his own observation. Therefore, he falls short of the exact standard by 3 minutes minus 3/4 <note>i.e., 2 1/4 minutes</note>, exactly as I had previously gathered from his own data and the numbers he constructed regarding solar motion. 

When my student returned to me, he brought back not only the sextant I had entrusted to him in perfect condition but also brought this other **Copernican Parallactic instrument** sent to me as a gift by the Canon I mentioned. As soon as I saw it, even though it was made of wood and unsuitable for actual use, it pleased me so much—out of memory for such a great artist as he was said to have made it—that I could not restrain myself from immediately composing a **Heroic Song**, which can be found in the first volume of our *Astronomical Letters* on page 295 and following. 

Indeed, a little while later, in imitation of it, I had another one made, covered with **brass plates** <note>original: "Orichalcicis laminis"</note>, with a few things changed and added for the sake of more convenient use; this is the one represented here. There are three rules: **DE**, **EF**, and **FD**. The first of these, **ED**, which is raised toward the Zenith, has another rule associated with it at the top near **D**, which can revolve on a smooth brass fitting. Both are of the same length, namely four cubits each, provided the length of the upper one is measured from the letter **D** to **F**. The part that extends further, namely **FG**, is added solely so that it may contain and restrain another longer rule within itself, for it is forked. The third rule, **EH**, which is longer and is similarly joined to the first rule **DE** near **E** (where it can also revolve), has the length required for when the two sides **DE** and **EF** form a right angle: specifically, when the rule **DF** is raised so that it aligns with the equilibrium of the Horizon.

The **sights** <term>Dioptrae <note>The sighting mechanisms or "vane" used to align the instrument with a star.</term>, through which the alignment with the stars is made, are located on the same rule near **H** and **I**. They accomplish the task through small slits parallel to the primary sight-vane, positioned above and below, according to a method we recently invented. In the Copernican instrument, there were holes in these places, through which stars are seen with great difficulty. There is also this disadvantage: the front hole located in sight-vane **I** must be larger than the other if stars are to be seen comfortably through it. When this is the case, it must necessarily occupy some portion of a degree, at least an eighth or a tenth part. However, while observing, it is unknown whether the star is seen exactly in the center of that hole or not; thus, a loss of several minutes can occur. It is a wonder how not only Copernicus, but also the ancients who used such sights, could achieve any certainty even if everything else was correct.

The equal divisions on the longer rule are marked by **transversal points** <note>A method of dividing a scale into very small, precise increments using diagonal lines.</note>, as is our custom. There are enough of them to fulfill the final number of the **Table of Sines** <term>Canon sinuum <note>A mathematical table used for calculating angles and distances in trigonometry.</term>, using the required six digits in the place where it matches the first two rules to form an equilateral triangle. 

It should also be noted that the Rule **DK**, which faces the zenith, contains a certain hollow space inside. In this cavity, a thin brass wire hangs from the upper part, with a small lead weight <note>original: "plumbeolum"</note> at the bottom near **K**, so that the wire it holds inside is not moved by the wind. Small oblong doors <note>original: "janiculæ"</note> are added to this cavity, which can be opened and closed as needed for the purpose I mentioned. This shows, by a suitable mark added there, when the side **DE** exactly faces the zenith. This can be achieved by the lower screws at **A** on the pedestal, which are turned by small iron handles at **L**. For these three rules are attached to the column **CB**, which stands on the said pedestal, in such a way that by using round pins...

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...can be turned from here and there. I added this convenience, regarding the supports, to this instrument; for it was not present in the **Copernican** one. I also added a small lead weight <term>plumula</term> <note>original: plumulam; likely a counterweight or a small plumb bob used to stabilize the instrument's moving parts.</note>, which is located near G, ensuring that rule AF remains steady at any observed angle until the numbering on the longer side is completed. I applied several other features for the sake of more convenient use and handling; I refrain from recounting each one individually, lest I seem to diminish the inventions of my predecessors by excessively correcting them.

The use of this **Parallactic** <term>Parallaticum</term> <note>Also known as a 'triquetrum' or 'Ptolemaic rules', this instrument used three hinged rods to determine the zenith distance of celestial bodies.</note> instrument is for capturing the distances of the stars from the vertex <note>The zenith, or the point directly overhead.</note>, just as **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose work defined the field for over a millennium.</note> most frequently did with the Moon, especially for seeking out its maximum latitude. However, he attained this neither with sufficient precision nor universality, as is clear from those things we restored concerning the orbit of the Moon in a certain appendix in Chapter 1 of the aforementioned volume of the **Progymnasmata** <term>Progymnaſmatum</term> <note>Referring to Brahe's major work, 'Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata' (Exercises for the Restoration of Astronomy).</note>. There, through accurate observations, it is indicated that the maximum latitude of the Moon, which occurs during full moons, is not the same as that in the **Quadratures** <term>Quadraturis</term> <note>The phases of the Moon (first and last quarters) when it is 90 degrees from the Sun.</note>; a notable difference intervenes, reaching as much as a third part of a degree, as can be seen there more extensively through its circumstances and occasions. Moreover, the angle of distance from the vertex FDE can be obtained from the **Fruitful Canon** <term>Canon fœcundus</term> <note>A Renaissance term for a table of tangents.</note> or even from the table of **Sines** <term>Sinuum</term> by means of the parts FE. For in the triangle, two equal sides, FD and ED, are given. Indeed, the third side, FE, is measured on the longer rule once the observation is completed. Therefore, the angle FDE cannot remain hidden, as it measures the distance of the star from the **Zenith** <term>Zenith</term>. Its complement to the Quadrant <note>The remaining amount needed to reach 90 degrees.</note> is the sought-after altitude. These rules also have this convenience: that they can be disassembled and folded with little effort and transported to any location. But, to tell the truth, they do not provide that precision and certainty quite consistently—no matter the diligence with which they are crafted—as do the aforementioned **Quadrants** <term>Quadrantes</term>. For it is very difficult for the rules to retain a perfectly straight line for long. And if they are longer, they bend under their own weight. But if they are shorter, that

->which they promise by their smallness to provide,<-
->they cannot.<-

# ANOTHER PARALLACTIC INSTRUMENT, OR
## RULES FACILITATING BOTH ALTITUDES AND
### Azimuths.

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...can be turned from here and there. I added this convenience, regarding the supports, to this instrument; for it was not present in the **Copernican** <note>Referring to the simplified triquetrum used by Nicolaus Copernicus, which Brahe found mechanically lacking for high-precision work.</note> one. I also added a small lead weight <term>plumula</term> <note>original: plumulam; a small lead weight used here as a counterweight or stabilizer to keep the sight-rule steady.</note>, which is located near G, ensuring that rule AF remains steady at any observed angle until the numbering on the longer side is completed. I applied several other features for the sake of more convenient use and handling; I refrain from recounting each one individually, lest I seem to diminish the inventions of my predecessors by excessively correcting them.

The use of this **Parallactic** <term>Parallaticum</term> <note>Also known as a triquetrum, this instrument used three hinged rods to calculate the distance of a star from the zenith (the point directly overhead) without needing a large, perfectly graduated arc.</note> instrument is for capturing the distances of the stars from the vertex <note>The zenith, or the point in the sky directly above the observer.</note>, just as **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose methods Brahe sought to improve.</note> most frequently did with the Moon, especially for seeking out its maximum latitude. However, he attained this neither with sufficient precision nor universality, as is clear from those things we restored concerning the orbit of the Moon in a certain appendix in Chapter 1 of the aforementioned volume of the **Progymnasmata** <term>Progymnaſmatum</term> <note>A reference to Brahe's book 'Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata' (Introduction to the New Astronomy), where he presented his revised lunar theory.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the parallactic instrument. ## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**I** indeed had built other Rules some years ago in a certain Tower, which, by their full rotation when necessary, occupied the entire space. This was done by a specific method, partly imitating the very ancient **Hipparchian** <note>Referring to Hipparchus of Nicaea (c. 190–120 BCE), the Greek astronomer who is often credited with inventing the triquetrum/parallactic rules.</note> manner (as it is said), and partly intended for more stable and convenient use through new additions of my own invention. The following figure represents their structure: in which **A D** should be understood as the first and fundamental rule, arranged according to the plane of the Horizon and rotated as needed. Its length is equal to $8 \frac{1}{2}$ cubits <note>A cubit is approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters; thus, this rule was over 12 feet long.</note>. It contains on its upper surface the numbers of the <term>canon of sines</term> <note>A table of trigonometric values used to convert the linear measurements of the rules into angular degrees.</note>, divided by <term>transversal points</term> <note>original: puncta transversalia; a method Tycho perfected using a grid of dots to read measurements to a fraction of a degree.</note> in my usual manner, with six-digit figures counted twice. Two other rules, when taken together and applied to the first, are made equal to it: namely **A B** and **B C**, each of which is $4 \frac{1}{4}$ cubits in length. The first, **A B**, is joined to the beginning of the longer one near **A**, so that it can be raised and lowered there, while simultaneously pulling the latter, **B C**, which is attached to it near **B** so that it may turn. In these two smaller rules there are no divisions, but the first one, **A B**, at least has <term>dioptras</term> and <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>original: dioptras & pinnacidia; these are the sighting vanes or "sights" through which the astronomer looks at a star.</note>, through which the alignment is made. There are slits in the lower sight near the eye at **E** and in the further one at **F**, which are parallel in every direction, so that alignment may be made through these everywhere, both in Altitudes and in <term>azimuths</term> <note>The horizontal direction or compass bearing of a celestial body.</note>. All three of these Rules are made everywhere of <term>brass</term> <note>original: Orichalcicæ; literally "orichalcum," a golden-colored brass alloy highly valued for scientific instruments.</note>, most finely smoothed and polished, possessing a quadrilateral shape of sufficient size so that they are not subject to bending.

However, so that the two upper rules can be more conveniently raised and lowered, an upright support has been added to them, indicated by **L N O P**, which also has its own bracing below, represented by **P V Z**. Near **V** there is a **Screw**, which directs the brace itself upon the circular plate at **W** on the established base of the entire Instrument (of which more later) in every direction, so that it does not weigh itself down by its own mass. This structure rising toward the **Zenith** <note>The point directly overhead.</note> has the advantage of raising the uppermost rules (as I said) and lowering them at will, while at the same time confining them within their own plane so they are not pulled to one side or the other. They are raised and lowered by means of a small cord **K L N M**, which has a pulley near **L N** above, over which it is easily pulled. This same cord is attached to the first rule near **K**, where a metallic fitting is placed that turns on a round pin; it is open enough on the inside so that the sight at **F** can be discerned from the eye near the sight **E** from all sides without any obstacle. It also has the advantage that the position of the Instrument can be examined by a <term>plumb line</term> that ends at **Q**, to see whether **A D** is exactly aligned with the Horizon and if **L N P O** is perfectly *orthogonal* <note>original: Orthogonaliss.; meaning perfectly vertical or at a right angle to the horizon.</note> to it. Although this same thing can also be checked from the plumb line **B R**, for it ought to be exactly halfway between **A** and **C**.

Furthermore, so that the Rules may be raised more conveniently and remain stationary in a set place (as desired), and also so they do not wobble from side to side but maintain the same plane with the lower and longer body throughout, however they are moved up or down, two **Quadrants** <note>Quarter-circle guides used to steady the movement of the rules.</note> **I H G** have been added to them on both sides. The first Rule is contained and moves within these, with a steady lead weight <note>original: plumulâ</note> attached near **H**. These Quadrants are covered everywhere with brass plates. From the center **A** to the circumference **I**, it is nearly three cubits. Finally, when the divisions are to be counted after an observation is completed—as in an *example case*—they are indicated very precisely by a steel index. On both sides there are steel weights which also help in holding rule **B C** so that it does not fall back by its own weight.

Additionally, the support or base (of which I spoke before) in which the whole Instrument rests on one side, especially the rear part, and revolves, is to be understood by **X W**. In this, near **T** at the top, is an **endless screw**, similar to those I spoke of before regarding the semicircular Instrument. This screw, raised and lowered by the handle at **T**, also correctly directs this entire Instrument, so that the lower and longest Rule corresponds exactly to the plane of the Horizon and fits it perfectly. Finally, the outermost part of the Rule **D** rests on a circular wall, expressed by the letters **Y**. Beneath it, on the same firm Wall, it has a brass azimuthal circle, suitably subdivided and analogous to the surface of the Horizon, corresponding to the Altitude of the first base. It is twelve cubits in Diameter.

The use of the entire Instrument is such that, by its benefit, it reveals both altitudes (through the Rules themselves, where the lowest one is reached) and the azimuths themselves, where its extremity near the index at **D** touches and marks the azimuthal Horizon. The Altitudes are obtained as follows: Since both sides of the two rules **A B** and **B C** are equal, and the third side **A C** is established according to Observation and counting, the angle **B A C**, showing the sought-after altitude, cannot remain unknown. The same is accomplished by another method if one counts where the plumb line **B R** touches the lower rule in its divisions, starting from **A**. For then there is given...

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

A <term>right-angled triangle</term> <note>original: Triangulum rectangulum; Tycho uses the fixed lengths of the instrument's arms to form a triangle, allowing him to calculate angles using trigonometry.</note> is thus provided; one side of which is known from the construction [of the device], while the other is measured according to the <term>observation</term>. Therefore, the angle at A, which was being sought, cannot remain unknown. As to how <term>altitudes</term> and <term>azimuths</term> <note>Altitude refers to the height of a celestial body above the horizon; azimuth refers to its horizontal direction (e.g., North, East, etc.).</note> are applied, this must be learned from <term>astronomical</term> practice, as we have suggested before.

This <term>instrument</term> <note>Referring to the large parallactic rules described on the previous page.</note>, because it exceeds the previous <term>rules</term> in size and length, displays all measurements more precisely and is more stable and suitable for use—especially for taking <term>altitudes</term>. In tracking <term>azimuths</term> at the same time, however, there is some inherent difficulty due to the very size and mass of the <term>instrument</term>. This can nevertheless be avoided by applying a certain diligence: the lower <term>rule</term> is positioned before the <term>observation</term> and fixed immovably at a specific azimuth toward which the star to be observed is moving. Then, the plumb lines should be checked before the <term>altitude</term> is taken, so that everything is correctly arranged, both according to the level of the <term>horizon</term> and the vertical plane.

# ZODIACAL ARMILLARY SPHERES.

<note>original: *Armillæ Zodiacales*. These were complex instruments composed of nested metal rings representing the great circles of the heavens, used to determine the coordinates of stars relative to the ecliptic (the Sun's path).</note>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

A <term>right-angled triangle</term> <note>original: *Triangulum rectangulum*; Tycho uses the fixed lengths of the instrument's arms to form a triangle, allowing him to calculate angles—specifically the zenith distance—using basic trigonometry.</note> is thus provided; one side of which is known from the construction [of the device], while the other is measured according to the <term>observation</term>. This <term>instrument</term> <note>Referring here to the large parallactic rules described on the previous page, which Tycho is comparing to smaller versions.</note>, because it exceeds the previous <term>rules</term> <note>The "rules" (regulae) are the long, graduated wooden or metal rods that make up the frame of the measuring device.</note> in size and length, displays all measurements more precisely and is more stable and suitable for use—especially for taking <term>altitudes</term>. In tracking <term>azimuths</term> at the same time, however, there is some inherent difficulty due to the very size and mass of the <term>instrument</term>. <meta>This page continues the transition from describing instruments used for measuring altitudes and azimuths to more complex devices designed for directly determining the longitude and latitude of celestial bodies. The author, Tycho Brahe, critiques older designs and introduces his own improved version of the zodiacal armillary sphere.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

The preceding <term>instruments</term> <note>Referring to the quadrant and the large parallactic rules described earlier.</note> were primarily intended for the observation of <term>altitudes</term> and <term>azimuths</term> <note>Altitude is the vertical angle above the horizon; azimuth is the horizontal angle from the North or South point.</note> from the heavens, either separately or together. However, because the astronomical application of these measurements largely requires the <term>calculation of triangles</term>—a method not easily accessible to everyone and, in itself, a tedious labor for those who shy away from such work—other machines were devised. By these, the <term>longitudes</term> and <term>latitudes</term> of the stars, which are the two primary things sought, can be obtained with minimal effort and without troublesome calculation.

Among these, I find that two were especially in use among the ancients. One they called the **Armillary Instrument**, which was used by **Hipparchus** and **Ptolemy** and received its name from them. The other is called the **Torquetum**, an instrument devised (as I believe) and used by the **Arabs** or **Chaldeans**. On flat, circular surfaces, it performs the same function as the former does with <term>armillae</term> <note>original: *armillis*; rings or hoops of metal.</note>. I have not yet wished to have this latter instrument constructed because, if the machine were of the required size, it would be weighed down by its own mass and become unfit for use. Nevertheless, if anyone desires its construction, they may consult the *Caesarean Astronomy* of **Apian** near the end, or **Schöner's** booklet on the same, which is prefixed to the observations of that most refined man, **Regiomontanus**, and his student **Walther**, a citizen of Nuremberg. There, his wish will be fulfilled.

I have, however, already had an **Armillary Instrument** constructed, in which I have changed several things used by **Hipparchus** and **Ptolemy** for the sake of more certain convenience. You see its <term>representation</term> <note>original: *Ideam*; likely referring to a diagram on the page or an upcoming illustration.</note> here. This instrument consists of at least four circular rings (whereas my predecessors used five or six), the first of which, **E B G N H**, represents the **Meridian**. It must be arranged to be analogous to the celestial one. In this ring, near **C D**, are the poles of the <term>equator</term>, which are raised or lowered until they correspond exactly to the latitude of the place where the observation is being made. This is checked by the <term>plumb line</term> **B S** hanging from above near the <term>Zenith</term>, where the division of the Meridian marks the complement of the altitude of the pole, and it is removed by the same amount below from the other pole.

This Meridian contains all the other rings within it. The first is **C F I H**, which carries the second, the **Zodiacal** ring **P O Q N**, transversely. Its poles are at **I K**, which are distant from the previously mentioned <term>equinoctial</term> <note>equatorial</note> poles by the difference between the poles of the <term>ecliptic</term> and the equator—that is, the maximum <term>declination of the Zodiac</term> <note>This angle is known as the obliquity of the ecliptic, the tilt of the Earth's axis.</note>, which we have several times determined with great exactness to be **23 degrees and 31.5 minutes**, slightly different from our predecessors. Through the poles of this Zodiac passes a polished <term>brass</term> <note>original: *Orichalcicus*</note> tube **I A K**, having a cylinder near the center **A**. By these, the convenience of the complete rings used by the ancients is supplied to us. Furthermore, another ring serving for <term>latitudes</term> is revolved around these same poles of the Zodiac, though inside it, indicated by **K L R M**.

Moreover, the Zodiacal ring itself has its four <term>slotted sights</term> <note>original: *pinnacidia rimosa*; these are plates with narrow slits used for aligning the instrument with a star.</note>, in our usual manner, three of which are seen near the letters **Q**; the fourth is hidden. Likewise, the latitude ring also has four of this kind near **R**, where the fourth is also concealed. All the sights are made of **brass**, just as all the rings are plated in brass and have the divisions of the degrees on the outer part made by the **transversal points** <note>Tycho's "transversal" method of graduation allowed for more precise readings of fractions of a degree.</note> used by us.

The Meridian itself consists of solid **steel** <note>original: *Chalybe*</note> so that it may more firmly support the inner rings. It measures **three cubits** <note>approx. 4.5 feet or 1.4 meters</note> in diameter. From this, one may gather the size of the inner and contiguous parts. Furthermore, these rings have a <term>pedestal</term> made of dense, solid iron upon which they entirely rest, indicated by the letters **V T D W Y Z**. There is a hole near **S** for the lead weight of the plumb line. In the lower part of this pedestal, at **b**, there are screws passing through an iron circle in four equidistant places; these, placed upon four stone columns protected by iron plates at **a**, can raise and lower the whole instrument so that all its rings correspond exactly in their planes and paths to the celestial circles they represent.

Additionally, four iron supports **X** have been added so that the pedestal and the entire instrument do not wobble from side to side due to its size. Steps **d** have also been added for climbing when necessary during observation or counting. This instrument also requires a unique small <term>tower</term>, the roof of which can be half-removed during observation, while the remaining part can be rotated as desired.

The use of these armillaries, like those of **Ptolemy**, is specifically for taking the **longitudes** and **latitudes** of the stars. It requires two observers: one of them fixes any sight on the Zodiacal ring at the position of some known fixed star. Looking through both slits, he views that same fixed star on either side of the axis **I K**, and thus holds all the rings firmly so that the plane of the armillary ecliptic matches the celestial one in every way. Meanwhile, the second observer moves any of the other sights on the Zodiac, as...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the construction of his improved Zodiacal Armillary Sphere, contrasting it with older, more cumbersome designs like the Torquetum. He details the use of steel and brass components, and the inclusion of precise sights and a rotating observatory roof to measure star coordinates without complex trigonometry.</summary>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...to that Star, whose longitude he is busy seeking, it will be most convenient: until he sees that Star exactly through both slits [of the sight] alongside the said axis; for then the position of the same sight <term>numbered on the Zodiacal Ring</term> <note>original: *pinnacidii in Zodiacali Armilla numeratus*; the Zodiacal Ring represents the path of the Sun, and the "sight" is a sliding piece used to align with a star.</note> reveals the sought-after longitude. Furthermore, to determine the latitude, the <term>Ring of latitudes</term> <note>original: *Armilla latitudinum*; one of the nested rings in the instrument specifically used to measure the angle above or below the Ecliptic.</note> must be moved to that same longitude of the <term>Ecliptic</term> <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the celestial sphere.</note> which the star was found to have, and then one of the sights on it must be moved up or down until that Star is seen through both slits both above and below, near the Cylinder at **A**. For then the sight itself will show the latitude one wished to find, to be counted from the Ecliptic on the said Ring of latitude either above or below, according to whether it is <term>Southern</term> <note>original: *Australis*</note> or <term>Northern</term> <note>original: *Borea*</note>; and thus both the longitude and latitude of the proposed star are easily made clear. 

Although indeed this Armillary Instrument is convenient for measuring the positions of stars quickly and promptly, and as constructed by us in this explained form, it allows its rings <note>original: *armillas*</note> to wobble outside their proper plane less than other versions do, yet not even so does it guard against it so exactly that an error of one or two <term>minutes</term> <note>A minute of arc is 1/60th of a degree.</note> might not creep in from this. This happens especially because the Zodiacal Ring does not rotate in equilibrium everywhere, but by its own weight it pulls itself and the others somewhat out of their proper planes, and in various ways. And for this reason, it has happened that we use such rings more rarely, especially when the highest precision is required. Rather, we have devised other <term>Equatorial rings</term> <note>Instruments aligned with the Earth's equator rather than the Ecliptic.</note> not subject to this flaw. This issue can in no way be corrected in the larger Zodiacal instruments because of the even heavier weight of the rings. In smaller ones, it is indeed not so noticeable. **But**

->**these, by their smallness, do not allow**<-
->**exact observation in minutes.**<-

<meta>This page follows a title plate identifying the Equatorial Armillary Spheres. It begins the detailed technical "Explanation" (Explicatio) of the instrument's design, focusing on the mechanical improvements made by Tycho Brahe to ensure stability and precision compared to earlier models.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

We have therefore devised certain **Equatorial Armillaries** <note>Also called rings or armillary spheres; "Equatorial" refers to their alignment with the Earth's equator</note>, which are arranged in equilibrium on the Axis of the World's Poles. They are balanced so that they are not pulled out of alignment in either direction by their own weight or by their rotation, nor can they be forced away from the exact standard of the plane of the Circles which they represent. This stability was lacking in earlier versions for the reasons previously mentioned. Their form is as shown in the accompanying illustration. There are only three Armillaries with a smooth axis, which measure the equatorial distances of the stars as well as their declinations <note>Declination is the celestial equivalent of latitude, measuring how far north or south an object is from the celestial equator</note>.

The first and largest of these, **A E S F**, which contains and supports the others, fulfills the role of the **Meridian**. It has a diameter of nearly 4 cubits <note>A cubit is approximately 18–20 inches; this instrument was massive, roughly 6 feet in diameter</note>. From this, the other interior parts are to be measured. They must also be arranged and regulated according to its plane. Near **A** and at the opposite location at **D**, it has a certain smooth axis (as I mentioned), which revolves with the interior Armillary and serves for measuring Declinations. In the middle of this axis (which is also the Center of all the Armillaries), it supports a Cylinder at **G**, fixed to it orthogonally <note>at a right angle</note>, of the same diameter as the axis itself.

The Axis, along with the Meridian which it passes through diametrically, must be raised until the plumb line **B V**, at the top and bottom, touches the complement of the Altitude of the Pole <note>The "co-latitude," calculated as 90 degrees minus the observer's latitude</note> on its divisions, which mark individual minutes. It must be placed so that it is equidistant from the plane of the Meridian, or lightly touching it on both sides.

The interior Armillary, which I said serves for Declinations, is represented by **N M O P**. This can sometimes be joined to the plane of the Meridian, and again rotated within it in any direction, as the position of the star to be observed requires. In two places, where it encircles the other transverse Armillary (which is assigned to the **Equator**) by passing through it at right angles, it has a plate on the outside indicating the Degrees and Minutes of the equator on both sides—that is, from one side as well as the opposite side. It also contains its <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>Sights or "pinnules" used to align the eye with the star</note> in four places, or Quadrants; at least two of these are shown here at **M** and **O**. In these, there are small slits parallel and equidistant to the Cylinder at **G**, and also to the Axis **A D** on the other side. Each Quadrant is divided on the outer part of the Armillary closest to the eye into 90 Degrees, and each of these into 60 minutes in our usual manner, often indicated by transverse points <note>Tycho’s "diagonal scale" method, which allowed for much more precise readings than standard ticks</note>.

The other transverse Armillary, which I said performs the duties of the **Equator**, is shown by **H L K I**. This is also firmly attached to the aforementioned Meridian in two opposite places at **C** and its diameter, and it stands exactly at right angles to it, remaining immobile therein. It is also subtly distributed into all the Degrees of the Circle and their minutes on its outer part. It has four <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>Sights</note> made in the same way as the previous one, two of which, **I K**, are shown here on one part of the semicircle. The remaining two are to be understood as being in the other hemisphere.

All the <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>Sights</note> are skillfully crafted from brass. The Meridian itself is made entirely of solid steel, so that it is strong enough to support the remaining interior parts. The two Armillaries that cross each other within it are fitted with a strong brass plate only on the back part (of which we spoke), on which the divisions also exist; for they are not covered in brass on all sides, in order to have less weight. Nevertheless, they are constructed from wooden slats forced into a round shape and glued together in many layers, so artfully composed that they do not consist of a single solid piece of wood, but of several thin ones smoothed lengthwise. By this precaution, they are glued together so that as one piece grows upward, the next is applied to it in the opposite way—that is, the part facing the top and the root are alternately swapped. For experience has taught me that wooden material for Instruments must be treated in this way so that they are less subject to warping and change. For this purpose, the best of all woods are walnut and that species of fir which the Germans call <term>Feuchtenholz</term> <note>original: *Feuchtenholz*; usually refers to spruce or fir wood</note>, provided they are without defects and have been dried for a long time.

Afterward, the Armillaries thus glued from various wooden strips and brought to the correct form and size, are wrapped with a thin linen cloth or parchment glued to them. They are then encrusted and colored with red lead mixed with white lead and linseed oil, so that they feel the change of the air less <note>A Renaissance method of weatherproofing scientific instruments</note>.

Furthermore, these Armillaries rest on a <term>pedeſtali</term> <note>Pedestal or base</note> also made of solid and strong wood of a peculiar shape, as you see, designated by the letter **Y**. The square base at the bottom has screws at each corner at **X**, by which the whole Instrument is directed into the required position analogous to the celestial Circles it mimics. Four iron supports are also added toward its upper part where they lie adjacent to the meridian, serving as a fulcrum and stronger support so the Armillaries do not wobble; one of these is marked by **F b X**, another by **E A X**. The remaining two are on the back side. In that place where this pedestal touches the Meridian, it is round like a semicircle, so that the Meridian resting on it touches it on all sides and leans firmly upon it. For which also...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the construction of his large-scale Equatorial Armillary, detailing the use of steel, brass, and laminated wood to prevent warping and ensure the precision of its astronomical measurements.</summary>
<keywords>Equatorial Armillary, Meridian, Declination, celestial equator, pinnacidia, astronomical sights, instrument making, laminated wood, Feuchtenholz, Tycho Brahe</keywords>

catchword: ARMIL-

# EXPLANATION OF CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

For this reason, four metallic clamps <note>tenacula</note> are placed near **F**, **S**, **R**, and **E**, within which the **Meridian** is held and can be rotated gradually and firmly until its Axis corresponds with the World Axis <note>the imaginary line around which the heavens appear to rotate</note>; in this way, it is fixed immovably. Below, on the sides of this pedestal on either side of the hole containing the lead weight at **V**, four images are skillfully painted for the sake of decoration: these represent the figures of **Ptolemy**, **Albategnius** <note>al-Battani, a famous 9th-century Arab astronomer</note>, and **Copernicus**; they also represent our own <note>the author, Tycho Brahe</note>. Two of these—namely Copernicus and mine—are visible nearby. The remaining two are hidden on the other side. Other matters concerning this pedestal—such as the crossbeams near **g**, and any other parts added for the sake of either stability or decoration—are sufficiently clear from the illustration itself to anyone looking closely.

The use of this Instrument (as I have said) is for observing both the **Declinations** <note>the distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator</note> and the **Right Ascensions** <note>the distance of a star measured eastward along the celestial equator</note> of the Stars. For when the **Armilla** <note>the ring</note> **D N M P** is rotated, its **Sights** <term>pinnacidia; sliding viewing-slots used to align the instrument with a star</term> are moved back and forth along its Axis until any Star is aligned parallel to the **Cylinder G**, both above and below. Then, the sight itself reveals the desired Declination on the outer brass plate at the marked Divisions.

For the other measurement—Right Ascension—a suitable sight is moved along the transverse ring toward some Star whose Right Ascension is already known from elsewhere. Meanwhile, the remaining sight, whichever is most convenient, is moved by another Observer (for it is necessary to have two people for this) toward any other star, until it too is seen on both sides of the Instrument’s Axis. Afterward, the Arc intercepted between both sights is numbered; this shows the **Ascensional Difference** of the stars, which—when added or subtracted as the position requires—reveals the previously unknown Right Ascension from the known one.

The same can be achieved if the **Declination Armilla** is fixed at the location of a known Right Ascension and the known star is viewed through it, while in the meantime another sight on the **Equatorial Armilla** is moved until the star whose Right Ascension is sought is aligned around the Axis. Then, that same sight shows the desired angle on the outer limb <note>the outer edge or scale</note> of the Equator without any need for addition or subtraction. To what end the rightly observed Declinations and Right Ascensions of the Stars lead, and how easy it is to derive both **Longitude** and **Latitude** from them, is well known to those practiced in the **Astronomical Arena** <note>original: "Astronomica Palaestra"; referring to the rigorous training ground of professional astronomy</note>; and we shall explain this elsewhere in a concise manner, God willing.

->H<-

# OTHER EQUATORIAL 
# ARMILLARIES.
<note>original Latin title: "ARMILLÆ ALIÆ AEQUATORIÆ"</note>
