Tycho Brahe redesigned these classical instruments to achieve unprecedented precision. The accompanying illustration (described in the metadata) shows a sophisticated version with multiple intersecting rings that allow an astronomer to directly read a star’s coordinates in relation to the celestial equator.</note>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Tycho Brahe's astronomical instruments, moving from the general introduction of equatorial armillaries to a specific "second" design characterized by a rotating equatorial ring.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

I have also seen to the construction of other <term>Equatorial Armillaries</term><note>A series of nested metal rings used to determine the positions of stars relative to the celestial equator.</note> of the same size, the design of which you see here. The outer ring represents the <term>Meridian</term><note>The Great Circle passing through the celestial poles and the observer's zenith.</note> (as before). It is made entirely of steel, polished perfectly smooth on all sides, and marked with divisions for the whole circle, distributed so that it even contains individual minutes of arc. In this ring, all other parts—such as the plumb line, the poles, and the axis—are to be understood just as in the previous instruments. Contained within this outer ring are three further rings, so that the entire instrument consists of four, one more than the previous model: this is in addition to the smooth axis and the cylinder fixed to it, which also conveniently serve the function of a ring.

This instrument has three interior rings (excluding the Meridian) because the Equator is designed to revolve, rather than remaining stationary as in the previous model. Therefore, it requires the ring *I F G* to support it and hold it at a right angle; this ring is also made of pure steel to ensure it is strong enough for the task, though it is marked with no divisions. The Equator itself, indicated by *P S R*, has its own divisions down to individual minutes, marked on both surfaces as required for discerning either equatorial distances or moments of time. <term>Sights</term><note>original Latin: "pinnacidia"; small plates or devices used to align the eye with a star.</note> are fitted to it at *R* and *P*, along with several others not shown here that assist in observation; their form is the same as in the preceding model, as is the method for subdividing the degrees.

Another interior ring, designated by the letters *N K L M*, is suitable for investigating <term>Declinations</term><note>The angular distance of a heavenly body north or south of the celestial equator.</note>, just as before. It can rotate within the equatorial ring, which it crosses at a right angle, so that it indicates the equatorial points at its place of contact by means of suitable small plates positioned at *S* and *M*. It further possesses its own sights at *Q* and *O*, and two others not depicted here but to be understood as being on the opposite side. It also contains divisions marked in minutes for each of its four quadrants.

All these rings contained within the Meridian are covered everywhere with thin plates of <term>brass</term><note>original Latin: "Orichalcum"; a golden-colored alloy of copper and zinc frequently used for scientific instruments.</note>; this is done with such precision and smoothness that you would say they were cast entirely from solid brass. The smooth axis is also made of brass, as are the sights themselves. The plumb line *R T* consists of a thin brass wire, as is our usual custom. Any other details omitted here you may grasp by looking at the illustration, as with the previous descriptions.

These rings rest upon a support or pedestal made of solid steel, designated by *X T V Y Z* and the other letters placed between them. The upper semicircular part, *G W V*, continuously surrounds and supports the Meridian (which contains the other rings) from below. Four clamps are added, just as in the previous model. The rest of the form of this pedestal is clear enough from the figure itself: some parts were added for the sake of beauty rather than utility or strength, though even these provide support. As stated before, everything is made of solid steel.

Beneath these lies a thick iron circle *Z*, which has screws at four equidistant locations. These are set into small iron fittings on a square stone base, so that by turning these screws, the whole instrument can be directed exactly as the rings (corresponding to the heavens) require. This is done with the help of the plumb line *B T*, which aligns the plane of the mechanical meridian with the celestial one and further organizes its arrangement according to the <term>altitude of the Pole</term><note>The observer's latitude.</note>. Thus, it makes all the interior rings correspond to the celestial circles they emulate. The entire machine is quite robust in itself and not easily subject to warping, as it is metallic throughout.

**Its use** is similar to the previous one, except that here the Equator may be revolved within the Meridian as desired. Declinations are taken just as before on the Declination ring, using the central cylinder at *A* and the appropriate sights. Equatorial distances, or <term>Right Ascensions</term><note>The distance of a point east of the First Point of Aries, measured along the celestial equator.</note>, are measured on the transverse equatorial ring as before, except that here the steel ring that carries them may also have some use in this procedure. 

There is also this advantage: when a star is aligned at its proper right ascension, the point of the equator passing the Meridian indicates the right ascension of the <term>Midheaven</term><note>original Latin: "medii Coeli"; the point where the ecliptic crosses the local meridian.</note>. Consequently, it also indicates the very moment of time, provided the exact position of the Sun is known, such as our Tables provide. The Equator can also be fixed to the Meridian, and thus, by moving a sight upon it, the distance of any star from the Meridian can be taken and the moment of time known, just as in the previous model (which I neglected to mention before). Thus, through these two armillary instruments, both Right Ascensions and Declinations—along with the most precise moments of time, all of which are of the greatest use in astronomy—can be conveniently measured from the heavens.

If anyone requires more regarding the construction and use of this instrument, let them consult the second chapter of the first volume of our *Astronomical Exercises*<note>original Latin: "Progymnasmatum"; Tycho's major work "Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata".</note>, from page 250 to the end of 252. However, for the most part, the essential details have been sufficiently indicated here.

<summary>This page describes a second, more complex version of an equatorial armillary instrument featuring a rotating equatorial ring and a sturdy steel pedestal. It explains how the device is used to simultaneously measure a star's declination, right ascension, and the precise time of observation.</summary>
<keywords>Equatorial Armillary, Tycho Brahe, Right Ascension, Declination, brass instruments, Midheaven, astronomical measurement, Progymnasmata</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe's description of his second set of Equatorial Armillaries, moving from the technical mechanics of the rings to the practical and philosophical considerations of their portability and the astronomer's role in society.</meta>
<meta>catchword: ARMILLARY-</meta>

## *EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.*

I will also add this: these <term>Armillaries</term><note>A model of objects in the sky, consisting of a spherical framework of rings, centered on Earth or the Sun, that represent lines of celestial longitude and latitude.</note> can be dismantled by specific screws and then reassembled. The same can be done with the steel pedestal or support itself, so that they may be conveniently transported to other locations. This is something that should be achieved in all instruments, wherever it can be done without damaging them. For it is necessary for an Astronomer to be a <term>citizen of the world</term><note>original Greek: κοσμοπολίτην (kosmopolitēn). Tycho argues that scientific pursuit should not be hindered by national borders or local politics.</note>, so that if necessity and use require it, he is not so bound to any one region that he is unwilling or unable to move elsewhere, freely and gladly taking his instruments with him. 

For since there are very few who occupy themselves with these arduous disciplines and exercises, and since among politicians—who wish to preside over the administration of the State—one very rarely finds any who are greatly delighted by these things and think it wise to cherish and support them; but rather far more who, out of ignorance, are averse to them and judge them useless; the student of Divine Astronomy ought not to be subject to such ignorant judgments. Instead, he should disregard them from on high, and think nothing better than to stay awake for these studies, and not be moved by the insolence of others. And when they become too troublesome, whether they be politicians or others, he should be prepared to move himself and his belongings elsewhere. He should do this so readily that not even his <note>Tycho is paraphrasing Ovid here: "Natalem scilicet proprium ad deum..." regarding the innate love of one's homeland.</note> native soil—which charms all people with its sweetness and does not allow one to be unmindful of it—should teach him to place it above these celestial and sublime concerns, which bear the traces of immortality. Instead, with a constant and persevering mind, he should always reflect within himself upon every turn of events, for:

> ->*Everywhere the earth lies below, the Sky lies open everywhere above,*<-
> ->*And any shore is a homeland to a brave man.*<-

# LARGE EQUATORIAL AR-
# MILLARIES, CONSISTING OF
### a circle and a half.

## *EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.*

I will also add this: these <term>Armillaries</term><note>A model of the celestial sphere, consisting of a framework of rings that represent lines of celestial longitude and latitude. Tycho's "equatorial" versions were specialized for measuring coordinates relative to the celestial equator.</note> can be dismantled by specific screws and then reassembled. The same can be done with the steel pedestal or support itself, so that they may be conveniently transported to other locations. This is something that should be achieved in all instruments, wherever it can be done without damaging them. For it is necessary for an Astronomer to be a <term>citizen of the world</term><note>original Greek: κοσμοπολίτην (kosmopolitēn). Tycho argues that a scientist's loyalty belongs to the truth of the stars rather than a specific kingdom, allowing him to move where his work is best supported.</note>, so that if necessity and use require it, he is not so bound to any one region that he is unwilling or unable to move elsewhere, freely and gladly taking his instruments with him.

For since there are very few who occupy themselves with these arduous disciplines and exercises, and since among politicians—who wish to preside over the administration of the State—one very rarely finds any who are greatly delighted by these things and think it wise to cherish and support them; but rather far more who, out of ignorance, are averse to them and judge them useless; the student of Divine Astronomy ought not to be subject to such ignorant judgments. Instead, he should disregard them from on high, and think nothing better than to stay awake for these studies, and not be moved by the insolence of others. And when they become too troublesome, whether they be politicians or others, he should <note>The text likely continues on the next page, suggesting the astronomer should seek a more hospitable patron.</note>

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the modular and portable design of his large equatorial armillaries. He uses this technical feature to justify his philosophy that an astronomer must be a "citizen of the world," independent of the whims or ignorance of local politicians.</summary>
<keywords>Equatorial Armillary, portability, Tycho Brahe, citizen of the world, scientific independence, 16th-century astronomy, patronage</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of Tycho Brahe's astronomical instruments, moving from the general philosophy of portable instruments to the specific technical details of the "Large Equatorial Armillary." The catchword "nacidia" from the previous page concludes the term "pinnacidia" (sighting vanes).</meta>

### EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**A**lthough the two machines of <term>Equatorial Armillaries</term><note>A system of rings aligned with the celestial equator and the Earth's axis, used to measure the positions of stars.</note> already depicted and explained (which also cost us a great deal) serve the purpose I mentioned with sufficient accuracy, I nevertheless later devised and saw to the construction of others that require fewer components. These use only a single, complete revolving <term>Armillary</term><note>A ring or hoop of metal.</note> and a second, stationary semicircular one. By these two, all the measurements I described regarding the previous instruments are carried out not only conveniently, but also much more quickly and without any of the obstacles that the earlier ones present when a sighting must be made near the <term>Equatorial Ring</term> itself. For in those cases, the front part of the ring obstructs the view; likewise, in the <term>Declination Ring</term>, sighting becomes difficult near the <term>Equator</term> itself, to say nothing of certain other impediments. These new versions have this further advantage: they can be made much larger. Therefore, the **Degrees** with their divisions can be displayed even more distinctly, while still remaining suitable for use in terms of stability and rotation—and this more easily than the previous ones. The form of these huge and exceptionally efficient <term>Armillaries</term> is the one we show here, as far as it was possible to represent it on a flat surface.

The letters **A** indicate the largest <term>Armillary</term>, which is reinforced everywhere with **brass** plates and has a diameter of 7 cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length; Tycho's Danish cubit was roughly 63 centimeters. A 7-cubit ring would be over 4 meters across.</note>. Consequently, the **Degrees** are so large that individual minutes are subdivided into four parts. Its **Axis**, marked **B**, is made of steel and is perfectly smooth all around; however, it is hollow inside so that it does not cause trouble by its weight. It is three inches in diameter, and near its midpoint, a smooth **brass cylinder**, **C**, is attached. Closely around this cylinder, next to the **Axis**, two **brass rulers**, **E** and **F**, rotate on both sides. Near **F**, at the surface of the **Armillary**, they have <term>sighting vanes</term><note>original: pinnacidia; small plates used to align the eye with a target.</note> with <term>sights</term><note>original: dioptra; the technical term for sighting holes or slits.</note> consisting of slits parallel to the **Cylinder**. The remaining spaces you see at the letters **G** are cross-beams or frameworks that hold and restrain the entire **Armillary**, preventing it from losing its roundness or flatness due to its great size.

Further down, near **D**, the axis ends in a smooth point made of square-hardened steel, which fits into a similar pointed hole. This can be nudged slightly in any direction by means of screws placed on either side. Upon this rests that continuous smooth **Axis**, which rotates the entire **Armillary**. The iron plate beneath this is fixed into a large, long stone column of 4 cubits. Two of these columns stand on a firm foundation below the ground; the other two above them feature, on the front part, a likeness of **King Atlas of Mauretania**, **I**, artistically carved into the same stone. With his crown, he touches the **stone globe**, **H**, placed on top of the column for the sake of ornament. The uppermost part of the **Axis** rests upon another iron support near **C**, which is bifurcated near **K** and branches into two arms, each of which is strongly fixed to a large, smooth column of solid stone. These columns are spaced far enough apart to provide a doorway for entering the **Instrument**, and are indicated by **M** and **L**.

Upperward, near **C**, is a **Screw** that moves the iron cross-piece on which the **Axis** rests, so that it may correspond to the plane of the <term>Meridian</term><note>The imaginary circle passing through the celestial poles and the observer's zenith.</note>. It is also adjusted in another way both there and lower down near **D**, and is moved firmly until it corresponds exactly to the <term>World Axis</term><note>original: Axi Mundi; the line around which the heavens appear to rotate, passing through the celestial poles.</note>; then it is secured. The other semicircular ring is indicated by the letters **C**; it is supported immovably in the plane of the <term>Equator</term> by eight small stone columns marked **P**, with an equal number of iron clamps existing near **Q**. These hold and direct it until they mark out an exact semicircle around the middle of the axis and, furthermore, remain constantly inclined according to the plane of the <term>Equator</term>. It is also covered everywhere in **brass** and has a diameter of 9 cubits; therefore, it is capable of even finer subdivisions than the main **Armillary** itself. This semicircular ring is two cubits wider than the full **Armillary** so that one can conveniently pass between the two as needed. By the stone **Stairs**, **N**, outside the ring (which are not fully shown here), one ascends and descends following the path of the **Armillary** so that the **Observer’s** eye may be conveniently applied to it. On the inside, there are other round **Stairs** indicated by the letters **T**, by which one ascends or descends around the full **Armillary** during observations, according to whether the position of the star to be noted is high or low. Near each letter **N** appears a wall which, encircling the entire **Instrument**, creates a sort of small tower and semi-crypt surrounding this whole machine. It has a roof above which can be opened in any direction desired during observation and closed afterward, so that this precious machine is safe from the harm of weather and rain.

Its **Use** is such that on the **Rotating Circular Armillary**, **Declinations**<note>The angular distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator.</note> are taken by moving the <term>sights</term> at **F** and sighting the star on both sides near the **Cylinder**, **E**. In this way, the sought-after **Declination** is read at **E**. This can be done in two ways, namely with each sight, by revolving the **Armillary** so that the other part is also brought to the eye; thus, the **Declination** of the same **Star** is checked twice, and it ought to be the same in both places. On the other semicircular ring, let its sight-vanes be moved...

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

[pin]nacidia <term>sighting vanes</term><note>Latin: *pinnacidia*. Small plates with slits or holes used to align the eye with a celestial object.</note> near **R**, until the Star to be observed is seen through those slits on both sides of **Axis B**. For then the sighting vane itself shows its <term>Equatorial distance from the Meridian</term><note>Essentially the hour angle of the star.</note> on that same semicircular <term>armilla</term><note>A ring or hoop.</note>, to be counted either toward the east or the west. From here, the exact moment of time can be known with the greatest precision from the known position of the Star and the **Sun**. But if the **Right Ascension** <term>Right Ascension</term><note>The distance of a point east of the First Point of Aries, measured along the celestial equator.</note> of any Star is to be sought, one observer should direct one of the sighting vanes toward a star that has a known position; while another observer similarly directs the remaining sighting vane toward another star whose right ascension is sought: so that both stars are seen at exactly the same instant around the same axis through the slits of the sighting vanes. For the arc thus intercepted between both sighting vanes will then reveal their <term>ascensional difference</term><note>The difference in Right Ascension between two celestial bodies.</note>, from which the sought right ascension is produced, as has been indicated in the preceding pages. If anyone requires more concerning the construction and use of this most magnificent instrument, let him consult the first book of our *Astronomical Epistles* <note>Latin: *Epistolarum Astronomicarum*. This refers to Tycho Brahe’s published correspondence which detailed his scientific methods and instruments.</note>, page 246 and the two following. There, several things are indicated more extensively, which I have omitted here for the sake of brevity. Furthermore, from the provided illustration itself, if it is

->than through long-winded<-

# THE BIPARTITE ARC FOR SMALL-
# ER DISTANCES OF THE STARS
### *serving this purpose.*

<margin>D 3</margin>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

Move the <term>sighting vanes</term><note>Latin: *pinnacidia*. These are small, adjustable plates with slits or holes used to align the observer's eye with a celestial object.</note> near **R**, until the Star to be observed is seen through those slits on both sides of **Axis B**. For then the sighting vane itself shows its <term>equatorial distance from the Meridian</term><note>In modern terms, this is the hour angle, which measures how far a star has moved past the celestial meridian.</note> on that same semicircular <term>armilla</term><note>Latin for "hoop" or "ring"; here referring to the graduated circular part of the instrument.</note>, to be counted either toward the east or the west. From here, the exact moment of time can be known with the greatest precision from the known position of the Star and the **Sun**. 

But if the **Right Ascension** <term>Right Ascension</term><note>The celestial equivalent of longitude, measuring the distance of a point east of the First Point of Aries along the celestial equator.</note> of any Star is to be sought, one observer should direct one of the sighting vanes toward a star that has a known position; while another observer similarly directs the remaining sighting vane toward another star whose right ascension is sought: so that both stars are seen at exactly the same instant around the same axis through the slits of the sighting vanes. For the arc thus intercepted between both sighting vanes will then reveal their <term>ascensional difference</term><note>The difference in Right Ascension between two celestial bodies, allowing an astronomer to calculate the position of an unknown star relative to a known one.</note>, from which the sought right ascension is produced, as has been indicated in the preceding pages. 

If anyone requires more concerning the construction and use of this most magnificent instrument, let him consult the first book of our *Astronomical Epistles*<note>Latin: *Epistolarum Astronomicarum Libri 1* (1596), a collection of Tycho Brahe’s correspondence regarding his observations and instrument designs.</note> where it is described in greater detail.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the Bipartite Arc, moving from its general purpose to a detailed explanation of its mechanical construction, materials, and specific procedure for measuring the angular distance between two stars within a 30-degree range.</meta>

<margin>D 4</margin>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

We have already dealt with those astronomical instruments which investigate the altitudes and <term>azimuths</term><note>The horizontal angle of a celestial object from the North or South point of the horizon.</note> of stars in various ways, as well as those that provide longitudes, latitudes, and additionally declinations and <term>right ascensions</term><note>The distance of a point east of the First Point of Aries, measured along the celestial equator.</note>. It remains for us to take account of those which examine the <term>intervals</term><note>Latin: *intercapedines*; the angular distance or "gap" between two stars.</note> between objects, and thereafter to discuss several others of a mixed nature. As far as the distances of the stars are concerned: in years past, an **Astronomical Radius** <note>Latin: *Radius Astronomicus*; a device also known as a cross-staff or Jacob's staff, used for measuring angles.</note> was fashioned by some, which is handy enough in itself and suitable for transport; at the same time, it is theoretically sound in its geometric demonstrations. However, in actual practice, it does not perform in its results what it promises in its concept. This is for various reasons which I will refrain from listing here; perhaps elsewhere I shall deal sufficiently with them and their correction, as far as possible. Meanwhile, I shall speak here of certain other instruments which I have devised—and used until now—which are suited for practical use and provide the required certainty. And first, I present one that provides smaller distances within the twelfth part of a circle, or one **sign** (as they call it) <note>A zodiacal sign represents 30 degrees of the 360-degree circle.</note>; it is such as the depicted figure shows.

The **FIRST** part is an oblong **Ruler** starting from **F** and ending at the transversal **B A**: its length is 4 cubits <note>Approximately 6 feet or 1.8 meters, though the "astronomical cubit" varied.</note>. On the said transversal, it has two **Cylinders** near **A** and **B**, equidistant from the common joint. From their centers, starting from point **F**, two **Arcs** **F C** and **F D** are drawn below; each of these—beyond the equidistance of the half of **A B** counted from both sides, which ought to have been represented by **K I** (though the figure does not sufficiently suggest it)—shows the start of the numbering on arc **K C** or **K D**, making fifteen degrees on each side. The remaining parts shown through **C G** and **D H**, and those interspersed around **L**, are added for the sake of the framework and support, as well as for ornament. This instrument rests upon a smooth and thick support, shown by **T R S Q**: having in the middle, where the equilibrium is, a square hole near **E**, in which it is placed on its support by a square pin. The support is arranged so that near **S** and **T**, and also **Q** and **R**, there are round iron plates which rotate on a smooth underlying cylinder (having a diameter of at least a span), with <term>small feathers</term><note>Latin: *plumulis*; likely small tension springs or friction-fitting shims.</note> on four sides holding it firmly so that it may easily stay in any position. In this manner, the instrument itself rotates only up and down. If, however, it must be tilted or reclined to the side, that is done by moving the semicircle **O N M** and tightening the screw near **N**. In this way, the whole instrument turns as one. It can also be rotated in all directions toward every part of the sky via the channel **P V**, which stands on a round axis. That channel rests upon another pedestal signified by **V** and the letter **X**, in which it also revolves in every direction with the whole instrument as needed, so that it can be directed toward the plane of any stars, as the logic of the observation requires. The interior axis of that same channel can also be raised higher in the underlying pedestal along with the instrument resting on it, and thus fixed immovably as use requires. For a solid quadrilateral piece of wood descends within the quadrilateral shape of the pedestal, which can be carried upward or let down and fixed wherever one pleases; thus the instrument can be raised or lowered together, depending on whether the stars whose interval is to be investigated are higher or lower in the sky. Within the instrument itself, as far as the limbs where the divisions are located, as well as the transversal and cylinders, everything is made of <term>brass</term><note>Latin: *Orichalcica*; specifically orichalcum, a high-quality brass alloy used for scientific instruments.</note>. The frameworks and supports are skillfully fashioned from ironwork. The rest is made of wood, cleverly assembled and reinforced with metal plates where necessary, so that the whole instrument is firm and suitable for observations.

Its use (as I said) is for conveniently measuring smaller distances; for in the sextant (of which we will speak later), which also requires two observers, this cannot be easily performed because of the proximity of their heads, which requires a suitable space: although we have remedied this inconvenience by another means, about which we will speak in turn. To ensure that the intervals of the stars which do not exceed the twelfth part of a circle are recorded by this instrument, let one observer fix a <term>sighting vane</term><note>Latin: *pinnacidium*; a small plate with a slit for precise sighting.</note> at **K** or **I**, as is most convenient: and let the other pull the one that is not fixed along the outer limb, until both have sighted the stars they are investigating around cylinders **B** and **A** on both sides; each observer attending to his own cylinder on the same side. The distance sought is then to be counted on the lower limb in degrees and <term>transversal minutes</term><note>A method of dividing degrees into smaller parts using diagonal lines to achieve high precision, a hallmark of Tycho Brahe's instruments.</note> next to the appropriate sighting vane. But if both observers move both vanes, a still greater interval can be established in the same way, provided it does not exceed the prescribed limit. It follows, therefore, that when both observers have placed each sighting vane near the very beginning of the divisions, which is at **K** and **I**, and thus through the cylinders on both sides...

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

[...cy]linders at the front, marked **A** and **B**, have established the <term>collimation</term><note>The alignment of the line of sight of an optical instrument; ensuring the path of light is accurately adjusted.</note>, so that each observer looks at their own target, arranged from the same side of the long ruler, they then sight the same star; provided, that is, they direct their vision in a parallel fashion. From this, the accuracy of the instrument can be tested—whether it is functioning correctly or not. And although the support or pedestal of this instrument is convenient enough and suitable for the required uses, nevertheless, another method which is performed on a spherical body (of which we shall speak later in the description of the **Sextant**) is even more manageable, and allows the instrument to be directed more quickly toward the plane of any stars. Yet, variety in these matters is not only pleasing but also has its own utility.

With this instrument, I have investigated especially the mutual distances of the stars in **Cassiopeia**; these are set forth in the fourth chapter of our work, *Introductory Exercises for the Restoration of Astronomy* <note>original: "Progymnasmatum Astronomiæ instaurandæ"</note>, on page 145 and those following. I compared these with the distances found earlier while the **New Star** <note>The famous supernova of 1572 in Cassiopeia, which Tycho famously observed, proving that the "heavens" were not unchangeable as Aristotle had claimed.</note> still lasted—for the sake of which those measurements were primarily taken—and I found them to be correct. If anyone possesses any instrument with which they believe they can investigate the distances of the stars, let them make a trial on these same stars of **Cassiopeia** to see if they find the same <term>angular distance</term><note>Latin: "intercapedinem"; the gap or separation between celestial bodies measured in degrees.</note> between them that we have noted in the cited passage, down to the arc-minute itself, and even to half a minute <note>Tycho is claiming an extraordinary precision of 30 arc-seconds.</note>. Otherwise, let them know that the instrument they use, and

->the method of observation, by no means<-
->stands correctly.<-

The previous page ended by suggesting that other instruments might be even more effective for "investigating" stellar distances; the title of this page completes that thought both grammatically and conceptually, as the Latin word "rimandis" (to be investigated) follows the preceding discussion.</meta>

# THE ASTRONOMICAL SEXTANT

## TRIANGULAR, FOR DISTANCES

->to be investigated.<-

<note>The term "Sextant" refers to the fact that the instrument's arc covers 60 degrees, or one-sixth of a full circle. Tycho Brahe used this specific "Triangular" version to measure the angular distance between two stars by having two observers sight them simultaneously through the adjustable arms.</note>

<note>The word "investigating" (original: "rimandis") refers back to the final sentence of the previous page. Tycho often used this word to describe the diligent, deep "probing" or "searching" of the heavens that his precision instruments made possible.</note>

<note>The illustration on this page (noted in the source metadata) depicts the instrument on its elaborate "column" or pedestal. This mounting system allowed the sextant to be tilted and rotated to align with any two stars in the sky, regardless of their orientation. The checkered floor in the engraving is a common Renaissance artistic convention, but also emphasizes the geometric precision of Tycho's observatory at Uraniborg.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the title and primary illustration for the Astronomical Sextant, one of Tycho Brahe's most important inventions for measuring the precise angular gaps between celestial bodies.</summary>

<keywords>Sextant, Tycho Brahe, astronomical instruments, angular distance, Uraniborg, stellar observation</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>The catchword "dem" at the bottom of the Latin text indicates that the next page begins with a word starting with that syllable, likely part of a phrase like "eodem modo" (in the same way). This page provides the technical specifications and operational instructions for the Astronomical Sextant described in the previous section.</meta>

-> # EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE <-

I come now to the explanation of the **Astronomical Sextant**, insofar as it is exceptionally well-suited for investigating the distances between the stars in the heavens. Since I have found through many years of experience that the **Astronomical Staff** <note>original: "Radium Astronomicum," also known as a Cross-staff; a simpler instrument Tycho found lacking in precision</note> is by no means sufficient for this task with the necessary precision and certainty, I have—driven by necessity—devised several sextants of various forms. These perform the task exactly and without any error. Among these, the ones following the design you see here seemed most convenient to me. Therefore, I had three of this kind made, differing only slightly in their framework.

The explanation of it is as follows: The frame contained within **A B C** represents the sextant itself with its joinery. Its center, at **A**, has a round cylinder. The circumference **C B**, containing a sixth part of a circle, is removed from the center by the sides **A C** and **A B**, which are four cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18–22 inches; Tycho’s "astronomical cubits" were standardized measurements for his workshop</note> long. Attached to this from the center itself is the **Rule A D** with its sighting vane <term>pinnacidium <gloss>a small plate with slits used for sighting</gloss></term> near **D**, which has slits parallel to the cylinder resting at **A**. It can be moved back and forth along the circumference and fixed wherever one pleases.

It also has another cylinder resting near **F**, equal to the first one. For this, another sighting vane is intended near **G**, to be added or removed as the use requires. The remaining intermediate parts are joined together for the sake of stability, as appears here. Near **E**, a quadrilateral hole passes through, which is placed at the balance point of the entire instrument, so that by its means the sextant can be placed upon the lower support and removed after the observation is finished.

The limb near the circumference—which has <term>transversal divisions <gloss>a method of dividing degrees into smaller minutes using diagonal lines</gloss></term> and can distinguish single minutes into four parts—is made entirely of brass <note>original: "Orichalcicus," referring to a golden-colored copper alloy like brass or bronze</note>, as are the cylinders and sighting vanes. The remaining parts are indeed made of wood, but so skillfully composed of various pieces and glued together (as we indicated previously in the explanation of the armillary spheres) that they cannot warp or change according to the fluctuations of the air. It is even covered with a linen cloth colored with oil-paint. For if all its parts were covered with metal plates, the instrument would be rendered unmanageable due to excessive weight. Indeed, it would harm itself by its own heaviness in maintaining its proper plane.

The pedestal or support on which it rests is as follows: Near **I** is a globe, encased everywhere with copper plates and perfectly round, having a diameter of a little more than two feet. Around it are four semicircular iron fittings, gripping a round iron ring <term>armilla <gloss>a ring or bracelet-like fitting</gloss></term> around the middle of the globe crosswise in eight places. All of these are as far from the surface of the globe on the inside as the thickness of one finger, or a little more. In those places where these fittings contain that ring, there are eight iron screws which drive a round plate (cut long on all sides so that it functions like a spring <note>original: "instar plumae," literally "like a feather," referring here to a flexible metal leaf or spring providing tension</note>) against the globe, so that it is gripped tenaciously by them and remains firm however it is turned. These semicircular fittings also have such a spring on the inside, in which the globe rests usefully; this is the ninth.

On the outside, it is attached by a certain square iron to a hollow square wooden column. This column passes through the lower pedestal seen around the letter **K**, so that it can be raised and lowered in it and fixed in a certain place as the convenience of the observation requires. For in taking distances at lower inclinations, the column must be lowered. In higher ones, it must be raised, so that the sightings <term>collimationes <gloss>the act of aligning a sight on a target</gloss></term> can be performed without discomfort.

Furthermore, the sextant itself is placed from above near hole **E**, as we said, upon this globe which lies beneath it. Together with the globe, it is most conveniently directed into the plane of any two stars. And so that it remains there more firmly without wavering until the observation is finished, two long and smooth rods, **I M** and **N O**, having a pointed iron tip below near **M** and **O**, are added to rest more firmly on the floor. These cross each other above near **P**. One of the observers holds them there by hand and guides them as he wishes.

The use of the instrument requires two observers. One of them, with his eye applied to the fixed sighting vane near **C**, sees a certain star on one side or the other of cylinder **A** through its slits, and thus holds the sextant unmoved by the aid of the rods near **P**. The plane of the sextant itself lies in the plane of the two stars whose distance one wishes to measure; the globe performs this service for it, on which it is rotated until both stars are seen along its plane. The second observer then moves the **Rule** with its sighting vane at **D**, until he also sights the other star on both sides of cylinder **A** through the slits. This is to be attempted until both observers have sighted their star at the same time. Then, at a given signal by voice, they stop, and the circumference of the sextant is drawn back on its globular support to the eye of either observer, so that he can conveniently count the distance **C D** intercepted by the sighting vanes, which they were seeking. Furthermore, so that even smaller distances [can be measured] in this same...

<summary>This page describes the structural components and mechanical operation of Tycho Brahe's triangular sextant, highlighting its brass-on-wood construction and its innovative universal globe mount that allows for steady, two-person observations of stellar distances.</summary>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

in the same sextant may be conveniently captured; we have devised this shortcut: so that by placing another **cylinder** at **F**, and another <term>sighting vane<gloss>pinnacidium; a small plate with slits used for sighting</gloss></term> near **G**, these two are positioned equidistant to **AE**—the line passing from the center to the middle of the circumference. Thus, while one observer looks through this sighting vane and this cylinder, the other observer attends to the sighting vane at **D** and the cylinder at **A**. The distance of the stars from the middle of the circumference (near the thirtieth degree) all the way to sighting vane **D** must be counted; in this way, no matter how small the distance might be, nothing hinders either observer from obtaining the measurement.

If you desire more details regarding this instrument, consult the first volume of my <term>Astronomical Exercises <gloss>Progymnasmata Astronomica</gloss></term>, page 247 and the three following pages. There you will find these matters explained more extensively. Indeed, in recording the distances of the **fixed stars** <note>Stars that, unlike planets, appear to remain in the same relative positions to one another</note>, which we have set forth according to the rules in the second chapter of that work and expanded with great precision according to the celestial standard, this instrument was of the greatest use. Concerning this instrument, I can testify that it provides the intervals between the stars with such certainty and precision, and so quickly and easily, that nothing more could be desired of it.

This is even evident if one considers more deeply the distances of the stars indicated and applied for use in the aforementioned book from page 195 to page 203: namely, how—when the **Declination** <note>The angular distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator</note> of the stars is also applied—it encompasses and closes the entire circuit of the sphere most exactly through the <term>ascensional differences</term> <note>The difference between the right ascension and the oblique ascension of a celestial body</note> constructed here with the help of triangles, where they are gathered together. This

# THE STEEL SEXTANT FOR
## DISTANCES TO BE MEASURED
### BY A SINGLE OBSERVER.

<note>original: "SEXTANS CHALYBEVS PRO DISTANTIIS PER UNICUM OBSERVATOREM dimetiendus." Tycho Brahe used the word "Chalybeus" (steel) to highlight the material strength of this instrument compared to his larger wooden or brass versions.</note>

<note>Diagram labels: A, B, G, H, I, K, L, M, N, O, P, Q, R, S, T, V, X, Y, Z, 9. These letters refer to the mechanical parts of the instrument depicted in the accompanying engraving, including the sighting vanes, the precision screws for adjustment, and the central pivot.</note>

<note>This instrument was a smaller version of the sextant, mounted on a sophisticated pedestal that allowed it to be tilted and rotated in any direction. This mechanical flexibility allowed a single observer to align the fixed and movable sights with two different stars to measure the angular distance between them without needing a second assistant to hold the instrument steady.</note>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous introduction of the Steel Sextant. While the previous page provided the title and labels for the instrument, this page provides the detailed technical explanation of its mechanical construction, its use by a single observer, and its historical context during Tycho Brahe’s travels in Germany and his observations of the Great Comet of 1577.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**I** HAD also constructed long before another sextant made of solid **Steel**, and so arranged that a single observer, by means of it, might conveniently investigate the distances of the stars. This whole instrument can be taken apart—both the sextant itself and its support—by means of appropriate screws, and then put back together again, so that it can be packed in a chest and transported to other regions whenever necessary. This is exactly what I did in my youth, about 24 years ago, when I was traveling through Germany. Its construction is somewhat evident from the figure itself; however, we shall add a fuller explanation here, summarized from the second book of our *Progymnasmata* <note>original: "Progymnasmatum" — This refers to Tycho's work *Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata* (Exercises for the Restoration of Astronomy).</note>. There, toward the end, where the same instrument is both illustrated and explained, the sextant itself is represented by A B C D. Its center is at A, and its Arc B D is divided into sixty degrees, and each single minute of those degrees, by the method of transversal lines familiar to us <note>Transversal lines were a system of diagonal dots used to read fractions of a degree with high precision before the invention of the vernier scale.</note>. The arms of the instrument, A B and B C, are joined at A around a round bolt so that it can be opened or closed like a pair of compasses. This is done by the screw indicated by the letters G H; although this screw is straight, it is attached to the arms by movable sockets on both sides so that it serves the circular motion of the instrument nonetheless. It also has two handles marked with the letters I K, by which the instrument is held during the sighting. B and C are two sighting vanes <term>pinnacidia<gloss>small plates or "diopters" with slits or holes used to align the instrument with a star</gloss></term>, through which the eye, directed toward O near the center, observes two stars simultaneously. By means of the screw G H, the angle B A C is widened or narrowed until the Arc B D shows the desired distance—though the parallax of the instrument arising from this must be subtracted. However, I later found another method so that the sighting occurs in parallel, no less than in other diopters and vanes, so that the subtraction of parallax will not be necessary. But because this arrangement of sights is not represented here, we have decided to explain it elsewhere, where this very sextant is equipped with them.

Furthermore, it has a pedestal in which the sextant can be more conveniently directed toward any plane. Each arm of this sextant, A B and A C, is about three cubits <note>A cubit was roughly 18 inches, making the arms about 4.5 feet long.</note> long, which the chord of the Arc B D matches. This entire sextant is skillfully crafted from solid steel, except that the sighting vanes are made of **Brass** <note>original: "Orichalcica" — Orichalcum was a golden-colored alloy, usually brass.</note>; and it rests upon a certain support so that it is more suitable for use and easier to handle during observation. The support and pedestal are arranged in this manner: First, it stands firmly on the ground by a cross-shaped base V X Y Z. Next to this is a hollow leg at T, so that a long screw at Q may descend into it; this is raised or lowered as needed by another screw, called a "perpetual screw," enclosed within P, and with it the sextant itself. Both screws are made of solid iron, as is the entire upper part of the support. The polished iron piece A O is hollowed out inside around another long axis attached to the screw Q, where a small cap is visible. From this, a point emerges to which another long iron piece, shown by letters L M, is attached. This carries the sextant itself, fixed at I, which can be extended along its entire length. That cap is quadrilateral and is so fitted inside with a mechanism of rollers that when its screw is turned at N, that point tips the long iron L M, together with the whole sextant attached to it, sideways to the position of two stars. Also, by the Arc S R, the center placed at A is raised and lowered together with the sextant itself, as the altitude or declination of the star requires, by a special joint inserted into the polished iron O H at S. It is fixed as desired on the same arc by a screw added on the other side near S. But all these things are better understood by sight and by handling. 

With this instrument, which I had made many years ago, I investigated the distances between the stars. Its greatest use was in the observations of the **Comet of the year 1577**. Therefore, in the book which we wrote clearly concerning that comet, this sextant is described more fully toward the end (as I said). If anyone wishes, they may read more about it there from page 459 to page 461.

Its use is for taking the distances of stars within the sixth part of a circle <note>A sextant covers 60 degrees, which is 1/6th of a 360-degree circle.</note>. This is done by only one observer, who, placing his eye at the center A and having disposed the whole sextant in the plane of the stars by the pedestal screws, turns the screw G H in either direction. In this way, he widens or narrows the angle B A C until both stars are aligned exactly within the sighting vanes B and C. Then the division of the arc B D will show the distance that was sought. From the beginning, its circumference had not only the common division but also the **Nonnian** division <note>original: "Nonnianam" — A method of dividing the scale into 45 or 90 concentric arcs, invented by Pedro Nunes (Petrus Nonnius) to read fractions of a degree.</note>. But since I was taught by experience that another method using transversal points is much more convenient and accurate, I set those aside and used the transversals afterward. I also made the circumference of the sextant out of **Brass**, so that it would not be subject to rust as the steel was.

<summary>This page describes the technical mechanics and assembly of Tycho Brahe's portable steel sextant, emphasizing its single-observer operation and its role in observing the Comet of 1577. Brahe also explains his preference for transversal scale divisions over the Nonnian system and his decision to use brass for the scale to prevent rust.</summary>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...and I completed the scale upon it in my usual manner by means of ten dots placed transversally in each sixth part of a degree <note>This refers to the "transversal" method of graduation, where diagonal rows of dots allowed the observer to read measurements far more precisely than standard divisions</note>, and thus its use was made more convenient. For what purpose the distances of the stars in astronomical matters pertain; let it be sought elsewhere, both from our own writings and those of other authors. For their utility is most widely evident: and the observations of the aforementioned Comet <note>The Great Comet of 1577</note> reviewed in the second chapter in the above-named book <note>The *Progymnasmata*</note>, which were made with this very sextant, provide examples of such things, which in the following chapter are brought to their intended conclusion through the calculations of spherical triangles.

When **Paul Wittich**, a distinguished mathematician of Wrocław, saw this sextant at my home in the year 1580, he later revealed it at Kassel to the most Illustrious Prince **WILLIAM**, Landgrave of Hesse, etc., of most praised memory. He [the Landgrave] soon saw to it that such an instrument was constructed by his skillful <term>automatopoeus</term> <note>A maker of automatons or complex mechanical devices; here referring to the famous clockmaker Jost Bürgi</note>, with certain things altered, which Wittich either remembered well enough, or as it otherwise seemed best to them. For in place of the screw G they attached a chain, by which the angle of the instrument is widened or contracted. They retained the same form of division which Wittich had learned from me. Others later also imitated this structure; and some have even claimed the invention for themselves, as has happened with many other things of mine. Indeed, the validation of this instrument is excellent, because the distances measured with it at Kassel agree entirely, to the very minute and even its half, with those which we performed in Denmark with our own sextants: as is clear from the first volume
-> of our *Astronomical Letters*. <-

<meta>This page continues the technical description of astronomical instruments, moving from the influence of Paul Wittich and the Landgrave of Hesse to Tycho Brahe’s own early developments in Augsburg and his specialized instrument for measuring the "New Star" of 1572.</meta>

<meta>catchword: I return</meta>

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**P**reviously, I had already prepared a similar instrument while I was staying for some time in my youth at **Augsburg** <note>original: "Augustæ Vindelicorum," a major Roman-founded city in Bavaria.</note> in Upper Germany. That version was made only of wood and lacked a stand, though it was otherwise manageable due to its lightness. Upon leaving that place, I left it as a gift for the Consul of that most noble Republic, **Paul Hainzel**, of most excellent memory and a great lover of these studies. When I returned to my homeland and that new and beyond measure wonderful star <note>The Supernova of 1572 in the constellation Cassiopeia, which proved the heavens were not unchanging.</note> suddenly shone forth from the heavens, I took care to have another instrument of this kind made for me, in order to precisely denote its distances from the neighboring stars of **Cassiopeia**. You see the diagram of it here. Its *Arc*, in which the divisions are marked, is understood by *B C*. It is at least a twelfth part of a circle, or 30 degrees, because it sufficed for the smaller distances such as those we have mentioned; thus, it would not be heavy or cumbersome due to its length. Nevertheless, other *Arcs* can also be applied to the same device, whether larger or shorter, as one pleases. The *Arms* <term>Regula</term> <note>The "regulae" are the straight, pivoting arms of the instrument.</note> *A B* and *A C* are joined at the Center *A*, and are there mutually rotatable, containing the circumference itself at *B* and *C*. They have a length of 4 cubits, a width of three digits, and a thickness of two. Indeed, they are four-sided, consisting of very dry walnut wood; this wood is less liable than others to change due to the quality of the air. Where the *Arms* are coupled around the Center *A*, they are secured with a metallic bolt, and the joint and the entire structure are reinforced with small brass plates. The *Arc* itself is solid metal; however, it is not too thick or wide, lest it cause harm by its weight—this prevents the warping to which wooden material is subject. The *Sights* <term>Pinnacidia</term> at *B C* are also made of brass <note>original: "Orichalcica," a golden-colored alloy like brass or bronze.</note>, and are so adapted that their inner side corresponds precisely to the inner surface of the *Arms*. On the other lower side, there is a screw by which the movable sight is fixed after an observation is completed, until the reading has been made on the scale. Furthermore, another oblong screw stands out near *D E*, by whose benefit the angle of the instrument *B A C* is contracted and expanded, as the distance to be measured requires. And although this screw moves in a straight line, it can nevertheless suffice for the circular motion that occurs around *D E* above the center *A*, since on the other side, where it has its housings, it moves on small round pins; just as we have indicated in the preceding section. Moreover, inside this screw toward the Center and even closer to the eye, there is a certain circumference *G L H*, fixed to the first *Arm* at *G* and passing through the other at *L*, whose duty it is to hold both *Arms* in their plane, lest they be somewhat pulled apart due to their length. The angle of the instrument can also be secured on the same *Arc* from the rear side. This is located about one-third of the length of the *Arms* from the center, so that with one hand conveniently applied there, the movement of the *Arms* may be guided and stabilized. It also has two handles on the other side, which could not be represented here. I had not yet constructed a pedestal for it then; but since the instrument was not too heavy, it could be turned and held in any plane of the stars without much difficulty upon some transverse support firmly placed beneath it, until the observation was finished.

**ITS USE** is just as in the previous one: with the eye applied at *I*, by turning the handle *F* and the screw *D E* together, the Angle of the instrument *B A C* is contracted or dilated until both stars, whose distances we seek, are seen precisely and centrally within the sights *B* and *C*. For then the *Arc* intercepted by the sights and the *Arms* renders the observation successful. But since the center of sight <note>The observer's pupil.</note> cannot be placed at the center of the instrument <note>Point A, the vertex of the angle.</note> relative to which the divisions proceed—because the solid part from *A* to *I* prevents it—it is therefore necessary that the distances measured with the eye placed eccentrically and numbered on the *Arc* are slightly larger than those that truly belong to the sky itself.

Therefore, to remedy this inconvenience, I had constructed a certain Table and marked it on the other side of the instrument so it would always be ready. This table provided the *Instrumental Parallaxes* <note>In this context, "parallax" refers to the geometric error caused by the observer's eye being at a different point than the instrument's mathematical center.</note>—namely, how much must be subtracted for each degree of observation made, so that the true distance, consistent with the heavens, might result, and precisely the same as would come from the Center of the instrument if the center of sight were united with it. The method by which this Table was prepared, following the logic of plane triangles, is demonstrated by us in the first volume of our *Introduction to the Restoration of Astronomy* <note>original: "Progymnasmatis Instaurationis Astronomicæ"</note> on page 342, in its fourth chapter; which elsewhere, from page 335 to 343, provides a brilliant description of this instrument. To which, for the reader desiring to know more about this—

He explains the "instrumental parallax" error caused by the eye's position and mentions a correction table he calculated to ensure accuracy.</summary>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

I refer [the reader] back [to that work]. There, one will also find a little later the distances of the **New Star** <note>The famous Supernova of 1572, which appeared in the constellation Cassiopeia and challenged the Aristotelian idea that the heavens were unchanging.</note> from the neighboring stars of **Cassiopeia** and certain others, as well as the distances of the fixed stars among themselves, measured most exactly with that same instrument and put to use. At that time, I had no other device suited for this task at hand; for I had recently returned home from my travels in Germany and was then occupied with **pyronomic** <term>Pyronomicis</term> <note>original: "Pyronomicis"; this refers to "pyronomy," the art of managing fire, which in Brahe's time meant chemical or alchemical laboratory work.</note> exercises rather than astronomical ones.

Although this instrument is not as excellent as those which I devised in subsequent years and had constructed with great effort and expense—and was therefore later considered obsolete by me—nevertheless, such as it is, since it is not inconvenient to use, I have decided to present it here to be illustrated and explained along with the rest. I do this so that I might also take account of those who do not have the resources to build those more elaborate and costly instruments.

Content with this, they shall investigate the intervals between stars more correctly and precisely than has been done previously using the **Astronomical Staff** <term>Radius Astronomicus</term> <note>Also known as a Cross-staff, a common navigating and surveying tool that Brahe sought to improve upon for higher precision.</note>, provided they properly and promptly guard against the **Parallax** <term>Parallaxin</term> <note>original: "Parallaxin"; here Brahe refers specifically to the "parallax of the eye," an error caused when the observer's eye is not placed exactly at the vertex of the measuring instrument.</note> of which I have spoken.

# THE ARRANGEMENT OF THE SAME INSTRUMENT
## SO THAT IT MAY SERVE
### FOR TAKING ALTITUDES.

<note>In an astronomical context, taking "altitudes" <term>altitudines</term> refers to measuring the angular height of a star, planet, or the sun above the horizon. This specific "arrangement" <term>dispositio</term> shows the instrument mounted vertically—as seen in the detailed woodcut—to allow the observer to determine how high a body sits in the sky, which is essential for calculating geographic latitude and mapping the stars.</note>

<summary>This page serves as a title and introduction to the vertical configuration of Tycho Brahe's instrument, designed specifically for measuring celestial altitudes. It features a technical woodcut of the device mounted on a sturdy, decorative column.</summary>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous section where the general vertical mounting of the instrument was introduced. Here, Tycho Brahe provides a detailed technical explanation of how to use this specific configuration—a sextant mounted vertically—to measure the altitude of stars, specifically referencing his observations of the "New Star" of 1572.</meta>

<sig>F 4</sig>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

<note>Decorative woodcut initial 'I' depicting an astronomer in robes using a quadrant to observe the stars.</note>
This very instrument, especially if it comprises a <term>sextant</term> <note>A sextant represents a sixth of a circle, or 60 degrees.</note> of a circle, is not unsuitable for investigating the Altitudes of the stars as well, provided it is arranged as this accompanying illustration shows. By this same method, at that time when that new star was seen in the year [15]72 and the following year, I examined its minimum altitude when it was on the Meridian below the Pole. <note>Tycho is referring to the famous supernova of 1572. Because it was "circumpolar" from his location, it never set, allowing him to measure it at its lowest point in the sky.</note> For its maximum altitude, when it approached the <term>zenith</term> <note>The point in the sky directly above the observer.</note>, it was not possible to measure it with this same device, since in our horizon that wonderful star, when it was above the Pole on the Meridian, was not far distant from the Zenith; therefore, its altitude could not be contained within the sixth part of a circle [60 degrees]. One may see these and similar things in the aforementioned book and the indicated chapter.

The method, however, by which I established the observation was also revealed there in this manner: This sextant, with respect to the lower Ruler A B, was arranged in a certain high window above the wall R, so that it responded precisely to the celestial Meridian which would be adapted for the observation. And there was a certain support indicated by the letters V X placed under it; this was from the rear part toward the Center near T, of such height that the Ruler A B itself exactly matched the equilibrium of the Horizon. And I tested it in this way: I directed the Arc of the instrument K N with its rulers toward the plane of the meridian, and at the end where the 60 degrees finish, near N, I hung a plumb line marked N S, and I raised or lowered the fundamental ruler A B by certain wedges placed between T and V X, as was needed; and thus I adjusted the whole instrument until the brass wire N O S, to which the lead weight is attached near S, precisely touched the midpoint between the Center of the sextant at A and the periphery of the division at O.

For it is clear from this that the ruler or base of the instrument A B was exactly parallel to the Horizon. Since, from the *Elements of Geometry*, a sextant of a circle constitutes an isosceles triangle (as demonstrated in the 15th Proposition of the fourth book of Euclid) through the points R N A and the line of the ruler R A. <note>Euclid’s *Elements* was the foundational textbook for geometry; Tycho uses it here to prove the mathematical precision of his leveling method.</note> And since the line descending straight from N, which looks toward the Zenith, bisects the base R A, it necessarily follows that this happens at right angles, as appears from Proposition 12 of Book 1 and those immediately preceding it. Wherefore, the plumb line N S is always directed toward the Zenith, and N O is joined to it; it is necessary that the Ruler R A be entirely perpendicular to that same plumb line: and therefore also in the equilibrium of the Horizon, from which the vertex [Zenith] is distant on all sides by a quadrant of a circle, which makes a right angle.

Therefore, with all things prepared and arranged with as much accuracy as possible, until the new star reached the Meridian in its lower position by the motion of the universe, I paid attention, and then I aimed through another <term>sight</term> <note>original: <term>pinnacidium</term>; these were small brass plates with slits or holes used to align the eye with a star.</note> analogous to the first one near C of the Ruler, placed not far from the Center near R, which could be removed if necessity required. In the front part of it, a certain small slit had been made, along with the upper side of the first sight, parallel with respect to the lower part and the Center, through which, during the observation, by moving the long <term>screw</term> <note>original: <term>cochlea</term></note> O E, the angle of the instrument was varied until I aimed precisely at the new star in the upper edge of the sight at C, with half of it seen and the other half hidden, as far as could be discerned with any sharpness. For at that time I had not yet discovered the slits parallel to each other on the first sight, by whose benefit the center of a star is noted most exactly. In this manner, therefore, through the arc B C intercepted by each sight, I detected the minimum altitude of the star above the Horizon, such as I have recorded in the cited place of the aforementioned book.

Since, however, the Ruler B A and thus the whole sextant could easily be moved from the equilibrium of the Horizon during the observation, if the supports on which the instrument rested yielded somewhat in one direction or another through the driven wedges; therefore, near the small line at O located in the middle of the lower Ruler, which the plumb line of the instrument ought to strike by its contact when behaving correctly, certain divisions were adapted on both sides in a small arc P Q described around the center N, so that for the quantity of one Degree in such a diameter of a circle, minutes were distributed on each side, in the front O P to be subtracted, and in the rear O Q to be added, as the plumb line showed some inclination one way or the other from equilibrium. And in that way, after the observation was finished and the plumb line inspected again, I proved the Altitude found through the Arc B C and (wherever it seemed necessary) limited it more accurately. Though it is true the business would be done more exquisitely if the sighting were made through two parallel slits moved to the eye near the front sight; nevertheless, by acting in this way, as we have now said, that which was sought came forth precisely enough; especially where the experiment can be made quite often on the same star; as in that new one, which, for as long as it lasted, perpetually retained the same insensibly changed altitude on the Meridian. Truly, those things which have been said here as an example equally apply to measuring the altitudes of other stars, provided they do not exceed the sixth part of the circle—

<summary>Tycho Brahe explains how he used a vertically mounted sextant to measure the altitude of the famous 1572 supernova. He details the geometric principles, based on Euclid, used to ensure the instrument was perfectly level using plumb lines and adjustable wedges.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, sextant, altitude, 1572 supernova, Euclid, geometry, astronomical instruments, meridian, zenith, plumb line</keywords>

### EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

...[observa]tions are to be understood: and this not only when they are on the <term>meridian</term> <note>The imaginary great circle in the sky passing through the north and south poles and the observer's zenith.</note>, but also in any other vertical circle; namely, with the plane of the instrument directed into the plane of that same circle; and with all other things arranged just as was indicated before.

The use of this instrument in determining Altitudes is also clear from what was said previously: although (to speak the truth of the matter) it is possible to perform this same task with less effort and even greater precision using those <term>Quadrants</term> <note>An astronomical instrument in the shape of a quarter-circle (90 degrees), used for measuring altitudes.</note>, especially the larger ones, which I have already mentioned before. But since one might not have such instruments at hand, as they are quite expensive, it will be permitted to use this one, about which we have just treated, or something similar; just as we previously advised regarding the taking of distances with the benefit of this same device. The diligent observer, however, must beware of those instruments and their arrangement which easily wobble, or are in any way liable to contract a defect. For the matter concerns the smallest details, from which the greatest conclusions are drawn; lest we "lose both labor and oil," <note>original: "operam & oleum perdere"; a Latin proverb meaning to waste one's time and effort on a fruitless task, referring to the oil burned in a lamp while working late.</note> as the saying goes. This has happened to many of the more recent astronomers. Would that it were not permitted to suspect the same of those most ancient ones, regarding precise subtlety. Surely, a more exquisite method would be given for establishing an Astronomy that corresponds perfectly <note>original: "ad amussim"; literally "according to the carpenter's rule."</note> to all ages. In this task, however, so far as the observations and opinions of our ancestors allow, the supreme <term>Divine Will</term> <note>original: "Numine"; referring to God's providence or divine guidance.</note> being willing,

->we both have worked and will work, so that<-
->all things, purged of errors, may<-
->be restored to their<-
->entirety.<-

# THE <term>GREAT QUADRANT</term> <note>A large-scale astronomical instrument shaped like a quarter-circle (90 degrees). It was used to measure the height of stars and planets above the horizon with extreme precision.</note>
## OF THE KIND WE ONCE CONSTRUCTED NEAR
### AUGSBURG <note>original: "Augusta Vindelicorum"; the ancient Roman name for the city of Augsburg in Germany, where Tycho Brahe lived and worked from 1569 to 1570.</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of the Great Quadrant constructed in Augsburg. Having introduced the instrument on the previous page, Tycho Brahe now details its physical dimensions, the materials used in its construction (specifically oak and brass), and the mechanical systems—including a subterranean rotating base—that allowed for its operation.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

Regarding that massive **Quadrant**, which we had constructed 26 years ago in the garden of Lord **Paul Hainzel** at his estate half a mile distant from **Augsburg** <note>original: "Augusta Vindelicorum"</note>, I am pleased to include here both its **Illustration** and an explanation. Even though it no longer exists—having fallen into ruin five years after its construction—I nevertheless believe it will be worth the effort to provide these instructions, so that if anyone wishes to have a similar one built, they may have a model to imitate. This Quadrant is represented here by the points **A C P**, along with its interwoven framing. It was entirely crafted from oak wood that had been seasoned for many years, and the sides and framing members were as large as great beams. It had a radius—from the center to the circumference—of at least 14 <term>cubits</term> <note>A unit of length based on the forearm; 14 cubits here equals approximately 19 to 20 feet (6 meters).</note>. Upon the <term>limb</term> <note>The graduated edge or curved border of an astronomical instrument.</note>, or circumference, where the divisions were located, it was covered with plates of <term>Orichalcum</term> <note>A golden-colored brass or bronze alloy used for scientific instruments.</note>. The interwoven framing, composed of transverse beams fitted together and smoothed, was arranged for the purpose of holding the whole Quadrant more firmly together and maintaining its proper dimensions and flat plane.

The division of the circumference itself was only according to the common form. For at that time, I had not yet devised that other, more convenient method which I later used on other instruments. Nevertheless, each individual degree could be distributed not only into individual minutes, but even every minute into six small parts; so that it could provide even ten-second intervals distinctly. The <term>sights</term> <note>original: "Pinnacidia"; plates used to align the instrument with a star.</note> were on the other side of the Quadrant, **D E**, where the observer would stand near **D**. The sight closest to the eye had a small hole in the middle, through which the stars were sighted at the upper end near the diametrical line at **E**. If, however, the Sun was to be observed, another perforated sight was used in the forward position near **E**, which admitted the light of the Sun through a round hole onto a circle of corresponding size on the inner part of the lower sight. For this, a certain long channel was especially used, so that the light of the Sun would be less dissipated in the air and could be discerned more exactly within the limits of that same circle. Furthermore, near its center **A**, a thin brass wire was hung, which could support a lead weight of several pounds at the hole **H**, so that the wire would not be easily moved by the wind. For this wire showed the sought-after altitude upon the scale of divisions as exactly as possible.

This quadrant machine was attached to a thick and sturdy oak column at its center near **A** by a strong, rounded iron pin, secured at the back near **G**. Two braces also supported the limb itself, fitted a little above the hole where the lead plumb-bob hangs. These were also used for the purpose of binding and holding the whole Quadrant tightly to the column when it was raised or lowered as the observation required, lest it fall back by its own weight before the measurement of the observation was completed. The lower part of the column itself was secured and rotated within a certain subterranean structure, **X Y S P K**, so that its lowest part, being pointed and made of steel near **K**, could be turned very easily within a socket of the same shape placed beneath it. A round, cone-shaped hole at the top held it on all sides, so that it would not wobble back and forth, but rather the column itself and the Quadrant attached to it could be rotated into all <term>vertical planes</term> <note>The various directions around the horizon (azimuths) that allow the instrument to face any part of the sky.</note>. The rotation of which I speak was performed by the handles **Q O I N**, and the Quadrant itself stood unmoved by its own weight in whatever plane was necessary. All of this, seen below the handles, was hidden <note>original: "Crypticum"</note> and firmly joined together, supported by stones both below and on all sides, so that it might more firmly support the whole machine standing above it and prevent it from being pulled apart. For the machine itself stood <term>under the open sky</term> <note>original: "sub dio"</note>, and was therefore exposed to the winds and the harms of the weather. It was nonetheless covered, especially around its circumference, so that the limb of divisions would not suffer any damage.

Its **USE** was solely for measuring the altitudes of the Sun and the rest of the planets as accurately as possible, so that they could be noted within a sixth part of a minute <note>10 seconds of arc.</note>, provided the <term>observer</term> <note>original: "collimator"; the person aligning the sights.</note> applied the required diligence. Our predecessors hardly ever reached such precision, although we later achieved the same with even smaller instruments made with a different labor-saving design. When an altitude was to be taken, the observer placed his eye near the sight at **D** and searched for the star in the other forward sight, raising or lowering the whole Quadrant until this could be achieved. If, however, the altitudes were lower <note>Meaning the star was higher in the sky, requiring the eye-end of the quadrant to be lowered toward the ground.</note>, ladders were needed for the observer to climb; then, having left the machine unmoved, the **Altitude**...
