## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

The sought-after altitude was shown on the circumference according to the plumb line. And let these remarks suffice regarding the **construction** and **use** of the Instrument.

If anyone requires fuller details regarding the circumstances, he will find them in the first volume of our *Exercises* <note>original: "Progymnasmatum"; referring to Tycho Brahe's major work, *Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata*.</note> from page 353 up to 359. For the altitudes of the **new star** <note>This refers to the famous supernova of 1572 in the constellation Cassiopeia, which Tycho studied extensively.</note>, as well as those of certain other stars recorded for my sake by the aforementioned Lord **Paul Hainzel**, the Mayor <note>original: "Consule"</note>, using the same machine, and later communicated to me, are related on the subsequent pages in that same work. From these, their precision can be clear to a knowledgeable person; they even agreed perfectly with those measurements that I later took in Denmark with other <term>instruments</term> <note>original: "Organis", referring to astronomical tools.</note>. And it is to be wished that this exceptional machine had been preserved there longer and put to use, or at least that another had been constructed in its place. But since men for the most part attend rather to earthly things than to heavenly ones, they are accustomed to
->neglect such things, perhaps at a greater loss than they themselves<-
->easily notice.<-

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

The sought-after altitude was shown on the circumference according to the plumb line. And let these remarks suffice regarding the **construction** and **use** of the Instrument.

If anyone requires fuller details regarding the circumstances, he will find them in the first volume of our *Exercises* <note>original: "Progymnasmatum"; referring to Tycho Brahe's major work, *Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata*, which detailed his new system of the world.</note> from page 353 up to 359. From these, their precision can be clear to a knowledgeable person; they even agreed perfectly with those measurements that I later took in Denmark with other <term>instruments</term> <note>original: "Organis", a term commonly used in the Renaissance to describe complex scientific or musical tools as "organs" of discovery.</note>. And it is to be wished that this exceptional machine had been preserved there longer and put to use, or at least that another had been constructed in its place. ->neglect such things, perhaps at a greater loss than they themselves<-

## EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

We indeed explained this Quadrant previously among the illustrated instruments at number 7, but arranged in a different manner; so that it might revolve within a wall-recess on a strong steel axis looking toward the <term>Zenith</term> <note>The point directly above an observer.</note> and <term>Nadir</term> <note>The point directly below an observer.</note>, and at the same time might have a fixed <term>Azimuthal</term> <note>A horizontal circle used to measure the bearing of a celestial body.</note> Ring within the circumference of the wall-recess, just as it was sufficiently depicted and explained there with the other requirements pertaining to it. But since I promised in that same place that I would show its other arrangement, which I had used from the beginning, here you have it: it will be explained briefly as follows: **A B C D** is the Quadrant itself, described from the Center **A**, and enclosed by the Square **A E F G**. Its <term>Ruler</term> <note>original: "Regula"; the sighting arm or alidade of the instrument.</note> **A C I** is extended lower down to the handle **H**, near which is the <term>sights</term> <note>original: "pinnacidium"; a small plate with slits for viewing.</note>, having slits parallel to the upper ones at **C**. It is lengthened from **C** to **I** at the upper part so that it can touch the Square everywhere: to which some <term>brass</term> <note>original: "Orichalcica"; referring to orichalcum, a golden-hued alloy often used for scientific tools.</note> threads were added near **I** in place of a support, so that the index might remain everywhere in a straight line. What is seen from the back part, **A L M N O**, indicates the iron support, sustaining the whole Quadrant orthogonally on the underlying horizontal plane. For near **A**, it is joined to the Azimuthal Horizon like a fastener having a "winged" screw <note>original: "cochleolam plumatam"; likely a thumb-screw or a leveling screw with "wings" for easy turning.</note> beneath it, so that it can be led around in the Azimuthal circle itself wherever one pleases by means of the handle which is on the back part. Indeed, the Azimuthal Circle of which I speak is represented by the letters **P Q R S**. By **X**, however, are indicated five strong columns carrying the whole machine, at whose capitals near **Y Z** are certain screws, called "endless" <note>original: "perennes"; these are worm gears used for fine adjustment.</note>, also with their own revolving handles, arranging the Azimuthal Circle in the equilibrium of the Horizon, and directing the whole Quadrant into the vertical plane, just as the <term>plumb lines</term> <note>original: "perpendicula"; weights on strings used to ensure the instrument is perfectly vertical.</note> marked at **V** and **W** require. The rest is clear from the description made in the place cited above, where this same Quadrant was arranged differently and somewhat more conveniently. Therefore, I did not care to repeat more regarding this explanation, lest I should tiresomely "re-cook" <note>original: "recoquerem"; a Latin idiom for repeating something until it becomes stale.</note> the same things that were said before. However, so that the space below might nevertheless be filled, as before, a poem in <term>hexameter</term> <note>The classic rhythm of epic poetry.</note> has been added, which the noble and learned youth **Franz Gansneb Tengnagel** <note>Tengnagel was Brahe’s assistant and future son-in-law.</note>—who was a member of my household for some time and a companion of my migration from Denmark—composed on the spot to be added here.

-> Let humble Arts and sluggish cares give way; <-
-> Give way; for divine **Urania** <note>The Muse of Astronomy.</note> has raised her head <-
-> So far above all other Muses, as much as <-
-> Heavenly things surpass the Earth, and as much as <-
-> The nourishing light conquers the darkness. And **God** himself <-
-> Gave man a face so sublime, that he might look upon the stars, <-
-> That he might lift his eyes upward and look toward the ether, <-
-> And bring the wandering stars under his rule. <-
-> Happy are they, therefore, for whom these divine things are a care, <-
-> While others seek wealth, and pursue honors, <-
-> And seek delights and luxury; and handling <-
-> Whatever trifles, they pass their lives stupidly like sluggish cattle, <-
-> Worthless men. Ah, whither does blind ignorance <-
-> Drive these headlong, whose hearts do not care for lofty things? <-
-> You, whose noble mind burns with the fire of heavenly seed, <-
-> Ascend with alacrity into the ethereal recesses. <-
-> Come here, **Timochares**, **Ptolemy**, and **Copernicus**, <-
-> Although you trusted too much in deceptive instruments. <-
-> Is it permitted for small things to immediately climb a high tower <-
-> By steps, or to grasp the Heavens with a pole? <-

<meta>This page transitions from the technical description of astronomical instruments to a laudatory poem written by Franz Gansneb Tengnagel, Tycho Brahe’s son-in-law and assistant. It praises Tycho’s move from Denmark to the broader Holy Roman Empire (Germany) to continue his work.</meta>

*How might the inner secrets of the star-bearing world lie open skillfully?*
*If only that care had not frustrated you in times past, it would be granted*
*now to better reckon the stars with your numbers.*
*But you, indeed, whose mind is neighbor to the Gods, come near,*
**TYCHO**, *the most celebrated glory of the Danish people:*
*It pleased the Powers Above that you should see such obstacles*
*yielding to human strength: bring aid to this labor*
*greater than that of Hercules, which Atlas did not well endure.* <note>In mythology, Atlas held up the heavens; the poet suggests Tycho’s intellectual work is even more taxing.</note>
*Readily grant the instruments, which you knew how to build*
*as the greatest and best, and which this book of yours shares with the world:*
*Through thrice-seven years, in which you have diligently*
*surveyed the star-shining bodies, both fixed and wandering, in the whole sky.*
*Each of these, though once trackless, you have brought into a rule*
*through an easy circuit, to which the whole Ether* <note>The upper atmosphere or the substance filling the heavens.</note> *obeys.*
*O celebrated Danes! O thrice and four times blessed,* <note>original: "terq; quaterq; beati"; a classical expression of extreme good fortune.</note>
*to whom the stars have granted the birth of such a great man?*
*But a greater glory now rises for you from this (who would have thought it possible?)*
*that you did not grudge Germany* <note>original: "Teuthonia"; referring to Tycho's move to the Holy Roman Empire and Prague after leaving Denmark.</note> *your foster-son, whom you were permitted to keep.*
*Perhaps that highest God of Gods willed this,*
*so that the glory of Urania* <note>The Muse of Astronomy.</note> *might penetrate into all lands,*
*O* FREDERICK, *father of the Country,* <note>King Frederick II of Denmark (1534–1588), Tycho’s long-time patron.</note> *whose memorable*
*name flourishes in the world, it is not your lowest praise that you,*
**TYCHO** *receiving, voluntarily fostered his outstanding labor,*
*which will touch great minds and hearts,*
*and will make its celebrated devotees equal to Olympus.* <note>The home of the gods, often used to signify eternal fame or the heavens themselves.</note>
*But that you,* **TYCHO**, *have now left your ancestral shores,*
*bear it with a high spirit, just as you do: to a great-souled man,*
*the earth is everywhere his native soil. Hail, inhabitant of the world;*
*enough has been given to the North and the Danes. Well done, renowned* **TYCHO**,
*proceed the more to spread your praises through the world,*
*proceed to weave your eternal work for those who desire it, proceed*
*to produce the tokens of your genius with extraordinary fruit.*
*And so future ages will speak your name more fruitfully,*
*as long as golden-haired Cynthius* <note>A poetic name for Apollo, the god of the Sun.</note> *shall count the years,*
*while the wandering Moon shall change her horns through the months,*
*and while all the stars shall shine in the whole sky.*

<meta>The previous page concluded with a celebratory poem by Tycho’s son-in-law, praising Tycho’s move to Germany and his immense scientific labor. This page returns to Tycho's own voice as he provides technical descriptions of instruments that were functional but had not yet been illustrated with woodcuts for this publication.</meta>

### Concerning
# A BRIEF INDICATION OF CERTAIN OTHER INSTRUMENTS OF OURS, WHICH HAVE NOT YET BEEN ENGRAVED.

Hitherto we have shown and explained our **astronomical instruments**, as many as had been drawn and engraved <note>original: "exsculpta"; referring to the woodcut illustrations used throughout the book</note>, as far as was permitted for the present. In truth, I have certain others still at hand, which, because they have not yet been depicted and cut into wood, it was necessary to omit here. I will, however, take care that as soon as it can be done, these also may be joined to the previous ones, and this entire **mechanical treatise** may be more fully increased, with certain aids and tables conducive to this purpose being added. Meanwhile, I will nonetheless briefly touch upon some of the remaining items here.

## THE BIFURCATED SEXTANT.

We have also constructed a certain **sextant**, which, because it has arms <note>original: "regulas"; literally "rulers"</note> from the center on either side that can be narrowed or widened, crossing each other in the shape of an X, is for that reason called **bifurcated** (two-forked). It has a steel arc, comprising a **sextant** <note>One sixth of a circle, or 60 degrees.</note> of a circle. The length of the arms, moreover, is 4 <term>cubits</term> <note>A cubit was a traditional unit of length; Tycho's "astronomical cubit" was roughly 1.5 to 2 feet (approx. 40-60 cm).</note>. It also has **brass sights** <note>original: "pinnacidia orichalcica"</note> arranged in various ways away from the center near the extremities of the arms on both sides. The arms themselves are made of **Brazilwood** <note>A dense, heavy wood from South America, highly valued by instrument makers for its resistance to warping.</note>, because it is compact and durable. It has divisions made upon the arc in our usual manner. One may track the distances of the stars with this **sextant** quite precisely through two observers, each of whom looks through the sights of his own arm—except that this is performed with somewhat more effort than in the other sextants, and not quite so quickly. It is, however, easily portable, and occasionally convenient for various uses.

## A LARGE SEMICIRCLE FOR MARKING GREATER DISTANCES IN THE HEAVENS,

Since by the sextants devised and prepared by us, only those distances of the stars can be observed which are contained within a sixth part of the sky, and it sometimes happens in practice that distances greater than these must be tracked in the heavens—even so much so that sometimes distances larger than a fourth part of the sky <note>i.e., greater than 90 degrees</note> must be marked, as the convenience and practice of observation demands (which happens most frequently in the larger distances of the **Sun** and **Moon**, when they are seen together during the day around either quadrature or beyond; and also deserves a place for other reasons)—Therefore, we have caused a certain **semicircular instrument** to be made, reinforced on all sides with cross-beams, and firmly covered with **brass plates** both on the circumference and on the diametrical ruler. By its benefit, the intervals between stars, however great they may eventually be, may be exactly measured with it right up to the completion of a hemisphere <note>180 degrees</note>. This **semicircle** has 6 **cubits** in its diameter; and near the middle of the whole structure, where it balances itself as if on all sides, there is a square hole, in which

<summary>Tycho describes additional astronomical instruments not yet illustrated in the book, including a "bifurcated" sextant made of Brazilwood and a large brass-plated semicircle designed for measuring celestial distances up to 180 degrees.</summary>
<keywords>bifurcated sextant, large semicircle, Brazilwood, brass instruments, celestial distances, astronomical measurements, instrument construction, Tycho Brahe</keywords>

<meta>The previous page ended mid-sentence describing the construction of the bifurcated sextant. It concluded with the mention of the arms being made of Brazilwood. This page continues with the description of its support and then details Tycho's portable instruments: the Astronomical Staff and the Astronomical Ring.</meta>

...placed upon a strong support, particularly a **globular** one, during observations. It is directed into the plane of the stars by two observers, and its use is established in no different a way than with the other **sextants**. Therefore, there will be no need to add more about this; rather, let those who desire further details consult the explanations of the **sextant** used for taking distances.

## THE ASTRONOMICAL STAFF (JACOB’S STAFF).

We do not entirely reject the use of the **Astronomical Staff** <term>Radius Astronomicus</term> <note>Commonly known as a Jacob’s Staff, this was a handheld tool used to measure the angle between two celestial bodies or the height of a star above the horizon.</note>, especially while traveling, since it is easily portable and is contained within a very small case—even if it does not provide the most exact and completely certain distances of the stars. I have such a tool at hand, not constructed by myself, but prepared by **Gualterus Arsenius**, the nephew of that most excellent mathematician **Gemma Frisius** (who lived formerly at Louvain in Belgium and published a booklet on this subject). It consists entirely of finely fashioned **brass plates**, although its interior is wood; it has sights <term>pinnacidia</term> and divisions exactly as described by the same Gemma in his aforementioned book. The longer ruler of this instrument, or the **Staff** itself, is a little more than three <term>cubits</term> <note>Tycho's cubit was roughly 1.5 to 2 feet (45–60 cm).</note> in length. In its four-sided thickness, it is everywhere about the width of a large thumb. The cross-piece is about half the length and width of the Staff itself. Furthermore, I have also seen to it that my own craftsmen made another Staff, entirely of **brass**, but hollow inside and not stuffed with any wood. For wood has this nature: unless it is assisted in a peculiar way, it forces the brass plates with which it is covered to bend wherever it is pulled by its own instability and changes in the air <note>Tycho is noting that wood warps with humidity, which ruins the precision of the metal scales attached to it.</note>. That Staff which we constructed later is not four-sided, but **three-sided**, because it is lighter that way, and these three surfaces were sufficient for marking all the divisions upon it. Moreover, I fashioned equal divisions upon it using the **transversal points** <note>original: "puncta transversalia"; a diagonal grid system used to achieve much higher precision than simple tick marks.</note> we commonly use, so that it might match a **Table of Sines** <term>Canon Sinuum</term> <note>A mathematical table used for calculating angles in trigonometry.</note> of five digits, and might execute everything more subtly than the aforementioned Staff of Gemma; it also features an unequal division, arranged differently, since that one was faulty in his version. Its length, width, and entire proportion are nearly the same as the previous one, and it has similar sights. But to speak plainly <note>original: "Verùm ut dicam id quod res est"</note>, the Staff, however it is prepared, does not supply the most exact distances of the stars (as we also suggested before), not even those narrower ones within 15 degrees, let alone the wider ones, where it fails even more. It will not be difficult to explain the reasons for this elsewhere; here we aim for brevity. Nonetheless, I have personally devised a certain means by which the difficulties and errors <note>original: "hallucinationibus"; literally "hallucinations" or delusions, meaning systematic errors in reading.</note> of the Staff may be totally remedied, also employing sights with slits and a **cylinder** around which two observers may investigate distances, no less than with the sextants. We shall speak more of this to Your Lordship <note>original: "D. V." (Dominationi Vestrae)</note> at another time, when it has been completed (for it is currently under construction).

## THE ASTRONOMICAL RING.

In addition, we have at the ready an **Astronomical Ring**, an instrument used by many others, both ancient and modern, on account of its handiness; it was made some years ago by the same nephew of Gemma, in imitation of the description that the same Gemma set forth in a brief booklet. This Ring is entirely **brass** and finely fashioned. It has a diameter of about one cubit. To this, we have added another invention on the inside: namely, long and smooth **cylinders** crossing each other crosswise and at right angles, touching the surface of the inner ring, and fitted there with a square support. Furthermore, we have provided it with sights and certain other means so that it not only performs much more conveniently what Gemma and others have taught regarding the use of this Ring, but it also provides certain other things. For in this way, the **Declination** of the stars <note>The angular distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator.</note> can be captured more aptly than before; and even their **ascensional differences**, and thus their **Right Ascensions** <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude, measured along the equator.</note> themselves, can be marked from the heavens—to say nothing of the longitudes and latitudes of the stars, which can also be obtained by employing a certain peculiar method. We shall deal with these matters more fully, God willing <note>original: "volente Numine"</note>, when the instrument has been fully formed and engraved.

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the limitations of measuring celestial distances with a Jacob's Staff and introduced the Astronomical Ring. It concluded by suggesting that while portable, small instruments often lack the precision required for true astronomical advancement.</meta>

...convenience <note>original: "moditate", concluding the word "commoditate" from the previous page</note>. But let this be whatever it may be, it is not possible to scrutinize celestial appearances with sufficient precision using such small and playful <term>Instruments</term>, because their smallness does not allow the degrees to be subdivided into scrupulous minute-parts. And if they were to be made of such a great size as to provide this, they would become unmanageable due to their bulk and mass; moreover, they would weigh themselves down so much that they would not be suitable for use without some error. We also possess another certain minor ring made of **brass** <term>Orichalcum</term> <note>A gold-colored brass alloy common in Renaissance scientific instruments.</note>, containing in its diameter at least the length of a short span. But I approve of this one even less.

The primary use of such **Rings** is so that the hours of the night and day may be discerned through them, although they do not perform this with the ease and precision that they promise. They are, however, suitable for this in some way when other and more certain means are lacking, especially since they are conveniently portable due to their handle. As for the other uses which **Gemma** <note>Gemma Frisius (1508–1555), a famous Dutch physician and mathematician who designed several astronomical instruments.</note> and others have gathered, which are of secondary importance and not particularly certain for those who desire to know, let them seek those in their own booklets.

# PORTABLE ARMILLARY SPHERE.

->**Decorative Initial Q**<-

FURTHERMORE, we have produced a certain **Armilla** <term>Armillary Sphere</term> <note>A model of objects in the sky, consisting of a spherical framework of rings, centered on Earth or the Sun, used to show the lines of celestial longitude and latitude.</note> on its own, stabilized internally by suitable supports, which can be easily moved and set up under the open sky on stone pedestals with its own adapted screws. Thus, it can be appropriately rotated toward any part of the sky with very little effort. By the benefit of this instrument, only the **Declinations** <note>The angular distance of a point north or south of the celestial equator.</note> of the stars are captured, although not as subtly as with those larger ones mentioned in the preceding pages. Nevertheless, since this **Armilla** has a diameter of almost three **cubits** <note>Approximately 1.2 meters.</note>, and its circumference is covered everywhere with **brass**, it is possible to express every single minute of all the **Degrees** through **transversal points** <note>Tycho’s innovative method of dividing a degree into minutes by using a diagonal line of dots, allowing for much higher precision.</note>. It serves its purpose quite usefully, and I have used it most especially when the stars approach the **Horizon**; for with those larger instruments, it may not be possible to do this so conveniently because of the obstructions of the crypts <note>Likely referring to the built structures or walls of the observatory at Uraniborg.</note> that might get in the way. This is also for other reasons, for which a greater convenience is sometimes given in these than in those larger ones regarding certain peculiar locations for carrying out this business, and also so that the same thing which is performed may be confirmed by a variety of **Instruments**.

It is also possible to adapt to this **Armilla**, wherever one wishes, its own semicircular **Equator** and a smooth cylindrical **Axis**; thus, it can be adapted for several uses, namely for capturing **Right Ascensions** and moments of time. We have also been accustomed to doing this on occasion.

# ASTROLABE.

->**Decorative Initial A**<-

THE **ASTROLABE** is an instrument used by the ancients, and most especially by **Astrologers**, and first invented (as I believe) by the **Arabs** with a skillful design. It compresses and organizes the circles of the **Celestial Sphere** and their use into a flat plane. We have not, however, wished to have any made of a just size until now, because its use in observations of the stars is less suitable, nor is it sufficient or certain for them. I do, however, possess a small one purchased at my own expense, solidly and skillfully crafted from **brass**, which only equals the length of a large span in diameter. It is composed according to that ancient method with its **plates** <term>matrices</term> <note>The interchangeable plates of an astrolabe, each engraved for a specific latitude.</note>, which **Johannes Stofflerus** <note>Johannes Stöffler (1452–1531), a German mathematician whose work on the construction of the astrolabe was the standard textbook of the era.</note> and others followed and clearly handed down. Let those who wish to do so seek the construction and use there.

But several years ago at **Nuremberg**, I took care to have round **brass plates** diligently prepared, which have more than two **cubits** in diameter and a thickness suitable for this; on these, I decided to subtly elaborate a **Universal Astrolabe** <term>Astrolabium Catholicum</term> which, without various plates, might supply on at least two faces everything that can be handed down concerning the entire doctrine of the **First Mover** <term>Primum mobile</term> <note>In the Ptolemaic system, the outermost sphere that carries all the inner spheres and stars from East to West once every 24 hours.</note>, and which otherwise is conveniently performed in the globular body that this instrument represents in a plane. Therefore, I also wish to add a peculiar **rete** <note>The "net," the top rotating part of an astrolabe that serves as a star map.</note> to it, and to mark it with the principal fixed stars, and indeed with many more than were in use by the ancients. This **Astrolabe** will perhaps surpass, by its own particular convenience, the invention of **de Roys** <note>Philippus de Roys, a mathematician who designed a specific type of universal astrolabe.</note>, and that other even more plausible one of **Gemma Frisius** made shortly afterward...

...convenience <note>original: "moditate", concluding the word "commoditate" from the previous page</note> and will surpass it in subtle reasoning, and will exist even more universally. But for us, up until now, due to the construction of other **Instruments** and a manifold mass of business, the opportunity has not been given to arrange such an **Astrolabe** <note>A complex instrument used to map the positions of stars and solve problems related to time and the position of the sun.</note>. We left it unfinished even more willingly for this reason: its use does not explore the motions and locations of the stars properly and precisely, but rather presupposes them to be known from elsewhere. Furthermore, we did not yet have the fixed stars, with which it ought to be marked, thoroughly explored according to their proper restoration. Since these are now exactly established for us by Divine benefit, it will not be difficult—once some leisure is obtained—to construct an Astrolabe of this kind: especially since the brass <term>Orichalcum</term> plates formerly prepared for those uses, as I said, are still partially preserved in my possession.

# CERTAIN OTHER ASTRONOMICAL
## INSTRUMENTS; EXPEDITING MANY MATTERS WITH WONDERFUL
### BREVITY, WHICH I HAVE RECENTLY DEVISED AND, 
### BY THE FAVOR OF THE DIVINE WILL,
### *have determined to elaborate in due time,*

**TH**ose instruments which we have already reviewed—so manifoldly depicted and explained—as well as all those briefly indicated afterward, we now have prepared and ready at hand. And although they are sufficient for all kinds of observations of the celestial bodies in their variety and abundance, I nevertheless silently foresaw that the necessity of migrating from **Denmark**, my sweetest homeland, was incumbent upon me. So that I would not unpraiseworthily abandon these studies of so many years, such great effort, and maximum expense—reaching even into my third set of seven years, which is usually a <term>Climacteric</term> <note>In traditional medicine and astrology, the "climacteric" years (multiples of 7 or 9) were thought to be periods of major life changes or health risks.</note> and subject to changes—I began to reflect within myself whether it might be possible to devise other certain astronomical tools. These would require fewer components yet would exhibit all those things conveniently and certainly which the others built here provide. They would also not lack the ease of being transported to any other place, if by chance those things which I possessed here could not be moved to me quickly enough and without inconvenience. 

Therefore, by Divine favor, I am certain I have recently discovered several of this kind. Among these are three **Rules** <note>Long, straight rods used for sighting or measuring.</note> which will be sufficient not only for taking the Altitudes of the Stars, like those <term>Ptolemaic</term> <note>Referring to instruments described by Claudius Ptolemy, such as the 'triquetrum'.</note> ones, but also for scrutinizing their Distances; these can be aptly assembled and transported to any place whatsoever. Furthermore, we have invented an **Armillary Instrument** <term>Armillary Sphere</term> consisting of only one and a half rings, which will not only scrutinize the Declinations and Right Ascensions of the stars, but will also most easily reveal their longitudes and latitudes. It will also suffice for observing Altitudes and <term>Azimuths</term> <note>The horizontal angle or direction of a celestial object, measured clockwise from the north.</note> if it is properly arranged for those uses. Indeed, it will not shrink from providing the distances of the stars, if one is pleased to use it so. Thus, this single instrument will be equal to all kinds of uses, and can be disassembled and reassembled without difficulty when desired, and transferred to other shores protected and enclosed in its own case. 

Moreover, another method occurred to me: how the same most concise and useful Instrument could be formed with few means through oblong Rules composed and connected in square and triangular form, so that it might equally provide all the things we said of the first method, and also certain other things if it should seem fit. 

This one will have a prerogative over the preceding one: when disassembled and packed into a tighter space and a straighter—namely, an oblong—chest, it can be transported even more conveniently to various places; also, it can be safely made in a larger size than the previous one. Finally, I have imagined yet another instrument which (one might think it impossible) is suitable for skillfully searching out the Declinations, Right Ascensions, Longitudes, Latitudes, Distances, Altitudes, and Azimuths of the stars by means of a **Single Circle**. This can also be constructed in a square form, though not quite so conveniently. Indeed, these very things can be done with only a half-circle, or even a third or...

These things, however, when properly set forth, will provide precisely and certainly enough that which is sought.

Concerning these and similar things—which I have devised thus far, partly out of the urgency of necessity (which teaches the **Arts** <term>Artes: Here referring to the practical application of mathematical and mechanical knowledge</term>), and partly those I am yet to discover in the future by the favor and kindness of the same **Divine Power** <note>original: "Numinis"</note>—I shall hardly publish anything publicly, lest such rare inventions, if made known to everyone, become (as usually happens) cheapened. Instead, I shall share them only with Illustrious and Princely men who are deeply moved by such matters; for it is the role of such men, more than other people, to be steeped in these sublime **Studies** and to cultivate them in a way that is both generous and praiseworthy. Once I am certain of their **Merciful Will** <note>original: "Clementi voluntate"; a formal address to the favor of a royal patron</note> toward me,

so long as they keep them among their own secrets.

# THE GREAT BRASS
## GLOBE.

<note>This page introduces the "Globus Magnus Orichalcicus," a masterpiece of Renaissance engineering. This massive celestial globe was nearly five feet in diameter and served as the primary record for Brahe’s revolutionary star catalog. It took several years to complete, as each of the 1,000 stars was individually positioned based on Brahe's precise observations.</note>

On the sphere itself, the constellations of the Zodiac are visible—most notably **Gemini** (the Twins), **Cancer** (the Crab), and **Leo** (the Lion)—each engraved with artistic and mathematical precision.</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of the "Great Brass Globe" introduced on the previous page. ### EXPLANATION OF THE CONSTRUCTION AND USE.

**G**reatly concerned with the utmost diligence and no less expense (as with all our other endeavors), we saw to it that a very large globe was constructed. Its interior consists of wooden material, skillfully composed of many rings and small pieces woven together, supported here and there from the center, and afterward turned into a spherical shape. Regarding this wooden frame, I took care to have it prepared in Augsburg <note>original: "Augusta Vindelicorum"</note> in the year 1570, before I departed from there for my homeland, having found a suitable craftsman who had elsewhere been sought in vain for a long time. Because of its vast size, which caused difficulty in transport, it remained in Augsburg into the fifth year, at which time I returned there. Specifically, in the year 1575, when I was returning from Italy at the time of the coronation of the most august Emperor Rudolph II at Regensburg and passed through Augsburg, I found that globe indeed long since prepared. However, it was not sufficiently perfect in its roundness everywhere and was, moreover, afflicted with certain cracks here and there. Nevertheless, I took care to have it brought to me in Denmark the following year, not without difficulty. There, after its cracks were repaired and its exquisite sphericity restored by the application of several hundred layers of parchment <note>original: "membranis"</note>, I made a trial for two years to see if it would give way in any direction, so that it might withstand the changes of two summers and winters. Finally, when I discovered that it consistently retained the perfectly round form of a globe everywhere, I safely saw to it that it was entirely covered over with thin plates of brass <term>Orichalcum: A golden-hued brass or bronze alloy highly prized for its durability and appearance in scientific instruments.</term> of the proper thickness. This was done with such care and subtlety that you would say the globe consisted of solid brass, with the joints of the plates scarcely appearing. Soon after, I had it polished into an exact spherical shape. Finally, having marked the Zodiac and the Equator upon it with their poles, we subtly distributed each of their degrees into 60 individual minutes by means of dots and transversal lines (as is our custom) <term>Transversal lines: A precision-grading technique used by Brahe to divide degrees into minutes by using a diagonal grid, allowing for much finer readings than standard scales.</term>. However, a year's space was still left before the final distribution so that a trial could be made as to whether, after the application of the brass, it would preserve its roundness in every way through both winter and summer. When this had been sufficiently tested, I adapted to it not only the circles I have mentioned, but also the stars of the eighth sphere <note>The "eighth sphere" refers to the realm of the fixed stars in the traditional geocentric model.</note>, as many as it was possible to obtain from the heavens in the meantime. These were most diligently observed and noted in their proper places. As their number increased more and more in successive years, they eventually filled a thousand, so that I have adapted to it all stars that are even slightly visible to the eye, deliberately relating their positions to the completed year 1600, which is shortly approaching. Thus, approximately 25 years passed from the first construction of this globe until it was finished with its divisions and stars. Although this delay might seem tedious, it nonetheless held no small utility, so that everything might be presented more consistently, fully, and perfectly. And, "quickly enough, if well enough" <note>original: "sat citò, si sat benè"; a Latin proverb emphasizing that the quality of work is more important than the speed of its completion.</note>. Meanwhile, however, its outer rings <note>original: "Armillæ"</note> were adapted, namely the Meridian, which the letters **E F G H** represent, and then the Horizon (about which more later), within which the globe itself is contained and revolves on its axis around the poles **I K**. This Meridian consists of solid steel and has divisions for every minute of all degrees. The Horizon **L M N**, having a width of a span, is also covered with brass and divided into its degrees and minutes according to the azimuth. A brass quadrant of the vertical circles descends from the Zenith to the Horizon, beginning at **B** and ending at the Horizon, subdivided into 90 degrees with their individual minutes. This serves for measuring both altitudes on the quadrant itself and azimuths on the Horizon. The Horizon itself, with its Meridian and thus the entire intercepted globe, rests upon a firm pedestal represented by **O P Q R S T V X**. In this pedestal are two iron pieces crossing each other in a decussate <note>X-shaped</note> fashion, which you see on one side; and there are two similar ones on the other side for the sake of support and stability, lest the Horizon or the entire machine wobble at all on account of its size and weight. This entire support is about 5 feet high; in its lower part, various practitioners of mathematics are notably depicted for the sake of ornament, together with certain other things adding beauty. The globe itself has a diameter of nearly 6 feet. From this, the Meridian, Horizon, and other parts can be estimated. I believe (let there be no envy in the saying) that such a globe-machine, so solidly and subtly fashioned and properly consistent in every part, has been constructed and completed by no one else until now in any part of the world. It is a huge and magnificent work, which many from various regions have traveled to Denmark to see, along with my other instruments, while the Kingdom of Urania <note>Brahe refers to his observatory, Uraniborg, on the island of Hven.</note> remained unharmed and the noble citadel stood. Around the Horizon, these words are read in golden letters:

> IN THE YEAR 1584 FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST, DURING THE REIGN OF FREDERICK II IN DENMARK, TYCHO BRAHE, SON OF OTTO, DECREED THAT THIS GLOBE, CONFORMED TO THE CELESTIAL MACHINE, SHOULD REPRESENT WITH PRECISION THE FIXED STARS OF THE EIGHTH SPHERE IN THEIR RESPECTIVE PLACES AS DISCOVERED BY INSTRUMENTS FROM THE HEAVENS; AND THROUGH THESE, HE DECREED TO INVESTIGATE THE APPEARANCES OF THE WANDERING STARS: THE HEAVEN HAVING BEEN OPENED TO EARTH-BORN MEN WHO GRASP THAT REASONING BY THIS MECHANICAL WORK. HE MADE THIS FOR HIMSELF AND HIS POSTERITY.

The reason the year 1584 is inscribed here is that it fell around the middle of the time while this structure was being elaborated—specifically, four years before the death of King Frederick of most praised memory, who liberally and mercifully fostered me and my studies and pursued me with royal love as long as he lived. I will also add this: that this magnificent machine has a cover above, indicated by **Y Z**, which is concave and round on the inside so that it may encompass the upper hemisphere of the globe. When lowered by a cord on a screw attached to a beam wherever desired, it protects the globe itself from dust and other contaminations.

The use of this globe is the same as that of other celestial globes, which I intend to describe at my leisure in a specific book. For it cannot be completed in a few words. However, it has this prerogative above the others on account of its size: that all things may be performed upon it most exactly, even to the very minute. Thus, individual matters concerning the doctrine of the *primum mobile* <note>The "first moved" or outermost sphere of the heavens in classical astronomy, responsible for the daily rotation of the stars.</note> and the observations of celestial bodies with respect to the Ecliptic and Equator,

-> or any other circles in the sphere, are performed here <-
-> mechanically with the least trouble <-
-> and without laborious calculation. <-

Brahe emphasizes the instrument's unprecedented accuracy and its role in calculating celestial positions without complex manual arithmetic.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the technical description of the Great Brass Globe's construction to a more personal, autobiographical account. Tycho Brahe explains the origins of his astronomical passion, beginning with his early education in Leipzig and his secret observations conducted despite his tutor's disapproval.</meta>

# CONCERNING WHAT WE HAVE ACHIEVED IN ASTRONOMY BY GOD'S GIFT, AND WHAT REMAINS TO BE ACCOMPLISHED BY HIS FAVOR.
### AN ACCOUNT OF OUR LABORS UNTIL NOW AND OUR FUTURE GOALS.

**I**n the year of our Lord 1563—roughly thirty-five years ago—at the time when that great conjunction of the superior planets <note>The "superior planets" refers to Jupiter and Saturn, the outermost planets known at the time.</note> occurred near the end of Cancer and the beginning of Leo, I was just completing my sixteenth year. I was then at Leipzig, applying myself to the study of the humanities. I was supported there, along with my tutor, by my dearest paternal uncle **GEORGE BRAHE**, who passed away about thirty years ago. My own father of honorable memory, **OTTO BRAHE**, did not particularly desire that his sons (of whom he had five, I being the firstborn) be steeped in Latin letters—though he later regretted this. However, my said uncle had raised me from my earliest infancy and thereafter generously supported me as long as he lived, until my eighteenth year. He always treated me as a son and had decided to adopt me as his heir. His own marriage was childless; he had married the most noble and prudent Lady **INGERA OXE** <note>original: "INGERA OXONIA"</note>, the sister of the great **PETER OXE** <note>original: "PETRI OXONII"</note> (who later became Master of the Household for the Kingdom of Denmark). She died five years ago, and while she lived, she embraced me with singular love, just like a son. Afterward, for twelve years, she served as the Queen’s Mistress of the Robes <note>original: "Aulæ magistra"</note> during the time of King **FREDERICK II** of most praised memory. She was succeeded in that same office for eight years by my dearest and most honored mother, **BEATA BILLE** <note>original: "BEATA BILLEA"</note>, who by God’s grace is still surviving at the age of 71. Thus, it happened by a peculiar fate that I, having been taken away by my aforementioned uncle from my own parents in infancy without their knowledge, was placed by him in a Latin school around the age of seven. Around my fourteenth year, I was sent to Leipzig to continue my studies, where I remained for three years. I recount these details from a bit further back so that it may be clear by what occasion I was first applied to liberal studies and later turned my mind to Astronomy, and so that I may gratefully honor the memory of the parents to whom I owe so much.

Since I had already laid some foundations of the rudiments of Astronomy in my homeland of Denmark (to get to the point I intended) through books—especially the Ephemerides <note>Calendars predicting the positions of celestial bodies.</note>—to which I was drawn by a certain natural inclination, I began at Leipzig to cultivate Astronomy more and more. I did this using books I had bought secretly, acting against the wishes of my tutor, who preferred that I study law, claiming it was my parents' will (which I nevertheless did as much as my age allowed). I had to act in such a way that my tutor would not get wind of it. Soon, I gradually became accustomed to identifying the constellations of the sky. All of those that were conveniently visible on that horizon I learned within the space of a month by my own effort and with no one showing me, using a certain small globe, barely the size of a fist <note>original: "instar pugni"</note>, which I used to carry with me secretly in the evenings. Indeed, it never happened that I had a teacher in Mathematics elsewhere; otherwise, I might perhaps have progressed sooner and further in that field. 

Soon I also began to pay attention to the motions of the planets. Because I noticed—both from their alignments with the fixed stars and through lines drawn here and there—even from that tiny globe, that their celestial locations did not agree with the calculations of either the Alfonsine or the Copernican tables, though they were closer to the latter than the former. After that, I watched more diligently every day to note their appearances, and I frequently compared them with the calculations of the Prussian Tables (for by then I had made myself familiar with these through my own study), not trusting the Ephemerides. For I had found the Stadian tables <note>Johannes Stadius published popular ephemerides based on Copernican theory.</note>, which were then the only ones available derived from those numbers, to be careless and full of errors in many places. But since I had no instruments ready at hand—my tutor begrudging me them—I performed the task from the beginning as best I could with a certain large pair of dividers <note>original: "circino quodam majusculo"</note>. I would apply the hinge to my eye and direct both legs toward the planet to be observed and some nearby fixed star, or even by taking the distances of two planets from each other according to the size of the circle, I would mechanically investigate the degrees <term>sometimes</term> <gloss>sometimes</gloss> intercepted between them. Although this method of observation was not sufficiently exact, I was nevertheless led by it to clearly detect the intolerable errors of both sets of calculations. 

This was proven more than enough in the motion of Saturn and Jupiter by that great conjunction of the year 1563, which I mentioned at the beginning (and for which reason I primarily started from it). Indeed, that event misled the Alfonsine numbers by an entire month, and Copernicus by several days, though only a few. For his restoration of these two planets does not stray so far from the celestial norm, especially regarding Saturn, which I noticed never eluded the Copernican calculation in the sky by more than half a degree, or at most two-thirds of one; Jupiter sometimes suggests a slightly larger difference. Afterward, in the year 1564, I secretly had a certain wooden Astronomical Radius <note>A cross-staff or Jacob’s staff, used to measure angular distances between stars.</note> made for me according to the instructions of Gemma Frisius. Bartholomew Scultetus—who was also living in Leipzig then and was a close friend due to our shared studies—had carefully marked it with transversal points <note>A method for increasing the precision of scale readings.</note> learned from his teacher Homelius. Having obtained this "radius," I thereafter applied myself strenuously to the observation of the stars whenever a welcome clarity of sky was granted. Often, while my tutor was sleeping and unaware, I observed from the window of my room...

<summary>Tycho Brahe recounts his early life and how he secretly pursued astronomy at Leipzig against his tutor's wishes, discovering significant errors in existing planetary tables during the Great Conjunction of 1563.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s autobiographical account. He moves from his early secret observations in Leipzig to his travels across Europe, the appearance of the New Star of 1572, and the pivotal moment when King Frederick II granted him the island of Hven to build Uraniborg.</meta>

broad <note>original: "lati"</note> and of a certain size; I stayed awake to capture these observations and recorded them separately in a little book, which I still preserve. I noticed shortly after, however, that the angles of distance—taken in various ways according to the equal divisions of the <term>Cross-staff <note>An instrument used to measure the angle between two stars. Tycho refers to it here as a "Radius."</note></term> and related to numbers geometrically through the Rule of Proportion—did not entirely agree with one another. Therefore, having investigated the cause of the error, I discovered a Table by which it was possible to correct the defects of that Cross-staff. For at that time, I did not have the means to arrange for a new one to be made; my <term>tutor <note>original: "Petagogus." Brahe often complained that his chaperone, Anders Sørensen Vedel, tried to keep him focused on law rather than astronomy.</note></term>, who was in charge of the money, did not allow such things to be manufactured for me. And so I carried out many observations with that Cross-staff as long as I stayed in Leipzig, and afterwards even when I was called back to my homeland.

Then going back to Germany, first at **Wittenberg** and soon at **Rostock**, I attended to the stars as much as I could. Around the year 1569 and the following year, while staying at **Augsburg**, besides that very large <term>Quadrant <note>A 90-degree measuring tool. Tycho had built a massive version in Augsburg to achieve greater precision.</note></term> which we constructed in the Consul's garden outside the city (of which was spoken above in its place), I frequently observed the stars with another instrument, namely a wooden **Sextant** devised by us there, and recorded the notes in a special book. I did the same thing later, having returned home again, with another similar but somewhat larger instrument, especially when that wonderful **New Star** <note>The Supernova of 1572 in the constellation Cassiopeia, which proved the heavens were not changeless.</note> which shone out in the year 1572 recalled me to the consideration of heavenly things. At that time, I was sweating over <term>chemical labors <note>original: "Pyronomicis laboribus." Brahe refers here to alchemy and laboratory chemistry, which he practiced alongside astronomy.</note></term>, which I had begun in Augsburg and was then continuing; I carefully observed that star, describing it first in a small book at that time, and finally in a complete volume, faithfully and accurately.

Afterwards, I gradually arranged for more and more astronomical instruments to be made, some of which I carried with me while traveling again throughout all of Germany and passing through part of Italy, where I did not cease from observing the stars in the middle of my journeys whenever the opportunity was given. Finally, returning to my homeland around the age of 28, I was quietly preparing shortly after for another and more lasting departure. For I had decided to choose **Basel** <note>original: "Basileæ Rauracorum"</note> or its vicinity as my place of residence, which I had previously scouted for this purpose, so that I might lay the foundations for the restoration of astronomy there. For that place pleased me above others in Germany, both because of the very famous University and the exceptionally learned men there, as well as the healthiness of the air and the convenience of living; and because Basel was at the crossroads of three very large regions of Europe: Italy, France, and Germany. Thus, I could establish friendships by letter with many illustrious and learned men from all sides and spread my discoveries more widely for public use. 

For I foresaw that I could not cultivate these studies comfortably or safely enough in my homeland, especially if I remained in **Scania** <note>The southernmost province of modern Sweden, then part of Denmark.</note> at my seat of **Knudstrup**, or elsewhere in some large part of Denmark, where there would be a frequent gathering of nobles and friends who would be an obstacle by interrupting my philosophical leisure. But it truly happened that while I was turning these things over in my secret mind and was already girding myself for the journey as if I were not doing so, the most Serene and Powerful King of Denmark and Norway, **FREDERICK II** of blessed memory, sent one of his noble pages to Knudstrup with royal letters, which commanded me to come to him immediately, wherever he might be found in Zealand. 

After I had appeared immediately, that best and never-sufficiently-praised King, of his own accord and merciful will, offered me that island in the famous Danish Strait <note>The Øresund.</note> called **Hven** <note>original: "Huennam"</note> by our people (which may be called **Venusia** in Latin, while foreigners call it **Scarlatina**). He asked that I arrange for buildings and instruments for astronomical exercises and also for chemical labors to be built upon it, and he graciously promised that he would liberally provide for the expenses. Therefore, having deliberated for a short while and shared counsel with wise men, I changed my prior opinion and not unwillingly complied with the King's will—especially when I saw that on that island, which is situated separately between Scania and Zealand, I could be freed from the noise of interrupters. Thus, the leisure and opportunity I was seeking elsewhere could be granted to me in my own country, to which we owe more than to all other regions. 

Soon, therefore, I began to build the Castle of **Uraniborg**, suitable for astronomical matters, in the year 1576. Successively, I completed both the buildings and various astronomical instruments suitable for conducting observations accurately, the main part of which is described and explained in this book. Meanwhile, I also stayed awake vigorously for observations, having called to this service several students powerful in sharp wit and vision, some of whom I supported there continuously and instructed in these disciplines and many other philosophical matters. And so by God's kindness, it happened that with almost no clear night overlooked, we obtained very many and most exact celestial astronomical observations: both of the fixed stars and all the wandering planets, as well as the comets that shone out in the meantime, seven of which we accurately noted there from the heavens. 

In this way, twenty-one years of diligent observations were completed there. I first wrote them down in large volumes and later arranged them separately into individual books for each year, and I have seen to it that they are described for the world. I performed this with such an order that the fixed stars noted in that year had their own place, and all the planets were recorded particularly and distinctly, beginning with the Sun and Moon and passing through the remaining five planets down to **Mercury**. For we did not even leave Mercury unobserved, though it rarely appears. Indeed, almost every year it was diligently noted by us, both in the morning and evening, although the great **Copernicus** excuses himself for not observing it due to the excessive inclination of the sphere and the vapors of the Vistula River; yet...

This page continues Tycho Brahe’s autobiographical account of his astronomical career. Having previously discussed his early training and the construction of his large quadrant in Augsburg, he now moves to a critique of his predecessor Copernicus and a rigorous classification of his own lifelong observations.

...while we, in an even more inclined part of the sphere <note>Meaning a higher latitude, where the stars and planets appear at different angles than in southern Europe</note>, and on an island surrounded on all sides by a more vaporous sea, have many times (as mentioned) looked upon it and measured it. But perhaps the house where **Copernicus** lived was so arranged that it did not offer a free horizon in every direction, and thus it was less convenient for observations, especially those lower down <note>original: "declivioribus," referring to objects near the horizon, such as the planet Mercury</note>. This is also what my student reported to me, whom I had sent there 14 years ago for the purpose of examining the altitude of the Pole. Consequently, Copernicus, lacking his own specific readings for **Mercury**, was forced to borrow several from the book of observations by **Walther**, a student of **Regiomontanus**, and some from a citizen of Nuremberg; although he did not apply these quite faithfully or precisely to his own theories and demonstrations, it would nonetheless be desirable if, for the other planets—which he labored with great daring to restore from his own observations—he had not obtained results much more uncertain. Certainly then we would now have their <term>Apogees <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the center of motion.</note></term> and <term>Eccentricities <note>The measurement of how much an orbit deviates from a perfect circle.</note></term>, and other related matters, in a much more corrected state; and I could have spared myself many years of the greatest and most tireless labors and enormous expenses. Since I have ready at hand the most choice and accurate observations of 21 years, sought from the heavens with various and skillfully crafted instruments <note>original: "Organis"</note> which we showed previously (to say nothing now of the observations of the 14 preceding years), I guard them as a rare and most precious treasure; however, I may perhaps eventually make them all public, if Divine mercy grants me time to add even more to them.

From all this, it is clear that I have been accustomed to the observation of the stars since the sixteenth year of my age, and have continued them for almost 35 consecutive years until now; among these, however, some are more certain and excellent than others. For those which I carried out in **Leipzig** during my childhood, and up to the twenty-first year of my age, I am accustomed to call *childish and doubtful*. Those which I acquired thereafter up to my twenty-eighth year, I call *youthful and mediocre*. But the third set, which I measured thereafter at **Uraniborg** with those most exact instruments in my more mature age for nearly 21 years, up to the completion of my fiftieth year, I call and judge to be *manly, validated, and most certain*. Upon these I strive with arduous effort to found and build the restoration of astronomy, although several observations from the preceding years also contribute not a little here. What we have performed and worked out in these matters so far by the help of God, and what remains to be executed and perfected in the future by the favor of the same Deity, are as follows:

We have, above all, restored the motion of the **SUN** from the most accurate observations of many years, not only by most accurately investigating the <term>Equinoctial <note>The points where the sun crosses the celestial equator, commonly known as the Equinoxes.</note></term> entries, but also by including these positions together with the intermediate <term>Solstitial <note>The points of the summer and winter solstices.</note></term> points, especially in the northern semicircle of the <term>Ecliptic <note>The sun's apparent path across the sky.</note></term>; for there the midday Sun is not subject to <term>refractions <note>The bending of light through the atmosphere, which distorts the perceived position of celestial bodies near the horizon.</note></term>. Therefore, I have verified this many times on both sides, and from this I have geometrically deduced both the Apogee and the Eccentricity of the Sun corresponding to these times, in both of which an evident error crept into both the **Alphonsine** tables and **Copernicus**, so much so that the Sun's Apogee precedes the Copernican numbers by nearly three degrees, and the Eccentricity is almost 2 and 1/30 parts (where the radius of the eccentric circle is 60). In Copernicus, nearly a fourth part of one is missing. Even in establishing the simple motion of the Sun, he commits an error of nearly a quarter of a degree in these years. From this, the Alphonsine tables compared with the Copernican ones can be evaluated. I have indeed deduced from here the tables of both the equal motions and the <term>Prosthaphaereses <note>A mathematical correction used to find the true position of a planet based on its average motion.</note></term> in exact numbers: so that there is no longer any doubt about the course of the Sun, verified to the highest degree <note>original: "ad amussim"</note> and revealed by suitable numbers. And it was absolutely necessary that this be done first for the Sun of all things; for it stands as the rule for celestial motions and describes the Ecliptic, to which the other motions are referred. I also discovered its maximum <term>Obliquity <note>The tilt of the Earth's axis, or the angle between the celestial equator and the ecliptic.</note></term> from the Equator to be different than what Copernicus and his contemporaries thought, namely 23 degrees and 31 1/2 minutes. Therefore, it is 3 1/2 minutes greater than their discovery, specifically by avoiding the refraction of the Sun in its winter position, which they thoughtlessly neglected. We have also provided the Sun with special tables for Revolutions: then we also added new tables of Declinations and Right Ascensions based on our findings. Moreover, we have addressed its <term>Parallaxes <note>The apparent change in position of an object when viewed from different points.</note></term> and Refractions in singular tables.

In the **MOON** also we have applied no less diligence, so that its errors might be resolved, which are entangled in a manifold complexity and are not so simple or as easily apparent as the ancients and **Copernicus** thought. For it has another introduction of inequality in Longitude than was noticed by them. Nor are the proportions of the orbits denoted precisely enough in it. Furthermore, it exhibits other limits of maximum Latitude than those prescribed by **Ptolemy**, whom all other astronomers afterwards followed too securely in this part: indeed, the Moon changes this itself unequally to a difference of a third part of one degree. Nor does it subject those **Nodes** <note>The two points where the Moon's orbit intersects the Ecliptic.</note>, where its path crosses the Ecliptic, to equal motion as has been thought until now, but makes them "nod" <note>original: "nutare," likely referring to an oscillation or variation in the nodal motion</note> back and forth in each revolution, and that with a sensible difference which exceeds a degree and a half by a small amount on either side. We have both noticed and restored all these things from 16 years of most diligent observations, among which are 18 Lunar Eclipses, accurately recorded from the heavens. For not a few are sufficient to scrutinize its first inequality, as Pto...

<summary>Tycho Brahe critiques Copernicus's reliance on second-hand data for Mercury and categorizes his own life's work into three stages: "childish," "youthful," and "manly" (mature). He details his significant corrections to the solar and lunar models, specifically regarding orbital eccentricity, solar obliquity, and the complex motions of the Moon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s autobiographical account of his astronomical observations. Having just criticized Copernicus's reliance on second-hand data for Mercury, Brahe now details his own extensive efforts to reform the theories of the Moon and the fixed stars.</meta>

<sig>G 3</sig>

...Ptolemy, Al-Battani <note>original: "Albategnius," a 9th-century Arab astronomer whose work refined Ptolemaic models</note>, and Copernicus were of various opinions; six solar eclipses were also taken into council, insofar as they might contribute something to this matter. Furthermore, the Moon was explored in its quadratures <note>The phases where the moon is 90 degrees from the sun, such as the first and last quarter</note> and at its greatest elongations from its mean motion, both around its <term>Apogee <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note></term> and <term>Perigee <note>The point in an orbit closest to the Earth.</note></term>, as well as intermediate locations, in many ways and very frequently. This was done so that its intricate motion could be properly established. This motion tortured us with incredible labor over many years, yet we finally discovered the methods by which its unstable and multiform wanderings might obey circles and numbers. Therefore, having established another hypothesis consistent with appearances, we adapted the numbers for both equal and unequal motion, not only in longitude but also in latitude. We also provided for its <term>Parallaxes <note>The apparent displacement of an object when viewed from different positions; crucial for calculating the distance to the Moon.</term></note> in a manner different from what was practiced by Ptolemy and Copernicus, just as experience—agreeing with this single hypothesis—required. Nor did we omit the care of **Lunar refractions**, since without these, the rest cannot be exactly discerned. All these things, and several others concerning Lunar appearances, have been reduced into succinct <term>Canons <note>Mathematical tables or rules used for astronomical calculation.</note></term>, which we have intended for the calculation and extraction of its motions. Therefore, with the path of both luminaries <note>The Sun and the Moon</note> thus restored so that they correspond to the celestial appearances themselves, their eclipses as well as other configurations, methods, and entries can hereafter be established most correctly—something which has been long desired until now. Truly, what has been said so far regarding the restoration of the Sun and Moon, as far as their motions analogous to the heavens are concerned, these things and more, the first chapter of our <term>Progymnasmata <note>Tycho's work "Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata" (Exercises for the Restoration of Astronomy).</note></term> of astronomical restoration will clearly explain. There, he who is eager for these things will have his wish granted. It remains only, in the further consideration of these luminaries, that all things be adapted to several centuries and rendered more universal; this can be performed with no great trouble, provided that the observations of the ancients and our predecessors, upon which we must rely, are correctly maintained. This fuller and universal <note>original: "Catholicam," in the sense of all-encompassing or general</note> exposition we reserve for the work **Theatrum Astronomicum** <note>The "Astronomical Theater," a massive project Tycho planned but never completed.</note>. Meanwhile, those devoted to astronomy will be able to enjoy quite usefully what we have handed down in the cited place in the *Progymnasmata*, and satisfy their desire.

Furthermore, we have also most accurately verified all the **FIXED STARS**, whatever are visible to the eye, even those called of the sixth magnitude, both according to longitude and latitude, down to the very minute and sometimes even half a minute. In this way, a thousand stars have been rectified by us, whereas the ancients numbered no more than 1,022 <note>original: "2 2. plures," likely referring to the 1,022 stars in Ptolemy's Almagest</note>; those living in a more "upright sphere" <note>regions closer to the equator</note> could see 200 more stars that always remain hidden to us here. In their place, however, we have restored other tiny stars overlooked by them due to their smallness. We labored in this arduous work for nearly 20 years, because we wanted to test everything accurately with various instruments. Because tiny stars are only seen in winter when the nights are dark and the Moon is hidden, the expectation of many years was required before these things could be duly and sufficiently completed; especially since around the new moons themselves, when it is most necessary to put this into practice, it was rarely clear weather. By what method we arrived at an exact knowledge of the longitudes of the fixed stars from the <term>Equinox <note>One of the two times a year when the sun crosses the celestial equator and day and night are of equal length.</note></term>—using Venus, both as a morning and evening star, with the Sun as an intermediary—and how we proved this in many ways, referring everything back to that star which is brighter above the head <note>Likely referring to a star in the constellation Aries or a primary reference star like Alpha Arietis</note>, number three (which we established by deliberate effort as the foundation for the rest, since the two preceding ones are less visible), the second chapter of the said *Progymnasmata* will abundantly explain. It will also show by what method we deduced the others from there: and first, how by a triple reasoning throughout the whole circuit of the sky, along the Zodiac and the Equator, we reduced certain select stars into an exact completion of the entire great circle. I also noticed that there is not as much complexity in the inequality of their longitudes as Copernicus thought. For those things which he imagined concerning this matter crept in through the fault of observations, both ancient and recent. Therefore, the <term>Precession of the Equinox <note>The slow, circular shifting of the Earth's axis, which makes the stars appear to shift position over long periods.</note></term> in these years is not as slow as he wished. For the fixed stars do not now complete one degree in a hundred years, as his calculation maintains, but only in 71 1/2 years. This is what they have almost always been accustomed to do before, if the observations of our ancestors are correctly limited, with at least a small irregularity occurring accidentally from elsewhere; as we shall reveal more widely in its own time, God willing.

Indeed, I was the first to discover that the latitudes of the fixed stars are altered somewhat due to the variation of the obliquity of the <term>Ecliptic <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky; Tycho discovered that the angle of this path relative to the Earth's equator changes over time.</note></term>, and I have demonstrated this with various examples in the said chapter. Thus we can testify enough and more, and experience itself will support it, that the locations of the fixed stars have been verified by us with the highest and infallible accuracy, so much so that we have proven many of them many times even with different instruments, with the individual readings coinciding as one. Nor did we execute this business mechanically, even though the <term>Great Brass Globe <note>One of Tycho's most famous instruments: a 1.5-meter wooden globe covered in brass plates upon which he engraved the positions of the stars he observed.</note></term> was at hand. Rather, we laboriously reduced all the stars into their proper places through the reasoning of triangles <note>trigonometry</note>. This is evident from what we have exhibited concerning the **Constellation of Cassiopeia** (in which we number 26 stars, twice as many as the ancients) toward the end of the same chapter; although we used many other applications of triangles and various reasonings for other stars as seemed more convenient. If the ancients and our predecessors had applied such diligence in denoting the locations of the fixed stars, their catalog <note>original: "abacus"</note> reaching us from Hipparchus would by no means have been so faulty; it is not even correct in that sixth part of a degree which it alone displays, but often suggests a far greater and intolerable deviation—which even the mere distances between the stars, which always...
