<summary>Tycho Brahe describes his massive multi-decade project to reform lunar theory and create a new, 1,000-star catalog with unprecedented precision. He critiques Copernicus’s theories on the precession of the equinoxes and highlights his own discovery of latitude variations in fixed stars.</summary>

<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Moon, Fixed Stars, Copernicus, Precession of the Equinoxes, Cassiopeia, Astronomical Instruments, Trigonometry, Ecliptic, Star Catalog</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s account of his astronomical reforms. Following his work on the Moon, he moves to the cataloging of fixed stars and the correction of planetary motions, emphasizing the precision of his new instruments and the discovery of atmospheric refraction.</meta>

...remain unchanged, as is clearly shown. In very many stars, these positions are far different than the figures of the ancients require. However, that all the fixed stars remain perpetually at the same distances from one another is sufficiently proven by those which *Hipparchus* and *Ptolemy* <note>Hipparchus (2nd century BCE) and Ptolemy (2nd century CE) were the primary authorities for star positions until the early modern era.</note> recorded as being in a straight line; indeed, that same alignment remains unchanged to this day. Of those fixed stars whose longitude and latitude we have restored to the very minute, and sometimes even to half a minute (as was said), we shall provide a standard presentation in its proper time and place.

Nor were we occupied only with accurately establishing the longitudes and latitudes of the fixed stars, but for 100 of the principal ones, we derived their **Right Ascensions** and **Declinations** <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude and latitude used to map the sky.</note> through the logic of triangles. We have adapted these for two centuries in such a way that they can be proportionally satisfied for each intermediate year. Furthermore, we have aided the study of stars with a special table for **Refractions** <note>The bending of light as it passes through the Earth's atmosphere, which makes celestial objects appear higher than they actually are.</note>, constructed from long and varied experience. For unless these are guarded against, the true locations of the stars—especially when they approach the horizon at the twentieth degree of altitude—cannot be obtained. Therefore, in the star positions corrected by us, we have always provided for the inclusion of refractions where necessary. These refractions (to mention this in passing) behave slightly differently for the stars than they do for the *Sun*. In the stars, they also differ somewhat from those which the *Moon* introduces; these matters have long since been untangled and made known by us.

Thus, nothing more is required regarding the fixed stars, except that their universal motion be adapted for all ages of the world’s duration. This would not be difficult to perform accurately, provided that the observations of the ancients in these matters had not been recorded in too broad a manner. Nevertheless, by applying a suitable limit, I trust that I shall satisfy the students of astronomy in this part as well, as far as is possible.

It would be desirable, indeed, if the remaining stars noted in antiquity, which do not appear in our climate <note>Meaning the northern latitudes of Europe where Brahe observed.</note>, were added to the prior thousand verified by me. Then also those other stars which not even the ancients living in the region of *Egypt* could see, which exist near the **Antarctic Pole**. For we have heard by the report of those who have sailed beyond the *Equator* that most beautiful stars shine there as well. As far as the first part is concerned, one would have to set out for *Egypt* or a similar location in Africa, and there diligently note as many stars as arise. To obtain the second, one must sail to the more southern parts of *America* or another region beyond the *Equator* where all the constellations around the Antarctic Pole are visible, and there establish observations of them. Therefore, if any Illustrious and Powerful Lords should not find it burdensome to assist our desires and those of others in one or both of these tasks, they would surely perform a distinguished service, to be celebrated by posterity with perpetual memory; for as far as is known, this has been attempted by no one in a proper manner, let alone absolutely achieved. I shall not refuse to supply the instruments and means conducive to this, provided there are those willing to take charge of it and provide suitable men for such a laudable business.

Finally, we have not left untouched the investigation of the errors of the five remaining **PLANETS**, nor the task of remedying them. In all of these, we have reorganized the <term>Apogees <note>The point in a planet's orbit furthest from the center of motion.</note></term> and the <term>Eccentricities <note>The measurement of how much an orbit deviates from a perfect circle.</note></term> (which was the primary requirement), as well as their simple motion and the proportions of their orbits and circuits. We have done this so that they no longer teem with errors, as has been the case until now. In the Apogees themselves, we have detected a certain other inequality not previously perceived. Furthermore, we perceived that the annual circuit—which *Copernicus* explained through the motion of the Earth in a great orbit, and the ancients explained according to <term>Epicycles <note>Small circles moved upon by planets, which themselves move along larger circles; a key feature of the Ptolemaic system.</note></term>—is subject to a certain variation. For all these and other pertinent matters, we provided a certain unique hypothesis <note>Likely the "Tychonic System," where the planets orbit the Sun, and the Sun orbits a stationary Earth.</note> invented and established by us 14 years ago from the appearances themselves. Certain others (among whom I know three notable individuals) were not ashamed afterward to claim it as their own invention with too bold a face, and to sell it to others. I shall, in the proper time and place, indicate the occasions of these things and restrain and refute their excessive license. I shall prove it to be so evidently that there will be no room for hesitation or contradiction for those endowed with sincere judgment. I will, however, spare them if they frankly confess what they have done and restore what is mine; therefore, I willingly abstain from naming them now.

We also did not permit the latitudes of the planets to remain uncorrected, as our predecessors from *Ptolemy* onward had done. Instead, by diligently attending to these in the five planets throughout their entire circuit, we defined the <term>Nodes <note>The two points where a planet's orbit crosses the plane of the Ecliptic.</note></term> and other crossings through the Ecliptic, so that all things are consistent with the sky itself. In these, we clearly noticed that the nodes of the three superior planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>, or the limits of their greatest latitudes, are not subject to the motion of the Apogees in a conforming way, but possess a motion of their own—provided that what *Ptolemy* handed down concerning them is correct, from whom both the *Alphonsine* tables and *Copernicus* borrowed their data without applying any correction from their own observations. From this it happens that the planets are sometimes in the southern part of the sky when their figures show them to be in the northern part, and vice versa.

Nothing else remains to be labored over in the five wandering stars, except that these findings regarding Longitudes and Latitudes—and all matters pertaining to them which behave differently than the common tables—have been deduced from accurate observations from the heavens for over 25 years (to say nothing now of the first 10 years)...

<summary>Brahe discusses his cataloging of 1,000 fixed stars, his groundbreaking discovery of atmospheric refraction, and his call for southern hemisphere expeditions. He also claims priority over a new planetary hypothesis (the Tychonic system) and critiques his predecessors for inaccurate planetary latitude data.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s description of his life's work. Having established a new catalog of stars and corrected the positions of the Sun and Moon, he now discusses the publication of these results in new tables and his groundbreaking observations of "New Stars" and comets which challenged the ancient Aristotelian view of the universe.</meta>

<sig>G 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: properly</meta>

...provided through our explored and established observations. These, having been redacted into new and competent **Canons** <note>In this context, "canons" refers to systematic tables or rules used for astronomical calculations.</note>, shall be set forth in numbers; some of the beginnings and foundations of these have already been laid by us. The rest can be completed without difficulty by a few calculators, and thereafter, **Ephemerides** <note>term: Ephemerides: Tables giving the predicted daily positions of celestial bodies.</note> for as many of the following years as may be desired can be derived from them. The same is to be provided for the Sun and the Moon, whose Tables we already have ready at hand, so that in future times it may be proven with the least effort that the motions of the Celestial Bodies restored by us agree with the appearances themselves and are everywhere correctly established.

Finally, it would contribute greatly to the complete embrace of Astronomy to have not only the Latitudes, but much more importantly the **Longitudes** of terrestrial places properly known. In this part, we have hitherto labored diligently as much as was permitted, and we are persuaded that we have arranged several locations more correctly. However, since this business cannot be sufficiently supported unless several Lunar Eclipses are designated in different and far-distant places with equal diligence regarding the moments of time by various observers: if in this part also Kings and Princes and other Illustrious and Powerful men existing in disjointed Regions of the Earthly Globe would apply a liberal and suitable provision, they would indeed perform a great and worthwhile service. Thus Astronomy, insofar as it needs the differences of terrestrial Horizons, will emerge more consummate.

Furthermore, while we have attended diligently for many years with unwearied study to those perennial bodies of the Heaven that are coeval with the World, we have also scrutinized with no less diligence whatever adventitious bodies of that same Ethereal World have shone forth in the meantime. And first, that **New and exceedingly admirable Star** <note>This refers to the Supernova of 1572 in the constellation Cassiopeia, which proved that the "heavens" were not unchanging.</note>, which began to appear toward the end of the year 1572 and lasted for 16 months before it ceased to be visible at all. Concerning that Star (I say), while it still lasted, we wrote a certain little book (as we also briefly hinted above) and showed its appearances. But after several years, resuming this care, on account of the magnitude of the wonder we composed an entire volume about it, which it pleased us to insert into the first volume of our *Progymnasmata* <note>term: Progymnasmata: Tycho’s major work, "Introduction to the New Astronomy."</note> for certain reasons disclosed therein. There I not only clearly exhibit our observations on this stupendous star and demonstrate them Geometrically, but furthermore, I examine with Philosophical liberty the opinions of as many others as I could learn and obtain concerning the same, and I investigate and bring into the open to what extent they were congruent with the very truth itself, or not.

Concerning the **Comet** also of huge appearance, which followed five years later <note>The Great Comet of 1577.</note>, we have prepared a special book, in which we likewise treat sufficiently of the same both from our own observations and decisions as well as from the opinions of others. To these we join certain Apologies <note>In the classical sense, "Apologies" are formal defenses of a position or argument.</note> pertaining to this matter and more fully elucidating this Cometic business; and we wished this whole thing to fill the first part of the second volume of the *Progymnasmata*. In the second part, we shall be occupied in the future with the remaining six smaller Comets, which we noted with equal diligence in several successive years. Although these are not yet fully completed, nevertheless the principal and great part of them, which serve for Demonstrations, are prepared. For those perpetual stars did not leave us leisure to dwell too long on these vanishing and quickly passing ones. I hope, however, by the favor of Divine goodness, that I shall shortly complete this other part of the second volume. In this, through all the Comets which I have designated, I shall clearly demonstrate—more openly in some, but in others as opportunity permitted—that they were all situated in the **Ethereal region of the World** <note>That is, in "outer space," beyond the Moon.</note>, and by no means in the **Sublunary Air**, as Aristotle and his followers have in vain persuaded us for so many centuries. Why I treat of Comets in the second volume of the *Progymnasmata* before I betake myself to the remaining five Planets (of which I intend to treat in the third), I bring forward reasons in the Preface there. The chief of these is that from the Comets, which I prove to be truly Ethereal, it can be sufficiently established that the whole Heaven is most clear and fluid, and filled with no hard and real spheres. For these comets for the most part follow different paths than any Celestial Spheres could provide, and consequently, the **Hypothesis** invented by us <note>Tycho's Tychonic System, where the planets orbit the Sun, and the Sun orbits the Earth.</note> admits of no absurdity, since no penetration of Spheres and dimensions occurs where none are actually given.

And concerning those things which we have partly performed in **ASTRONOMICAL** matters until now, and partly remain yet to be performed, let it suffice to have indicated them thus briefly.

In **ASTROLOGICAL** matters also, which scrutinize the effects of the stars, we have placed no contemptible labor, so that these also, vindicated from faults and superstitions, may be for the most part consonant with the experience upon which they rest. For I deem it less possible to find an exact reason in them that is equal to Geometric and Astronomical truth. But since I had been more intensely addicted in my youth to this Prognostic part of Astronomy, which is prophetic and conjectural, and had afterwards set it aside because the motions of the Stars upon which it is founded were not sufficiently understood, until this inconvenience might be remedied: having finally discovered the paths of the Stars more exactly, I found upon resuming it that there is a greater certainty in this knowledge than one might easily think—however vain and frustrated it may be held not only by the common people, but also by many Learned men, and even some Mathematicians among them. This is true both in meteorological influences and predictions, and in **Genethlialogy** <note>term: Genethlialogy: The casting of birth charts or horoscopes.</note>, provided that the times...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes his work on new astronomical tables and his revolutionary findings on the Supernova of 1572 and the Comet of 1577, which proved the heavens are fluid rather than made of solid spheres. He also argues for a reformed astrology based on accurate data rather than superstition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s autobiographical account of his scientific pursuits. Having discussed his astronomical observations and the correction of terrestrial coordinates, he now turns to his work in astrology and alchemy (which he calls "Terrestrial Astronomy") before introducing letters of recommendation from fellow scholars.</meta>

<meta>catchword: properly</meta>

...are properly established, and the motions of the Stars and their entries <note>In astrology, "ingress" refers to the moment a planet enters a new zodiac sign.</note> into the heavens are applied in harmony, and both directions and revolutions <note>Technical astrological terms used to predict the timing of events in a person's life based on their birth chart.</note> are properly administered. In these two areas, we have also constructed another method derived from experience itself, different from what has been used until now. 

But we do not willingly share such **Astrological** matters with others, since we have found not a few things in them through exploration. Indeed, not everyone knows how to use them with the proper circumspection, discreetly and without superstition or excessive confidence (which should be attributed to no created thing). Therefore, we will publish none or very few of our findings on these matters. For this reason, let it suffice to have spoken briefly and generally about them now.

Indeed, I have also bestowed no small care upon **Spagyric** <note>Spagyric refers to the alchemical process of separating and recombining the constituents of plants or minerals to create medicines, often associated with Paracelsus.</note> preparations or **Pyronomic** <note>term: Pyronomy: The art of managing fire, specifically in alchemical or chemical experiments.</note> exercises—to mention this here in passing—since the materials they deal with are analogous to celestial bodies and their influences. For this reason, I am accustomed to calling it **Terrestrial Astronomy**. In learning and practicing this, from my twenty-third year of age, I have been no less occupied than with Celestial matters. I have hitherto experienced much in metals and minerals, as well as in gems, vegetables, and other growing things, and other materials belonging to this field, with much labor and no small expense. 

I shall not hesitate to confer frankly regarding these matters with Illustrious men and Princes, and other outstanding and learned persons who are interested in such things and have some knowledge of them. I will communicate some things to them as the occasion arises, provided I am certain of their goodwill and that they will keep these matters secret. For it is neither expedient nor right that such things become common knowledge. Nor is it granted to everyone—though many may profess such things—to carry out these mysteries in the proper manner according to the requirements of Nature, both harmlessly and usefully.

Lest the following pages remain empty, I decided to add three letters which two most excellent men wrote to me concerning the business of Astronomy. One of these is from the most Noble and Distinguished **Jacobus Curtius**, of best memory, the former Vice-Chancellor of the Empire. The remaining two are from **Giovanni Magini of Padua**, a most excellent mathematician and astronomer among the Italians. From these, one may see to some extent how much hope these men of prevailing and exceptional judgment conceived of me in this Art. Many more similar letters are kept by me. I thought only these should be added, lest I seem too excessive in these matters. 

->That I may one day satisfy the expectations<-
->of these and others concerning me, I strive with all my strength;<-
->and this is done by<-
->**GOD.**<-

<meta>This page transitions from Tycho Brahe’s first-person account of his scientific career to a series of letters from prominent contemporaries. This first letter is from Jacob Curtius, a high-ranking official in the Holy Roman Empire, written in 1590, providing external validation of Tycho’s work on comets and his astronomical instruments.</meta>

FOLLOWING IS THE TRANSCRIPTION OF THE LETTER

### Of the most distinguished and noble gentleman, Lord
# JACOB CURTIUS, FORMER VICE-
### CHANCELLOR OF THE EMPIRE,
->To<-
# TYCHO BRAHE IN DENMARK
### WRITTEN IN THE YEAR 1590.
## Heartfelt Greetings. <note>original: "S. P.", an abbreviation for "Salutem Plurimam," a standard formal greeting meaning "many greetings."</note>

**I** give you great thanks, noble as well as most learned man, because you have wished not only to address me in your letters, but also to offer me your kindness so lovingly. I certainly, although I have not seen your face, have for a long time loved and looked up to the better part of you—namely, your mind—from your writings, with which you have already consecrated yourself to eternity. For that reason, I have longed to see, honor, and embrace your whole self no less eagerly than those of old who set out from the farthest borders of Spain to Rome <note>original: "in urbem" (into the city). Curtius is referencing a famous anecdote by Pliny the Younger about a man traveling from Gades to see the historian Livy.</note> just to see Livy. This most kind address of yours has increased my desire, and increased it so much that, if the will of the Emperor <note>Emperor Rudolph II, whom Curtius served.</note> and the requirements of the public office I hold did not forbid it—so may God love me—neither the distance and hardship of the journey, nor the love and charms of my most sweet wife and dearest children would restrain me from seizing a companion for the journey, our mutual friend Thaddaeus Hageccius <note>Tadeáš Hájek (1525–1600), the Emperor's personal physician and a skilled astronomer who was a key link between Tycho and the Imperial court.</note>, and rushing out to join you all the way in Denmark. I cannot say how much it delights me to survey all of Hven <note>The island of Hven in the Øresund, where Tycho established his research center.</note> with Hageccius in my mind, since we cannot do so in body, and especially to embrace, if only in my imagination, your most precise observational instruments—indeed, that entire mathematical apparatus. But what if I were there as a spectator in person, and could see you yourself not only explaining everything, but putting it into practice? But because one may certainly wish for that, yet can hardly hope for it at this time (though I have not cast away all hope entirely), I will let that pass for now. Turning to your letter, I will respond briefly to its main points.

And this first: that you lovingly seek my judgment on your writings, and that you submit to my assessment that which is not so much a dispute as a friendly debate between you and certain most learned men. In this, I accept that you have granted me more honor than I would receive if Kings and Princes, disputing over entire realms and provinces, were to appoint me as their judge. However, my lack of learning—which in me is either non-existent or truly meager—and my modesty do not allow me to take up the task you ask and offer. Yet, so that I am not entirely deaf to such a friendly request, take this: what I think of your writings in general will be evidenced by the <term>Imperial Privilege</term> <note>A legal document providing copyright-like protection and official endorsement from the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> which I am sending to you attached to this letter. I was the one who advised the Emperor to write it in such a manner, and furthermore, I was the guarantor that the Emperor was testifying to the truth therein.

In particular, however, those things which you have handed down regarding the later comets <note>Specifically the Great Comet of 1577, which Tycho proved was further away than the moon, challenging the Aristotelian view that comets were atmospheric phenomena.</note>—as many as you yourself have observed—and which you have confirmed with the most solid reasons, I think are such that they can no longer even be doubted by learned men. Therefore, I cannot be brought to believe that those who still show themselves doubtful in this area, and who wish to seem to patronize the authority of Aristotle against demonstrated truth, truly feel that way. Instead, I think they are doing this so that, once they have turned every stone and searched everywhere for everything that might seem to confirm Aristotle’s opinion or weaken yours (or render it doubtful), they may propose these things to you as if to be tested against a Lydian stone <note>A "touchstone" used to test the purity of gold; here meaning a rigorous intellectual test.</note>. Thus, they provide you the occasion to use the sun of your learning to dissipate not only all shadows of error, but even the lightest little clouds, and to perfect the most precise doctrine of these matters for posterity. If they contend with you with this mind, truly I...

<summary>This page contains a 1590 letter from Jacob Curtius, the Imperial Vice-Chancellor, to Tycho Brahe, praising Tycho's intellect and his revolutionary work on comets. Curtius expresses his deep admiration for Tycho's instruments on the island of Hven and mentions his role in securing an Imperial Privilege (a form of official endorsement) for Tycho's publications.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Curtius, Tycho Brahe, Thaddaeus Hageccius, Hven, astronomy, comets, Aristotle, Imperial Privilege, Rudolph II, scientific instruments</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the letter from Jacob Curtius to Tycho Brahe begun on the previous page. Curtius is discussing Tycho’s ongoing conflict with rivals, his cosmological theories, and the design of astronomical instruments.</meta>

I wish those men as well as possible; yet if they are occupied with anything else, I certainly consider their persistence worthy of hatred. However, I embrace that which they have extorted from you by their own persistence for the common benefit—ordered as it was to be valid, or rather not to be valid—no differently than if they had done it with a good intention; and I congratulate the republic of letters <note>A term for the international community of scholars and intellectuals.</note> for that same thing.

Regarding the earlier Comets, which Regiomontanus <note>Johannes Müller von Königsberg (1436–1476), an influential German mathematician and astronomer.</note> and others observed, I await what you are about to publish any day now; until then, I keep my own opinion suspended as to whether all Comets generally are to be believed as ethereal <note>Existing in the "heavens" above the moon, which challenged the traditional Aristotelian view that comets were atmospheric phenomena.</note>, or whether some are truly elemental.

To those things which have been objected so far against your new hypotheses <note>Tycho’s "Tychonic System," which placed the Earth at the center but had the planets orbit the Sun.</note>, you seem to me to have responded solidly, and I find nothing absurd in them, but rather everything beautifully consistent with one another. Whether these hypotheses will provide that which is desired in the Copernican system—namely, that they show us the exact locations of the Stars for past, present, and future times—must only be judged when that "Great Work" <note>Tycho's massive projected astronomical treatise, which he worked on for decades.</note> you are laboring upon is brought to light. For I do not doubt that in that work, you will have compared with the greatest diligence the more exact observations of all existing ages with your own (which I am certain are most exact), and from there you will have constructed this new **Machine of the World** with truly divine genius.

Take heart then, most noble man, and see to it that we may enjoy your divine discoveries as quickly as possible. For although it is an arduous task, and as you truly write yourself, a work of many years, I nevertheless hope, indeed I trust, that you have already exhausted the greatest part of it, and are occupied at this time more with collecting and arranging those things you have already established and constructing tables from them, than in further confirming your discoveries. In which matter, since you can be helped in great part by the labor of your disciples, I wish to ask you again and again not to keep us in suspense for too long. I wish you, from my heart, a life as long as Nestor’s <note>A legendary king in Greek mythology known for his great age and wisdom.</note>; yet you know how deceptive our hopes are, and how premature a death often befalls especially divine geniuses. Therefore, avoid delays, and even if it is not a "mature birth," give this offspring of your genius to the world. If the fates grant you a longer life, as we greedily hope and pray for, you will be able to refine what has been published, and finally satisfy your own judgment (for I have no doubt that you will satisfy ours in the first edition). In the meantime, you will free us from the constant fear that, while you hesitate too much, we might be deprived of the entire work. I certainly have conceived such great hope for you and these hypotheses of yours, that I would dare to affirm for certain that a solid rebuilding of the Astronomical art must be expected from you or from no one. Indeed, there is no one in Europe (and when I say Europe, I mean the whole World) who can be compared to you, who is equipped with so much and such great divine power of genius for these necessary things, or who has contributed so much expense, labor, and so many years. So may God love me, there is no flattery here, from which both the dignity I hold and the gravity I strive to maintain among all people will sufficiently defend me before those who know me and those who do not, especially in praising a name that is not unknown to me.

Those things you have written in various places regarding the exact construction and use of the instruments necessary for observations have delighted me exceedingly. Since I have so far been unable to satisfy myself—as I do not allow myself even to see, much less possess, things that are not exact—I am entirely without any instruments, except for those acquired only for pleasure. The **Nonnian Quadrant** <note>An instrument design by Pedro Nunes intended to allow for very precise measurements of star altitudes.</note> once kept me very anxious; but because it is most difficult, indeed almost impossible, to complete it, I thought of other means, and found various ones, a part of which the distinguished mathematician **Christopher Clavius** <note>A Jesuit astronomer who was the primary architect of the Gregorian calendar.</note> published in his little book on solar clocks by means of a descriptive instrument. Finally, there came to my mind the construction of a certain quadrant which, because it seemed not inelegant to certain learned men, I wanted to send to you as well. However, to confess frankly the reality of the matter, as you most truly affirm: all these inventions have less in reserve than they promise at first glance. Nevertheless, it will be pleasing to me to hear your judgment on this latest invention of mine. From the little book of your Plagiarist, N. N. <note>original: "Plagiarij tui." Likely a reference to Nicolai Reymers Ursus, whom Tycho accused of stealing his planetary system.</note>, which he titled *The Astronomical Foundation*, and from its single diagram—

<summary>Jacob Curtius encourages Tycho Brahe to publish his "Great Work" on astronomy without delay, praising his new world system and discussing the technical challenges of creating precise astronomical instruments.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a letter from Jacob Curtius to Tycho Brahe dated June 28, 1590. Curtius is wrapping up his personal correspondence before providing a technical description of a new method for dividing an astronomical quadrant.</meta>

...system, which he dedicated to Paul Wittich <note>Paul Wittich (ca. 1546–1586) was a mathematician and astronomer who worked with both Tycho Brahe and Curtius; he is credited with developing 'prosthaphaeresis,' a method to simplify complex calculations.</note>, I myself have constructed in recent days—since I was unable to attend to public business due to poor health—a new doctrine of **spherical triangles**, in which, through a table of sines, tangents, and secants, all cases of both right-angled and oblique-angled triangles are most easily completed without any multiplication or division, through addition and subtraction alone <note>This refers to the method of <term>prosthaphaeresis</term>, which used trigonometric identities to turn multiplication into addition, a precursor to the invention of logarithms.</note>. I would also send it to you, were I not aware that you would easily grasp the whole matter simply by inspecting this diagram. For from that diagram and the axiom already demonstrated by many—that the radius is the mean proportional between the right sine of the arc and the secant of the complement—that entire method has been constructed. For the rest, I pray again and again, most noble man, that you may be well for many years; and I most affectionately offer you all the services of love and goodwill that can proceed from me to you, earnestly asking that you continue to love me, who loves you from the heart, as you have already begun to do of your own accord. Given at Prague, June 28, 1590.

->Jacob Curtius von Senftenau<-

*But because the description of the **Quadrant** <note>An instrument used to measure the altitudes of celestial bodies, consisting of a 90-degree graduated arc.</note>, which he mentions in his letter, was attached, it pleases us to include it here as well: since it is indeed ingenious, and surpasses other such inventions produced by Nonius <note>Pedro Nunes (1502–1578), a Portuguese mathematician who invented a system for taking fine measurements on circular instruments.</note> as well as others. However, as Master Curtius himself candidly admits, such subtleties do not possess in their inner depths what they promise at first glance. In this, he agrees with what is asserted on this matter in the first part of the second volume of my *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho’s "Introductory Exercises" toward his new astronomy.</note>, page 461, regarding the same business. For besides the fact that these intricate and laborious subdivisions sometimes easily introduce some hidden error, there is also this disadvantage: that certain Quadrants, the closer they are to the center, the smaller they become. Therefore they are less capable of subdivisions, and if the Ruler itself does not exactly show a straight line everywhere it passes, and does not split a point in the middle (which is difficult to discern), this labor is in vain; not to mention other inconveniences. Therefore, our method—which takes place near the very edge and circumference of the Quadrant, and does not occupy much space, and can also be easily prepared—is far more efficient and certain. Since things that can consist of fewer requirements do not need more. Nevertheless, it was permitted to subjoin here this very ingenious method of Master Curtius, even if not sufficiently suitable for practice, so that his invention may be attributed to that most excellent man, and others (as often happens) may not claim it for themselves; and so that I may, in some way, recall his memory and the praise of his extraordinary genius with a grateful mind after his death. It is as follows:*

### THE DESIGNATION OF THE SUBDIVISIONS
### OF THE QUADRANT FOLLOWS.
### Nobly invented by the most distinguished and generous Master:
# CURTIUS, VICE-CHANCELLOR OF THE EMPIRE.

**W**ithin any quadrant divided most exactly into ninety degrees, let fifty-nine other quadrants be described [drawn inside it]. And in the one that immediately follows the outermost quadrant, let an arc of sixty-one degrees be taken, and let it be divided into sixty equal parts; or let an arc of thirty and a half degrees be taken and divided into thirty equal parts; and in either case, each of those parts will contain one degree and one minute. We use only the first of these parts, the rest being omitted as if they were not in the quadrant; and for that reason, that division must be made secretly <note>meaning lightly or as a construction line</note>, or as we would more approve, let that division be made in some other quadrant, and from it let one of those parts be transferred into this one which we have constructed for use, lest the parts to be described later be confused with the previous ones.

From the end of this first part, let its radius <note>original: <term>semidiameter</term></note> be transferred into the quadrant, and let the arc which it subtends be divided into sixty equal parts. Each of these parts will be one degree or sixty minutes; for the radius of any circle subtends a sixth part of the circle, that is, sixty degrees. Then, from the end of this arc, twenty-eight of these parts are transferred into the remainder of the quadrant, and they will be equal parts, of which each...

<keywords>spherical trigonometry, prosthaphaeresis, quadrant, Jacob Curtius, Tycho Brahe, astronomical instruments, geometry, Paul Wittich, Pedro Nunes</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of an astronomical instrument's construction, following the conclusion of Jacob Curtius’s letter to Tycho Brahe. Curtius is explaining a sophisticated method of nested graduations on a quadrant to allow for precise readings of minutes without needing a massive instrument.</meta>

<meta>catchword: OF</meta>

...we said the scales of the degrees contain eighty-eight parts. If you add that first part which we showed contains one degree and one minute, eighty-nine degrees and one minute will result. Therefore, the remaining part that is left over until the end of the quadrant will contain fifty-nine minutes.

In another quadrant <note>The author uses "quadrant" here to refer to the concentric graduated arcs drawn on the surface of the instrument.</note>, which immediately follows this one, let an arc of sixty-two degrees be taken and divided into sixty equal parts—or let an arc of thirty-one degrees be divided into thirty equal parts—and each of these parts will contain one degree and two minutes. Of these parts, we use only the first, ignoring the rest. From the end of this first part, let the <term>radius</term> <note>original: *semidiameter*</note> be transferred again into the quadrant, and let the arc it subtends be divided into sixty equal parts. Twenty-eight of these should be extended into the remainder of the quadrant, and we will again have eighty-eight whole degrees. If we add the first part (which contains one degree and two minutes) to these, an arc of eighty-nine degrees and two minutes will result, and that final part remaining until the end of the quadrant will be fifty-eight minutes. For the third quadrant, we will take from the beginning an arc of sixty-three degrees and divide it into sixty equal parts, or its half into thirty equal parts. Taking the first part and omitting the rest, it will contain one degree and three minutes. We will complete the rest no differently than was done in the first and second quadrants.

For the fourth quadrant, an arc of sixty-four degrees is to be taken; for the fifth, an arc of sixty-five degrees, and so one must proceed, always taking an arc one degree larger for the following quadrant, all the way to the fifty-ninth quadrant. For that one, an arc of one hundred and nineteen degrees must be assumed and divided, as before, into sixty equal parts—or an arc of fifty-nine and a half degrees must be divided into thirty equal parts—and each of these parts will contain one degree and fifty-nine minutes. From the end of this first part, omitting the rest, the radius must again be transferred into the quadrant, and the arc subtended by it divided into sixty equal parts. Twenty-eight of these are to be extended into the remainder of the quadrant, and we will again have eighty-eight whole degrees. Adding that first part, eighty-nine degrees and fifty-nine minutes will result, and therefore the remaining part left until the end of the quadrant will contain one minute. With these quadrants divided in this manner, let **0** be assigned to the first quadrant, which we divided into ninety equal parts. For into whichever part of that quadrant the <term>fiducial line</term> <note>original: *linea fiduciae*; the straight, reliable edge of the instrument's sighting arm used for taking readings.</note> falls, that arc will contain precisely whole degrees and nothing more. To the next quadrant following this one, let **1** be assigned. For into whichever part of this quadrant the fiducial line points, [it shows] one minute. To the following quadrant let **2** be assigned, and to the one immediately following it **3**, then **4**, and so we will proceed to the innermost quadrant, to which **59** must be assigned. Because into whichever parts of this innermost quadrant the fiducial line falls, that arc will contain fifty-nine minutes beyond the whole degrees.

This quadrant actually and effectively displays five thousand four hundred parts—namely, all the individual minutes <note>original: *prima scrupula*</note> that are contained in ninety degrees. The use of it is very easy. When the fiducial line or the <term>plumb line</term> <note>original: *filio perpendiculi*; a weighted string used to ensure the instrument is vertical or to mark a reading on the scale.</note> falls on some whole part of any of these quadrants, there should always be added to the whole degrees (which the fiducial line or plumb line shows) as many minutes as are assigned to that quadrant on the side, and the number of degrees and minutes contained in the cut arc will result. For example: let the fiducial line fall on the forty-fourth part of that quadrant <note>Original text has "qudarantis" for "quadrantis"</note> to which thirty-five minutes are assigned on both sides; thus the arc cut by the fiducial line will contain forty-four whole degrees and, in addition, thirty-five minutes.

<summary>This page describes a mathematical method for graduating an astronomical quadrant using 60 concentric arcs, allowing the user to read precise minutes (1/60th of a degree) by observing where the sighting line intersects specific nested scales. This system effectively expands the 90-degree scale into 5,400 distinct parts for high-precision celestial observation.</summary>
<keywords>astronomical quadrant, graduation, fiducial line, minutes, degrees, mathematical instruments, Tycho Brahe, Jacob Curtius, celestial observation</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the technical description of astronomical instruments by Jacob Curtius to a letter from the Italian astronomer Giovanni Antonio Magini to Tycho Brahe. Magini, a professor at Bologna, was one of the most prominent astronomers of the late 16th century.</meta>

TRANSCRIPT OF THE LETTER
Of the most Illustrious and Distinguished Mathematician Master.
H
<meta>Catchword: latum (broadly/extended)</meta>

# GIOVANNI ANTONIO MAGINI OF PADUA
## Bologna, in the same year 1590.

### TO
### TYCHO BRAHE IN DENMARK
### written.

->ILLUSTRIOUS SIR.<-

**A**s I received your most learned book, sent to me in your name by a certain Danish student who was formerly a member of your household, nothing more pleasing could have been offered to me by you; for I am accustomed to seek out writings of this kind most inquisitively and to read through them most willingly. Indeed, I had hardly hoped it would come into my hands by any other way, since few volumes printed in those parts are usually imported into Italy because of the distance of the journey and the difficulty of transport. To this is added that, by this most elegant gift, you clearly signified how much you value me: on which account I give you the greatest possible thanks. For that which I greatly desire and usually study—namely, that access to the favor of men like you might be open to me—you, through your singular kindness, have offered to me of your own accord. The reason I was not able to signify this my feeling toward you immediately by letter, as was fitting and as I desired, was that I had decided to send to you, along with my letter, a **Work** <note>Magini is likely referring to his *De Planeticis revolutionibus* or his work on the correction of the calendar.</note> recently labored over by me and soon to be published. However, last summer, having happened upon a messenger, I dealt diligently with that same student by writing letters to him, so that he might not find it a burden to ask from you in my name the things I subjoin here. I would, therefore, greatly desire to have a brief description of the fixed stars as they have been corrected by you, so that it might be of use to me in my **Ephemerides** <term>Ephemerides: tables giving the predicted positions of celestial bodies for every day of the year</term> which are soon to be brought back to the press; in addition to this, some notice of the Eccentricities of the Planets, and likewise the measurements of the sphere of each of them, if you have found them to differ from the Copernican values and have corrected them. If you do not disdain to gratify me in this, I will certainly see to it that you do not regret your kindness toward me: for it is my intention, with the integrity that becomes scholars and professors of the liberal arts, to praise you as the author of your findings in my writings.

Indeed, do not think I lack a feeling and judgment regarding that distinguished labor of yours concerning the **Comet seen in the year 1577.** <note>Tycho Brahe’s study of this comet proved that comets were celestial bodies further away than the moon, challenging the Aristotelian belief that they were atmospheric phenomena.</note> Know this, most Illustrious sir: since I have perceived your incredible diligence in that work and your accurate way of observing, I am persuaded that it will come to pass that you can most exactly correct the motions of the celestial bodies, and that you will most abundantly satisfy the study and expectation of everyone with the greatest fruit of that labor, from which everlasting glory may be joined to your name. I would desire, however, that you apply yourself especially to observing and examining the motion of **Mars**, since it is a common persuasion that it cannot be observed exactly; and I am of such a heresy that I think its eccentricity is changed and has its own period, so that another equation of eccentricity must be introduced for it. Otherwise, if a table is made for the maximum eccentricity, it will not be able to serve for the minimum, and vice versa. From this we see that **Copernicus** (although he detected that eccentricity of this kind varies) did not therefore construct tables which would suit individual times, since the equations of **Mars** computed for the eccentricity observed by **Copernicus** of 5 parts, 51 minutes, and 30 seconds can by no means be fitted to the times of **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose geocentric model was the standard until the Copernican revolution.</note> themselves, at which time he defined the eccentricity of **Mars** as 6 parts.

I cannot but greatly approve the **System of the Universe** <note>Magini is referring to the Tychonic System, where the Earth is at the center, the Moon and Sun revolve around the Earth, and the other planets revolve around the Sun.</note> devised by you, although I could wish that the **Orbit of the Sun** and that of **Mars** did not intersect at all. But if, as I learned from that student, it has been observed by you that Mars at opposition <note>original: *acronyctum* — refers to a planet rising at sunset, when it is closest to Earth</note> approaches closer to the Earth than the **Sun**, an intersection of this kind must be admitted altogether. I am in great expectation of your observations and speculations of this kind, which I shall not be at all ashamed to both approve and follow. And if, in constructing the **Ephemerides** and the resolved tables from the **Copernican method** and the **Prutenic tables** <note>The Prutenic Tables were astronomical tables based on the work of Nicolaus Copernicus, published in 1551.</note> not...

<summary>Giovanni Antonio Magini writes to Tycho Brahe praising his work on the 1577 comet and requesting updated data on fixed stars and planetary eccentricities for his own publications. Magini expresses support for Tycho's new world system despite its intersecting orbits and shares specific technical concerns regarding the varying eccentricity of Mars.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Antonio Magini, Tycho Brahe, 1577 Comet, Mars, Eccentricity, Tychonic System, Copernicus, Ptolemy, Ephemerides, Planetary Orbits</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the 1590 letter from the Italian astronomer Giovanni Antonio Magini to Tycho Brahe, following Magini’s expression of gratitude for receiving Tycho’s books. Here, Magini discusses technical exchanges of texts and his own upcoming lectures in Bologna.</meta>

I have not turned aside even by the breadth of a fingernail <note>original: "latum quidem unguem"; a Latin idiom meaning "not in the slightest degree."</note>. I also hope, which I vehemently desire, to read this year your first book on the *Phenomena of the Aetherial World* <note>original: "de mundi Ætherei phænomenis"; this refers to Tycho's work on the Great Comet of 1577, which proved comets were above the moon.</note>, which you promise to publish again. The student I have mentioned often signaled to me that you were searching for Werner's book <note>Johannes Werner (1468–1522), a German mathematician. His work "On the Motion of the Eighth Sphere" was crucial for studying the precession of the equinoxes.</note> on the motion of the eighth sphere <note>The "eighth sphere" in the geocentric model was the realm of the fixed stars, beyond the seven planetary spheres.</note>, which, although you sought it throughout all of Germany, you were never able to find. Therefore, I have given him one copy so that he might transmit it to you in my name. If there is anything else I can do for your sake, I shall consider it a great honor to be commanded by you, for whom I wish all things to happen fortunately and to be most well-consulted. This year I am to lecture publicly on my own hypotheses of the Celestial Orbs: which will be a good occasion for me to explain at much greater length (as I have already begun to do) those things I have published in this field, and to illustrate them with commentaries, geometric demonstrations, and calculations. However, before these commentaries are made public, I would gladly wish to know and understand your labors, so that I might also insert and explain your suppositions within them. And lest I be more troublesome to you by too much length, I will make an end of writing here, asking you again and again that you receive my respect for you with a benevolent mind and take it in good part. At Bologna, on the Ides of September, 1590 <note>September 13, 1590.</note>.

->To your illustrious lordship,<-

->Most devotedly,<-

->**Giovanni Antonio Magini of Padua.**<-

---

*Another letter by the same most learned Magini, prefixed to a certain book which he entitles the Tetragonic Table, dedicated to me. However, although it was published six years ago, I did not receive it as sent by Magini himself; but later, having been found by chance at a bookseller's, one of my students of Astronomy brought it to me.*

### To the Most Illustrious by Nobility and Most Excellent in Erudition
## TYCHO BRAHE, THE DANE, LORD of KNUDSTRUP,
### and founder of the Fortress of Uraniborg <note>Tycho's famous observatory on the island of Hven.</note> on the Island of the Danish Hellespont, Hven.

->**GIOVANNI ANTONIO MAGINI OF PADUA, MATHEMATICIAN,**<-
->**Sends heartiest greetings.**<-

<insert>D</insert>uring many years and for a long time, I have thought much (most noble and learned man) how I might find a prompt and easy way by which the square root <term>quadrata radix: the number that, when multiplied by itself, gives the original number.</term> of any number, however large, could be gathered at a moment's notice. It was a truly arduous and very difficult task, in which no one will plainly believe how much I have sweated, unless he himself has at some time made the attempt; and indeed the difficulty of the matter often deterred me from my purpose. But in truth, the matter at last succeeded so easily for me that I found more than I had hoped for: for what I have achieved in this kind of calculation, you first, most learned Brahe, and then all other cultivators of Mathematics into whose hands these things shall come, will rightly perceive. Moreover, I do not wish to appear too bold to students here in praising my own inventions, lest some carping critic <note>original: "momus"; referring to Momus, the Greek god of mockery and criticism.</note> should cavil that I am a less-than-modest herald of my own praise. Nevertheless, whatever kind of work this may be, I wished to dedicate it to you, a man of most glorious name and the most eminent astronomer of our age, for the sake of a grateful mind and singular respect toward you, and that for the greatest reasons. For first, you yourself are the one to whom I owe the most, because you opened the doors of your friendship to me so lovingly and kindly, and granted me more honor than my thin and very slight erudition (if there is any) demanded, when you sent me your second book on the *Recent Phenomena of the World* <note>original: "de recentiorib. mundi Phænomenis"; this 1588 book contained Tycho's system of the world where planets circle the sun, and the sun circles the earth.</note> by a certain Danish student, and at the same time asked through him what I felt about them, and also because you responded most kindly to my letter, discussing many things with me concerning astronomical matters. Then, you are the one to whom I am most confident this my Tetragonic Table <note>A table of squares used to simplify complex multiplications and divisions in astronomical calculations.</note> will be welcome, because you will be able to use it most successfully to elicit the angles of the inequalities of the planetary motions, and indeed by a more ready shortcut than through the tables of Tangents and Secants. Finally, because you are that most excellent restorer of astronomy, to whom our age owes the most, and all posterity will owe, since not I alone, but almost all men of sincere mind have conceived that hope from your astronomical foundations: that from you alone the restoration of astronomy—

->—tion<- <note>This catchword "tion" completes the word "restitution" (restoration) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page contains the conclusion of a 1590 letter from Magini to Tycho Brahe and the beginning of a second, older letter accompanying Magini's "Tetragonic Table." Magini expresses his deep admiration for Tycho’s "restoration" of astronomy and discusses his own mathematical shortcuts for calculating planetary positions.</summary>

<lang>English</lang>
<sig>G 3</sig>
<meta>This page continues a 1592 letter from Giovanni Antonio Magini to Tycho Brahe. Magini is discussing his excitement for Tycho's upcoming publications and his own astronomical work in Italy using instruments based on Tycho's designs.</meta>

...illustration <note>The text begins mid-word, completing "illustrationem" from the previous page.</note> they eagerly expect. For you have fashioned such <term>hypotyposes<note>original: "hypotyposes"; these are preliminary models or structural sketches of the celestial system.</note></term> of the celestial spheres with such wondrous skill and invention that there can be no doubt they are fully sufficient to safeguard the phenomena and appearances observed in the heavens, and to define the locations of the celestial lights at any given time. I therefore urge you, and indeed entreat you, by this small gift I have offered, that you finally complete what you have so happily begun and have labored upon for so long in this branch of teaching. For I judge rightly and sincerely—and everyone hopes for the same—that no one else can perform this more correctly or successfully than you, who were born with the sharpest talent for the clarification of "collapsed" astronomy <note>original: "Astronomiæ collapsæ"; Magini refers to the state of astronomy before Tycho’s precision measurements corrected centuries of accumulated errors.</note>. Indeed, if you will communicate your most laborious and excellent *Theatrum* <note>Likely referring to Tycho's planned comprehensive work on astronomy.</note> to us as soon as it is finished, you will surely snatch all glory from those who attempted the correction of the motions in the past, and those who attempt it now; and for those who would attempt it in the future, you will forestall every occasion and material.

I know indeed that after the publication of your labors, my own writings will require correction; but so far from this disturbing me, I rather desire your speculations to be made public as soon as possible. For I shall immediately correct my <term>Ephemerides<note>Tables predicting the daily positions of stars and planets.</note></term>, the second edition of which is eagerly expected, or, if there be need, I shall weave others anew. Let me also signify to you through this letter that I have thus far omitted the investigation of the pole at Padua and Bologna, as well as the meridian altitudes of Mars and those fixed stars which you so greatly desired me to observe. Yet I would not wish to be accused of negligence by you on that account, since the power to perform this was snatched from me by various waves of business which overwhelmed me, added to the inconvenience of the location, especially at Padua, to which this same messenger can testify for you. I have perceived that the astronomical sextant, which I had constructed at Padua according to your invention and for your sake, corresponds most exactly to the heavens down to the individual minutes, since I detected the distances of several fixed stars to be precisely <note>original: "ad unguem"; literally "to the fingernail," a Latin idiom for perfect precision.</note> the same as those you noted in your catalog from your own observations. And I have now transported this with me to Bologna, where I shall attempt to complete many observations for your benefit. Furthermore, I do not wish this to be hidden from you: that the most noble and learned man Paolo Bonfiglio, a Bolognese patrician and a great devotee of yours, has seen to the construction at his own expense of a ten-foot <term>Quadrant<note>An instrument used to measure angles up to 90 degrees, vital for determining the altitude of celestial bodies.</note></term> made of wood and metal, which will show in its observations not just minutes, but the parts of minutes. This same most noble man also strives to have a sextant of the same size. With these instruments, in the very high tower which he has in his house, we may more conveniently perform celestial observations for your sake; you shall be informed of all these things in their proper place and time. Here I shall bring an end to this letter, earnestly requesting that if I am able to do anything, you use me as is your right. Farewell, most distinguished man, the glory of astronomers; proceed to perfect astronomy by your nightly studies and vigils, and, as they say, "adorn the Sparta which you have inherited." <note>original: "Spartam, quam accepisti orna"; a classical proverb meaning one should do honor to the position or task one has been assigned.</note> And again, long and happily, 

->farewell.<-

->Bologna, on the Calends of February [February 1st], 1592.<-

<meta>This page transitions from Giovanni Antonio Magini's personal correspondence to a collection of encomiums (poems of praise) and academic news from Italy. It highlights Tycho Brahe's international reputation in 1592, specifically within the scholarly circles of Padua and Venice, and mentions the arrival of Galileo Galilei at the University of Padua.</meta>

Appended to this letter were certain poems of distinguished men among the Italians; among which space permits adding this one by the most excellent Andreas Chioccus. <note>Andreas Chioccus (Andrea Chiocco) was a prominent physician and philosopher from Verona.</note>

->To the most Noble and Learned man<-

# TYCHO BRAHE OF URANIBORG
## founder, and restorer of Astronomy,

<note>The poem below is presented in two columns in the original; transcribed here continuously.</note>

**W**HILE you, seized by celestial love, are carried around the rapid spheres of the Heavens and the wandering stars, and recount the various courses of the Sun and the labors <note>original: "labores"; a common poetic term for eclipses or the difficult paths of celestial bodies</note> of the moon, as well as the Throne of high Jove <note>Jupiter</note> and the realms of the Paphian Goddess <note>Venus</note>; then how the Cyllenian fire <note>Mercury</note> permeates into the opposing Orbs and joins the other lights to itself, and gladly shines with a shared brilliance; and how the cold stars of Saturn and the stars of bristling Mars wander as enemies through the ethereal recesses—you seem, great BRAHE, not only wise in mind to survey the flaming walls of the world <note>original: "flammantia moenia mundi"; a famous expression from the Roman poet Lucretius referring to the boundaries of the universe</note>, and to weave an extraordinary work of celestial reason upon your papers, but at the same time to have opened the heavenly dwellings with your body through the immense void. These dwellings, having been examined with much labor, you then reveal from your heart as noble doctrines, by which descendants in a later age may rise up. Hence you, an admirer of Olympus from your sublime watchtower <note>A reference to Uraniborg, Tycho's observatory on the island of Hven.</note>, kindle your immortal soul with love for the celestial Fatherland, and teach us to despise the lowly earth. Thus you are above even fate and its gifts, leading the life of the Gods; neither mortal deaths, nor griefs, nor the portents of a fleeting life touch you. Success to your courage, great BRAHE, you who are able to set in motion a work greater in such a pivotal moment than to cut through dense columns of troops with a victorious right hand, or to lead captive Kings beneath your lineage, and to have added to a great triumph the ample spoils of Barbarian robes gleaming with gold and purple, Mygdonian bows, quivers, and Lycian arrows, or to have defeated the enemy soldier at the Caucasian gates, or surely to have hemmed in the trembling foe at furthest Bactra. Devouring time scatters all famous deeds into the thin air, sipping away at them with the sleep of Lethe <note>The river of forgetfulness in mythology.</note>: only the arduous Virtue of the mind climbs high Olympus, and fears not the contagion of cruel Lachesis <note>The Fate who measures the thread of life.</note>.

->Andreas Chioccus of Verona.<-

->TO HIM<- <note>original: "ΕΙΣ ΑΥΤΟΝ"; Greek title for "To the same person."</note>

Brahe tunefully shows the well-established <note>original: "Εὐσαλέας"; literally "well-shaken" or "firmly set"</note> rules and the intricate turnings of the circles. Though the children of the Ancients labored before, no one yet has shown these things so clearly. If therefore it is lawful to speak any word of verse, having learned the best laws of the dwelling of the heavenly ones—or rather, if it is right for the gifts of the Immortals to come to those who alone are the true children of the works of the gods—then let the gods wish to draw the noble man, who has separated himself from earthly things, quickly into the dance of the stars.

->By the Same [Author].<-

---

*Because some space still remains, I have allowed the few things that follow to be added, at the request of the Printer, excerpted from the letters of a certain Doctor of Medicine staying in Padua, sent to a certain Danish student six years ago.*

**M**AGINI stayed here in PADUA and VENICE for almost the entire summer. For what reason, is not entirely clear. Meanwhile, GALILEO GALILEI of FLORENCE has obtained the Mathematics Professorship here, and he began his lectures on the seventh of December. The beginning was splendid, with a great crowd of listeners. He is liberally commended by Lord PINELLI <note>Gian Vincenzo Pinelli (1535–1601), a famous humanist and mentor to many scientists in Padua.</note>, who, if he were able, would very gladly introduce him into the friendship of Lord TYCHO. You, who know the mind of TYCHO, will be able to arrange in these matters what will be beneficial. MAGINI recently published a book, which he titled *The Tetragonic Table* (*Tabula Tetragonica*), under the patronage of TYCHO. He gave me a copy to send to you, which I shall transmit at the first opportunity. He also reported that the most Illustrious VENETIANS, in the Council of the Rogati <note>The Senate of Venice.</note>, deliberated on sending someone skilled in Mathematics to EGYPT with a salary of 500 Crowns, who might make observations there for TYCHO. For certainly, TYCHO is of such great celebrity here as no one else among those who are now living. Given at Padua, 28th of December, in the year 1592.

<summary>This page contains a celebratory Latin and Greek poem by Andreas Chioccus honoring Tycho Brahe's astronomical achievements, followed by a 1592 news report from Padua mentioning the appointment of Galileo Galilei and the high esteem in which Tycho was held by Italian scholars and the Venetian Senate.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Andreas Chioccus, Galileo Galilei, Giovanni Antonio Magini, Padua, Venice, Uraniborg, Gian Vincenzo Pinelli, 1592, History of Astronomy, Latin poetry</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the correspondence following the praise of Tycho Brahe, specifically addressing a letter from the Italian astronomer Giovanni Antonio Magini regarding the state of astronomy in Italy and a proposed scientific expedition to Egypt.</meta>

**F**ROM what I understand, therefore, from the aforementioned letters of that most distinguished **MAGINI**, that there are in Italy certain magnificent and excellent men who, being devoted to astronomical matters, believe they should not neglect to prepare **instruments** <note>original: "Organa"</note> also in imitation of ours, and to complete observations of the celestial bodies with these in a subtle manner, it is indeed most pleasant for me to hear. Nor do I doubt that extraordinary utility for the expansion of the **Art of Astronomy** can flow from this: provided that they live in a more direct sphere than we do <note>meaning they are closer to the equator, providing a different vantage point for observations</note>, are endowed with remarkable skill, and moreover possess the wealth to easily sustain these expenses. I would certainly wish that those things which they have so far discovered from the Heavens be shared with me in a timely manner. I believe this can now be done more conveniently, since I am staying in Germany, and at this time I am residing in a certain **RANTZAU** <note>referring to the castle of Heinrich Rantzau, a Danish nobleman and patron of Tycho Brahe</note> castle near **HAMBURG**, a famous trade center which also has much commerce with Italy. Or even if I should happen to be elsewhere in Germany hereafter, whatever it may be will easily find me.

Most especially, however, I would wish with whatever prayers and devoted mind I can, that what the most illustrious Magnates of **VENICE** (as I have perceived from the letters of which I just made some mention) had decided a few years ago with a heroic and liberal purpose, might even now be put into execution, if it has been interrupted until now by some intervening delays (as sometimes happens). This purpose was namely that some young man, intelligent and skilled in these matters, should be sent to the city of **EGYPT**, formerly called **ALEXANDRIA** and now called **CAIRO** <note>original: "ALKAÏram"</note>. There, he should above all observe the height of the pole <note>latitude</note> most accurately, both from the Sun when it is at the very Meridian, and from the stars likewise crossing the Meridian toward both the South and the North. He should use the motion of the Sun as corrected by us, and our verification of the fixed stars. This should be done especially for this reason: so that it can be established for certain whether the height of the pole nods <note>shifts or fluctuates</note> somewhat over a long stretch of time, or not; so that the individual horizons of the Earth do not always perceive the same height after many intervals of centuries. Some excellent mathematicians, even among the Italians themselves, suspect this for no slight reasons. Furthermore, it would allow those things which that greatest **COPERNICUS** ingeniously imagined regarding the intricate motion of the Earth's axis—in order to preserve the change in the obliquity of the **Ecliptic** <note>the apparent path of the sun through the stars</note>—to be examined in some way. Although (to say what the matter seems to require), even if the altitude of the pole above any horizon remained exactly the same forever, the hypothesis could still stand in this part, provided other things did not interfere. However, it is not our purpose to dispute subtly about these things now, nor to interpose my judgment, nor does the space allow it. 

I would judge that these things should be performed and tested especially in **ALEXANDRIA**, because it is most highly credible that **PTOLEMY**, the principal master in the **Astronomical Arts**, measured the latitude of the place in which he lived (or, what is the same thing, the height of the pole) most exactly long ago, namely about fifteen centuries ago. Thus, an error of even one or two minutes <note>original: "scrupuli"</note> would not be hidden, since he recorded it as 32 degrees lacking only two minutes <note>31° 58'</note>; which some of his predecessors also discovered. For the reports concerning other inclinations of the Pole or the Equator related by him in his **Geographies**, or even elsewhere by others, lack exact reliability; and for the most part, they are taken according to estimation in a broad manner. Just as with the fixed stars, which are seen and can be observed for the most part every year in any one horizon, that diligence which was required was not applied according to the dimension of longitude and latitude. Therefore, a much easier slip could occur in places on the Earth's globe that are not so open and favorable to observation in any one location. Nor is it credible that what the ancients produced regarding the search for the latitudes of certain principal cities from the proportion of the <term>Gnomon</term> <note>the part of a sundial that casts a shadow</note> and the shadow is perfectly correct, since the extremity of the shadow is not clearly precise enough, and the Gnomon ought to be very long,

<sig>H 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: long,</meta>

<summary>Tycho Brahe expresses his approval of Italian astronomers building instruments based on his designs and urges the Venetian authorities to fund an expedition to Egypt to measure the latitude of Alexandria. He aims to determine if the Earth's axial tilt or the latitude of locations changes over centuries, comparing modern data with the records of Ptolemy.</summary>

<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Giovanni Antonio Magini, Alexandria, Cairo, Rantzau, latitude, axial tilt, Ptolemy, Copernicus, gnomon, astronomical instruments, Venice</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues Tycho Brahe’s argument for a scientific expedition to Egypt, transitioning from general astronomical theory to the specific difficulties of historical measurements and the necessity of new data from Alexandria.</meta>

...show, and most exactly arranged to its own plane, such as it reveals in the very minute. I pass over the fact that the equinoctial moments <note>The exact times of the spring and autumn equinoxes, when day and night are equal.</note>, around which they practiced this scientific method for the most part, were not as precisely known to them as this operation requires. And since the Sun changes its **Declination** <term>the angular distance of a heavenly body north or south of the celestial equator</term> most rapidly around the equinoxes, it was easy to commit some deviation in this part, even if it was not entirely obvious to the senses of the eyes. Therefore, those things related by **PLINY** regarding the relationship of the shadow to the **Gnomon** <term>the upright rod of a sundial that casts the shadow</term> in the city of **ROME**, which he says is as 8 to 9 at the equinoxes, even if rightly weighed and the Sun's semi-diameter considered together, prove in a way that the inclination of the World’s Axis at **ROME** is the same now as it was in the time of **PLINY**—provided, of course, a comparison is made with those things read as having been explored there about a hundred years ago by that most outstanding mathematician **JOHANNES REGIOMONTANUS**. However, since a doubt <note>original: "scrupulus"</note> can still be raised as to whether those ancient **Gnomonic** commensurations were precisely taken, we are almost no more certain in these matters than before. I do not wish to imply now that the notation of **Regiomontanus** himself is unstable, to the degree that it is inconsistent with itself within four minutes. Indeed, even these things which he brought forward from the position of the Sun were not sufficiently established, and therefore, its **Declination** being doubtful, he brought them forth a little too boldly—to say nothing of **Refraction** and the **Parallax** of the Sun <note>Refraction is the bending of light by the atmosphere; Parallax is the difference in apparent position of an object viewed along two different lines of sight. Both are crucial for precise astronomy.</note>, two things which he seems to have neglected. Therefore, by no other means, as far as I can see, will it be possible to experience without doubt whether the Axis of the revolution of the Universe and the diurnal motion has altered itself at all with respect to a certain Horizon on Earth over the passing of several centuries, than if now, at **ALEXANDRIA** especially (as has been said), the elevation of the Pole were denoted as accurately as possible and compared with that ancient **Ptolemaic** observation.

Furthermore, not only would that newly instituted observation in **EGYPT** grant this convenience, but also many fixed Stars could be recorded there from the Heavens by one and the same labor, which never rise in our Northern Horizons, even unto **Canopus** <note>one of the brightest stars in the night sky, visible from Egypt but not from Tycho's observatory in Denmark</note> itself. In the Sun also, and the Moon, and the other Planets, many things could be explored there—both regarding refractions and other matters for the purpose of examining their patterns most subtly—through which the observations of the ancients living there could be limited, and simultaneously compared with those things which have been detected in these Northern places: namely, to what extent they agree with them or not. From this, many aids for the total restoration of **Astronomy** would proceed, all of which would be too long to enumerate one by one.

You, therefore, most Illustrious and Powerful **VENETIANS**, if you do not find it burdensome to apply your exceptional and most praiseworthy care here—as I was previously informed of your noble intention—you will surely have done a thing not only useful and long desired in vain, but also most worthy of memory for all posterity and for your renowned praise, which otherwise shines far and wide.

I, indeed, as far as is in my power, shall in no way fail in my duty toward you, whether by arranging **Instruments** <note>original: "In-menta," likely "Instrumenta" with a printing error.</note> and means, or by prescribing those things which must be done; which I always reverently

->offer to you with all compliance: although I do not deny that there are found in **Italy**, and even in your<-
->renowned city, those who could perform these very things, and others pertaining to this,<-
->far more expeditiously than I. For neither<-
->will **Virgils** be lacking, where there are<-
->**Maecenates**.<-
<note>This is a classical allusion: "Maronies" (Virgil) refers to great artists or scientists, while "Maecenates" refers to wealthy patrons. Tycho is saying that if the Venetians provide the funding (patronage), great scientific work will follow.</note>

<summary>Tycho Brahe argues that only a new expedition to Alexandria can determine if the Earth's axis has shifted since antiquity. He urges the Venetian authorities to fund this mission, offering his technical expertise to help restore the accuracy of astronomy.</summary>
<keywords>Alexandria, Venice, Gnomon, Johannes Regiomontanus, Pliny, axial tilt, Canopus, patronage, astronomical instruments, refraction</keywords>
