<margin>APPENDIX ON ARCHITECTURAL STRUCTURES SUITED TO ASTRONOMICAL Observations.</margin>
<meta>Catchword: BUILDINGS-</meta>

# APPENDIX
## ON ARCHITECTURAL STRUCTURES SUITED TO ASTRONOMICAL
### observations.

**T**he descriptions of the Astronomical instruments have been set forth in this manner (as was seen above) and they treat—more than sufficiently—the mechanical part of the Art, which ought to be the very first of all, and without which the rest are attempted in vain. They suffice for all kinds of uses with a variety and abundance that is not only pleasing but also useful and nearly necessary. But if these instruments <note>original: "Organa"</note> are to be applied fitly and without inconvenience to their practice and the office for which they are intended, there is required not only a suitable place, but also opportune buildings, in which it is permitted to administer this business <term>pragmateia: a systematic or professional study</term> without any difficulty or disturbances. Indeed, let the PLACE be primarily elevated, from where it is granted to look freely at the Horizon in every direction, with no woods or mountains or other buildings obstructing the view. It is also conducive for the same place to be solitary and removed from the noise of the common people, so that one may enjoy **philosophical leisure**; yet, in such a way that necessities are at hand, and access is occasionally open to the Learned and intelligent, while the promiscuous crowd <note>Brahe often expressed a desire for privacy from those he deemed uneducated curiosity-seekers.</note> is kept away—those who neither understand such things nor hold them in the value they deserve. 

And although places which exist in the Southern region of the earth might seem preferable to those which incline toward the Poles (which are more northern in this Arctic position) on account of a more upright orientation of the **Sphere** <term>the Celestial Sphere; the closer one is to the Equator, the more vertically the stars appear to rise and set</term> and perhaps more frequent clear skies, nevertheless, since it is not convenient for everyone to change their Fatherland or the place in which they live for that reason—nor perhaps is that desired condition and convenience granted in any Southern parts of the Earth, especially those that are unknown and foreign—it will certainly be necessary to be content with the opportunity of place and time that is offered and at hand. One must overcome difficulties and inconveniences, if any occur, with constant diligence, lest the same thing happen which **SENECA** truly says of time: *We have too little, but we waste much.* 

In **EGYPT** indeed, and in other places of **AFRICA** and **ASIA** where the ancient Astronomers lived, it would be granted for these things to be done more readily on account of the sky being (as they say) more clear and the Sphere less inclined. But since **Bellona** <note>The Roman goddess of war; here referring to the military conflicts of the Ottoman Empire.</note> has expelled nearly all the liberal arts there through the Turkish Empire, it is likely that Astronomy is an exile there, or held in less value (except insofar as it is perhaps still practiced in some way by the **Arabs**). Concerning other parts of **EUROPE** which incline toward the South, better things would certainly be hoped for, provided they do not obey the **Turk** or the **Muscovite**—since they do not turn away from the noble arts there. But since those kingdoms are for the most part occupied with other matters and care less for Astronomical things (as far as is known), he who prefers those parts—even if more Southern—to those which incline somewhat toward the North seems likely to find a loss rather than a gain. 

Indeed, since observations are performed more conveniently and fully in the winter time when the nights are longer, the Northern region has this prerogative: not only does it provide this, but moreover, the intensity of the frost (especially when the Northern winds are blowing) so purifies and thins the air that for many consecutive days it is most refined, so that the stars shine and sparkle at night to the greatest degree. In Southern regions at that time, however, it very frequently either rains or the sky is otherwise covered with clouds and damp, being less open to the consideration of the heavens. 

Indeed, that Prince of most praised memory, **WILLIAM OF HESSE**, Landgrave <note>William IV (1532–1592), a fellow astronomer and patron who corresponded extensively with Brahe.</note>, being exceedingly devoted to Astronomical matters and not lightly touched by them, wished that I might obtain my habitation in a more Southern land, so that I might watch over the Heavens more fully, as is clear from certain letters of his to me printed in the first volume of my *Astronomical Epistles* on Page 23. There is no doubt that he did this with the best intention, and perhaps also to lure me there. But I believe that no fewer observations of the stars can be obtained in **DENMARK** than in **HESSE** or any other part of **GERMANY** (to say nothing now of the other kingdoms of Europe)—except that some stars rise in more Southern regions which remain perpetually hidden in the North. This, however, is not of great importance, nor does it contribute much to the restoration of Astronomy. 

My twenty-one manuscript books can bear witness to this; they are in my possession and comprise the most accurate observations of as many years performed in **DENMARK**. I judge that such observations, and of such quality, have hardly been obtained by anyone else in the meantime (may there be no envy in the saying) even in a more southern region. In these books, the positions and changes of all the **Planets** were explored frequently and in many ways every single year—and not even **MERCURY** appeared more rarely despite its smaller departure <note>original: "digressionem" referring to elongation; Mercury is usually difficult to see because it stays close to the Sun.</note> from the Sun. Furthermore, the positions of the **Fixed Stars**, as many as are in any way open to sight, are denoted most subtly, and were later recorded in the canonical description. It is found that no one until now—not even among those who once inhabited Egypt and the South and the (as it is thought) more serene places of the earth—has provided such a thing, nor have any of their successors, wherever on earth they may live. That I put this forward not out of any arrogance or contempt for the ancients, but because the truth of the matter supports it, I shall (God willing) shortly make plain.

And let it be enough to have advised these things concerning the choice of a place (in which, however, I permit everyone to abound in his own judgment); the occasion itself will easily suggest more. And if all things cannot be had according to one's wish, the convenience must nevertheless be accepted in whatever way it is offered, so that something rather than nothing may be done.

<summary>Tycho Brahe argues that a successful observatory requires an elevated, quiet location and defends the quality of his Northern observations in Denmark, asserting they surpass recent Southern efforts despite the colder climate.</summary>
<keywords>observatory location, Denmark, William of Hesse, Northern vs Southern astronomy, atmospheric clarity, Mercury, fixed stars, scholarly solitude</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of astronomical infrastructure, moving from the general location (elevation and solitude) to the specific architectural requirements for housing instruments and the staff who operate them.</meta>

Furthermore, **BUILDINGS** must be constructed in such a way that they serve observations conveniently. They should have each instrument firmly, properly, and suitably arranged according to its size and form. These buildings must keep the instruments "in good repair" <note>original: 'sarta tecta', a Roman legal idiom meaning 'watertight and well-maintained'</note> and protected from the harm of the air, winds, and rain. At the same time, they should be constructed with coverings above the instruments of such a kind that, when the situation demands, they can be opened with little effort and closed again. 

Indeed, the material of the structures ought to be entirely of stone—whether of fired bricks or other quarried stone. Where necessary, for the sake of stability, the instruments should be supported by marble or other stone columns, and everything must be fixed in a firm foundation, so that they do not wobble in any way or gradually acquire any instability. The coverings, however, should be made of some lighter framework, so that they may be more easily opened and put back together. There must also be living quarters and heated rooms <term>vaporaria: literally 'steam-rooms', referring here to rooms heated by stoves or hypocausts to provide warmth during long nights of observation</term> for the observers who keep watch over such things. And both these and other requirements must be so elegantly arranged that no troubles, no difficulties, or inconveniences delay the work itself or make it tedious.

Anyone for whom funds are sufficient will be able, according to his own judgment, to devise and construct various forms of buildings related to this purpose, according to the opportunity of the place and the shape and number of the instruments <note>original: 'Organorum'</note>. Therefore, I think nothing specific should be prescribed to anyone in this matter. If, however, anyone wishes to know this system which we chose and completed in these architectural matters—exhaustively worked out with no small labor and expense on that island in the Danish strait, famous for its ships, **Venusia** (commonly called **Hven**), and begun twenty-two years ago—behold, we shall give that one as well. We do this so that it may be established in what places and with what forms of structures those instruments (whose configuration and explanation we previously exhibited) were arranged. From this, anyone may then draw what he wishes and adapt it to his own uses. For I hope there will be some in the future who are touched by such deep care, and who will strive with great 
->efforts to imitate or even surpass these things.<-

***

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the ideal physical conditions for an observatory, emphasizing structural stability, protection from weather, and the inclusion of living quarters. He introduces his own observatory on the island of Hven as a prime example for future astronomers to emulate.</summary>

# THE CASTLE OF URANIBORG
## WITH REGARD TO ITS ENTIRE
### LAYOUT.

<note>This page presents a detailed architectural and landscape plan for Uraniborg ("The Castle of the Heavens"), Tycho Brahe's famous observatory on the island of Hven. Built between 1576 and 1580, it was the first building in modern Europe designed specifically for astronomical research. The plan shows the facility as a "total work of art," combining science, residence, and symbolic garden design.</note>

->**A PLAN OF THE CASTLE OF URANIBORG**<-

<note>Uraniborg was designed with strict geometric precision. The outer wall is a perfect square, oriented precisely to the four cardinal points of the compass. Within this square, the gardens are divided into four quadrants containing orchards and herbal gardens, reflecting the Renaissance ideal of man imposing order upon nature. The circular structures (marked M) are the *Stjerneborg* or "Star Castle," an underground addition built later to protect instruments from the vibrations caused by the wind.</note>

# EXPLANATION OF THE DELINEATION OF THE CASTLE OF URANIBORG,
## REGARDING ITS ENTIRE LAYOUT.

FREDERICK THE SECOND of most praiseworthy memory, King of Denmark and Norway, etc., yielding to no one in virtue, magnanimity, and integrity, who passed away ten years ago to the great grief not only of Denmark and his subjects but also of the neighboring regions, in the fifty-fourth year of his age—certainly most worthy of a longer life, had it so seemed fit to GOD: He (I say), that best of Kings, since besides other heroic gifts he was of a truly liberal and inclined mind toward noble studies, and understood that Astronomy holds the principal place among these, having always been held in the highest esteem by the greatest monarchs and kings—and that many of them had been laudably engaged in it and had attained no small degree of perpetual fame therefrom—he deemed it worthwhile that this science should be illustriously cultivated and fostered in his own Kingdom as well. Therefore, when he perceived that I was devoting my efforts to its restoration, and had already been engaged in that arena for many years since my youth; so much so that for the sake of continuing this work and bringing it to the desired end, I had resolved to leave my Fatherland for Germany, so that I might find leisure for it more quietly and opportunely, he encouraged me with kind benevolence, having summoned me to carry out the same work here in Denmark. By his Royal favor, he granted me the use of the Island of Hven, located in the famous Danish Sound <note>The Øresund</note>, commonly called Huenna <note>original: "Venuſia, communiter... Huenna appellata"</note>, so that I might apply myself to these Celestial studies there. I am accustomed to recall the mention and memory of the counsel of this never-sufficiently-praised King, since he has deserved exceptionally well not only of me, but of the entire field of Astronomy by this means. Therefore, it is fitting for all posterity, to whom (as I hope) the benefit of this thing will eventually reach, to celebrate the name of that most Serene and Best King with a grateful mind.

According to the decree and kind will of that most excellent King, when buildings suitable for astronomical business were to be constructed by me on the said island in a solid and magnificent manner, I chose a site on its highest elevation (since the whole island itself stands high), near its approximate center. There I began to construct the Castle from the foundations, named after Heaven itself <note>Urania was the Muse of Astronomy; 'Borg' means castle or fortress in Danish</note>, whose observations it was meant to serve, and for that reason called **Uraniborg**. Beginning in the year 1576, I completed it in successive stages. You see its layout represented here according to its entire form and capacity, as far as was possible to show on a flat plane. The particulars to be explained in this delineation are as follows, indicated by the letters marked in specific places:

**A.** The principal house, built in the very center of the entire area, its four sides forming an equal square and oriented exactly toward the four quarters of the world, so that the two small towers face South and North, while both gates look exquisitely toward the East and West.
**E.** The Eastern Gate, constructed in rustic and Tuscan stonework.
**D.** The Western Gate, fashioned in the same style. These two gates, however, had two large English Mastiffs <note>original: "Canes maiores Anglicanos"</note> as watchdogs in a suitable small shelter above, so that they might signal those approaching from any direction by their barking.
**B.** The Printing Office, which in a certain way reflects the design of the main house on a small scale. <note>Tycho operated his own press to maintain control over his scientific publications.</note>
**C.** A small house for assistants, also representing the main house in its gables and certain other features.
**F.** The inner face of the rampart.
**G.** Its outer form and arrangement.
**N.** Four paths leading toward both gates and both small houses, which run exactly toward the four quarters of the world.
**O.** Four garden gates.
**M.** Four gazebos <note>or "pavilions"</note> arranged in those semi-circular ramparts for the sake of pleasantness.
**L.** Herb and flower gardens.
**H.** Orchards containing various kinds of trees, about three hundred in number, etc.

And this is the description of the entire layout. The perimeter of the rampart has a square shape, interwoven with semi-circles in the middle, as is evident in the preceding diagram. Each side of the square contains 300 feet. The thickness of the rampart at its foundation is twenty feet; its height is 22 feet. The interior diameter of the semi-circular rampart is about ninety feet. The house itself, situated in the middle, which is also exactly square as I said, has about 60 feet on each side. The height of the wall is 45 feet. The round towers placed to the South and North have 22 feet in diameter, to which the external galleries attached add 10 feet. The porticos on the East and West of the square have 15 feet on each side below. The total height of the house from the surface of the earth up to the Pegasus <note>A weather vane or statue of the winged horse Pegasus at the highest point of the roof</note> at the top is 75 feet. The house also extends in every direction below ground, having subterranean buildings whose depth is 12 feet. Indeed, there are even certain other rooms below these. But since these are subterranean, they are not represented here, although they consist of many distinct areas arranged for various uses, which were completed at great expense—almost no less than the upper part of the house which stands above the level of the ground and is open to the sky.

I shall now add a fuller description and explanation of this principal house situated in the center, so that everything may be seen more clearly; and there I will exhibit, as far as possible and as much as is allowed on a flat plane, some of the subterranean and hidden features. For to recount everything finished there, not only below but also outside—though it would not be easily believed that this house could contain such and so many various things—would be both too lengthy and perhaps tedious to some.

->*The house sacred to Urania, the renowned watchtower of Heaven,*<-
->*Founded in a high place and strengthened by a rampart,*<-
->*Surrounded by trees and herbs in your gardens,*<-
->*You who observed all the stars through three times seven years,*<-
->*While you raised your noble head toward Olympus,*<-
->*Do you now lie thus despised? Do you now rest thus bereaved?*<-
->*Perhaps it seemed so to the gods above, who care for divine things;*<-
->*Lest great gifts be confined by narrow enclosures,*<-
->*Thus do all earthly things turn and change their lot.*<-
->*Praise be to You alone, who turns the Heaven and the Stars.*<-

The text concludes with a melancholy poem lamenting the observatory's eventual abandonment.</summary>

<header>ORTHOGRAPHY</header>

# THE PRINCIPAL BUILDING OF THE CASTLE OF URANIBORG ON THE
# ISLAND OF THE DANISH SOUND, VENUSIA, COMMONLY CALLED HVEN,
# BUILT BY TYCHO BRAHE AROUND THE YEAR 1580 FOR THE SAKE OF 
# RESTORING ASTRONOMY.

---

## ORTHOGRAPHY
<note>In architectural terms, an orthography is a scale drawing showing the elevation or front view of a building.</note>

**THE PRINCIPAL BUILDING OF THE CASTLE OF URANIBORG ON THE**
**ISLAND OF THE DANISH SOUND, HVEN,** *built by* **TYCHO BRAHE** *around the year 1580 for the sake of restoring Astronomy.*

---

## THE GROUND PLAN AND ITS EXPLANATION
<note>The "Ichnography" is the floor plan or footprint of the building.</note>

**A.** The East Door. **C.** The West Door. **☉.** Four passages meeting at right angles, which however were later reduced to three so that the winter dining room or heated room **D.** might be enlarged, and so that in its corner behind the furnace there might be a certain small and secret spagyric <term>spagyric: a branch of alchemy focused on medicine and the separation/recombination of natural elements</term> laboratory. In this laboratory, there were five distinct furnaces which more readily served the pyronomic <note>original: "Pyronomico," referring to the scientific management of fire and heat</note> work at hand, so that it would not always be necessary to descend into the larger laboratory. **B.** A fountain that rotates a wheel, which threw water high up here and there whenever it was desired. **D.** That winter dining room. **E. F. G.** Rooms for guests. **L.** Steps for ascending to the upper floor. **H.** The kitchen. **K.** A brick-lined well 40 cubits <note>About 80 feet</note> deep, serving a hydraulic mechanism and distributing water through siphons <note>pipes</note> passing secretly through the walls here and there into the individual rooms, both upper and lower. **P.** Steps for descending into the chemical laboratory. **T.** The library. **VV.** The large brass globe <note>original: "Globus magnus Orchalcius"</note> shown in its place. **V.** Four tables for the students. **4.** Chimneys, both those ascending from the lower laboratory and those in the four corners of the rooms. **Y.** Beds in those same rooms, arranged here and there. The observer will easily discern other details through their own close attention. However, it must be understood that all these things are shown in a size that would fit the foundation of the larger house depicted above: although they are shown here in almost half the size for the sake of the space's constraints.

<header>EXPLANATION OF THE PARTS OF THE MAIN AND PRINCIPAL HOUSE.</header>

# EXPLANATION OF THE PARTS OF THE MAIN AND PRINCIPAL HOUSE.

I began to build this main and principal house of the castle of Uraniborg in the year 1576. Its first foundation stone was laid by the Magnificent Lord **CHARLES DANZAY**, a native of Aquitaine and for many years the illustrious Ambassador of the most Serene and Powerful King of the Gauls <note>France</note> to Denmark. He was a man second to none in integrity, virtue, and practical experience, and moreover, he was remarkably well-versed in the liberal arts. Since he had pursued me with a singular and constant affection for as long as he lived (for he died eight years ago at the age of eighty-one), he was always a great supporter of me and my endeavors. As soon as he learned that this house was to be built, he voluntarily offered to place the first cornerstone there. Soon after, he saw to it that the following words were inscribed upon a certain stone of porphyry:

## DURING THE REIGN OF FREDERICK II IN DENMARK.

->CHARLES DANZAY OF AQUITAINE, AMBASSADOR OF THE KING OF FRANCE IN DENMARK,<-
->PLACED THIS VOTIVE STONE AS A MEMORIAL<-
->AND A HAPPY OMEN FOR THIS HOUSE, BUILT<-
->BY THE NOBLE TYCHO BRAHE OF KNUDSTRUP<-
->BY DECREE OF THE KING FOR PHILOSOPHY<-
->AND ESPECIALLY FOR THE CONTEMPLATION<-
->OF THE STARS,<-
->IN THE YEAR 1576, ON THE SIXTH DAY BEFORE THE IDES OF AUGUST.<-
<note>Original Latin: "CAROLVS DANZAEVS AQVITANVS, R. G. I. D. L. DOMVI HVIC, PHILOSOPHIAE INPRIMISQVE ASTRORVM CONTEMPLATIONI REGIS DECRETO A NOBILI VIRO TYCHONE BRAHE DE KNVDSTRVP EXTRVCTA VOTIVVM HVNC LAPIDEM MEMORIÆ ET FELICIS AVSPICII ERGO P. ANNO CIƆ. IƆ. LXXVI. VI. ID. AVG VSTI." The date corresponds to August 8, 1576.</note>

When the day destined for the foundation arrived, the excellent Danzay approached, accompanied by several noblemen and some learned friends and guests to take part in the ceremony. On the eighth day of August, early in the morning at sunrise—with Jupiter near the "Heart of the Lion" <note>the star Regulus</note> in <term>♌</term> <note>Leo</note> and the Moon occupying the western horizon in <term>♒</term> <note>Aquarius</note>—he set the stone in the presence of us all. He first performed a solemn libation with various wines and prayed for every success, supported by the friends standing around. This stone was placed in the eastern corner of the house toward the South-East, as expressed by the letter **F** in the accompanying illustration. Afterward, the entire house began to be built and was finished in successive years, although not a few or minor difficulties and delays crept in during the process, of which there is no need to speak now. The explanation of the **Orthography** <note>elevation drawing</note> of the house assigned here is as follows:

**A.** The East Door, composed of Ionic and Doric work.
**B.** The winter dining room.
**F.** That foundation stone I mentioned, which the said French Ambassador placed.
**G.** and **H.** Windows of the subterranean cellar.
**C.** A chamber for guests. There are two similar ones toward the west.
**M.** The Museum and library.
**I.** The circular subterranean chemical laboratory, containing 16 different furnaces destined for the <term>Spagyric Art</term> <note>Alchemy, specifically the production of medicines</note>.
**L.** A hole through which coals are lowered for the <term>Pyronomic</term> <note>science of fire/heat management</note> works.
**Z.** A subterranean cellar for storing wood.
**q.** The kitchen.
**D.** The chamber called the Red Room, from **C.** The octagonal Yellow Room.
**E.** The Blue Room. To these three corresponds a larger summer dining room toward the west, colored green and decorated with paintings of the principal herbs in a botanical style, from which a great number of ships sailing past may be pleasantly viewed, especially in the summer.
**X.** Windows of the upper floor.
**O.** The Large Southern Observatory, containing several notable and large astronomical machines, especially the Azimuthal Semicircle, Ptolemaic Rules, and a Brass Sextant for taking altitudes. Also a medium-sized Brass Azimuthal Quadrant: these are represented and explained in their own places above, specifically under numbers 8, 9, 4, and 3, following the same order in which the instruments are listed here. This should also be understood in the following descriptions (where necessary).
**P.** A certain globe upon which an instrument is placed during the observation of the distances of stars, serving as a support so it can be rotated toward all quarters; such a globe is also depicted above with the Sextant, number 16. A similar one corresponds to this toward the west.
**Q.** An octagonal gallery in which the aforementioned globe is situated.
**N.** The Small Southern Observatory, containing the brass Equatorial Armillaries indicated by number 13.
**V V.** The descent into the laboratory and the ascent into the Observatory.
**R.** The Large Northern Observatory, also filled with several large instruments, such as the Rules or the Large Brass Parallactic instrument, which also shows azimuths on the wall's circumference, explained at number 10. There was also in the same tower a Sextant that provides distances using a single observer, and that Bipartite Arc, and that instrument which was once used for observing the New Star <note>the supernova of 1572</note>; these three were explained previously under numbers 17 and 15. Also preserved there was a certain wooden parallactic instrument once used by the great **Copernicus**, which was sent to me as a gift from Frombork <note>original: "VVarmiā"</note>, which I mentioned in its proper place.
**S.** The Small Northern Observatory, containing other Equatorial Armillaries, drawn at number 12.
**T.** Another globe similar to the first, serving as a support for placing sextants, to which a similar one corresponds toward the west.

In the upper part of the house itself, where the round windows are seen, there are eight rooms marked **p** for students. The highest Octagonal Chamber is located just below the spire of the house; from here there is an open view in all directions, with a surrounding walkway called a **Gallery** above the roof itself.
**yy.** Octagonal structures in which carved statues representing the four seasons of the year are seen.
**ββ.** Seven chimneys merging into one toward the south, and another seven toward the north, so that all the chimneys of the whole house meet in two distinct places.
**N.** The clock, whose bell hangs above.
**λ.** A gilded **Pegasus**, which serves as a weather vane; by means of a rotating pointer below the ceiling of the highest chamber, it shows from which direction the winds blow. There, another pointer on the same dial shows the hours.

Such is the exterior face of the house, whether viewed from the East or the West; and if one compares the Southern part with the Northern, one will find the same form, so that everything corresponds to each other and stands with proper symmetry, as is required in architectural works if they are to be crafted skillfully and in the proper manner. Furthermore, the subterranean parts, which are seen below with dotted lines, are to be understood thus: whatever is seen within the Southern Tower represents the chemical laboratory as far as possible; where **1.** is a round table situated in its center, upon which those things to be prepared are placed. The number **2.** represents the various types of furnaces arranged all around. There were 16 in number: three different kinds of baths <note>water baths for gentle heating</note>, one digestor in ashes, four large <term>athanors</term> <note>self-feeding furnaces for constant heat</note>, two small ones, two distillation furnaces in sand or ashes, one for a large vessel <note>original: "vesicâ"</note> adapted with double pipes. Another secret furnace with lamps, and two reverberatory furnaces, one of which reverberated in a straight line, the other through a helical line, both open and closed. The greater part of the furnaces were made of stones brought from Norway, which they call "Bergen stones," since these withstand the strongest fire and can be skillfully worked. But if every single thing in this laboratory were to be explained, it would take many words.

Those things marked below the main house itself indicate the subterranean cellars, storerooms, and larder. The doors through which entry is made here and there are marked **3.** The columns arranged as supports are marked **4.** Under the Northern Tower near number **5.** is seen that masonry well, 40 cubits deep, of which further mention is made later in the Ground Plan. At Number **6.** are masonry chests in which certain foodstuffs were preserved. The rest is partly expressed by the figure and partly to be understood from the context.

## LOCATED OUTSIDE THE CASTLE OF URANIA.

Stjerneborg, or "Star Castle," was Tycho Brahe's second observatory, built largely underground to provide greater stability and protection for his instruments.</note>

# EXPLANATION OF STJERNEBORG.

<margin>70.</margin>
Although the towers in the main residence of the Castle <note>Uraniborg</note> facing South and North, with their various wings extending here and there, were sufficient for the convenient arrangement of quite a few large instruments, I eventually saw to it that even more were constructed for specific reasons. These could not be properly contained within those spaces if each was to be used without obstructing another. Consequently, around the year 1584, on a certain hill outside the Castle toward the South, about 70 small paces away from the rampart, I had a certain subterranean observatory constructed. This consisted of various <term>Crypts</term> <note>vaulted underground chambers</note> built of solid masonry from bottom to top with no small labor and expense. I did this partly so that I could arrange some of the most important instruments safely and firmly, ensuring they would not be disturbed by any winds and would serve their purpose more conveniently. Partly, I did it so that I might separate my students when many were present, assigning some to the Castle itself and others to these crypts to perform observations, so they would not get in each other’s way or compare their findings before I wished them to. I named this observatory <term>Stjerneborg</term> <note>original: "Stiernburg"</note> in the Danish tongue, which sounds much the same to the Germans, and in Latin is called <term>Stellæburgum</term> <note>The Star Castle</note>. You see its form and <term>Elevation</term> <note>architectural view</note> along with its <term>Ground Plan</term> <note>original: "Ichnographia"</note> here. The explanation of these, briefly indicated, is as follows:

**A** is the Portal, composed in the Ionic style, through which one descends into the crypts of this observatory. Above it are three skillfully carved Crowned Lions, and on either side are <term>Porphyry stones</term> <note>a hard, purple-red igneous rock prized since antiquity</note>. These display appropriate inscriptions which were first included in the first book of my *Astronomical Letters* in the section concerning this cryptic observatory; it would be too long to repeat them all here. However, we shall soon provide the latter one, which is more general. **B** is the round ceiling/vault, beneath which is a <term>hypocaust</term> <note>a heated room; originally a Roman system of underfloor heating</note>, which can be entered from all sides via the crypts. **C** is the crypt for the larger <term>Equatorial Armillaries</term> <note>instruments consisting of nested rings used to determine the coordinates of celestial bodies</note> shown in number 14. **D** is the crypt for the large "revolving" <term>Quadrant</term> expressed previously in number 6. **E** is the crypt for the <term>Zodiacal Armillaries</term> shown in number 11. **F** is the crypt for the large Geometric Square, which contains a steel Quadrant inside, marked as number 7. **G** is the crypt for the <term>Four-Cubit Sextant</term> <note>a sextant with a radius of about six feet</note> on its support and revolving globe, represented by number 16. These descriptions for the crypts themselves are briefly noted. Furthermore, the inscription I mentioned, located on the other side of the portal toward the South and inscribed in gold letters on a porphyry stone, is as follows:

->TO GOD, THE BEST AND GREATEST,<-
->AND SACRED TO POSTERITY.<-

>*Observing that Astronomy, the oldest and most excellent of all sciences, though long and greatly cultivated, was not yet firmly established or freed from errors, Tycho Brahe discovered and constructed various and precise instruments sufficient for every kind of observation of the celestial bodies. With incredible labor, diligence, and expense, he arranged these—partly in the nearby Castle of URANIBORG, built for the same purpose, and partly in these Crypts for more constant and convenient use. To you, O noble posterity, who shall follow in every age, he commends, dedicates, and consecrates this rarest and most precious treasure, so that you may preserve it perpetually for the glory of the eternal GOD, the Architect of the wondrous celestial machine, for the propagation of the divine art, and for the honor of the fatherland. Do not allow it to fall into ruin through age or any misfortune, nor be moved elsewhere, nor violated in any way: if for no other reason, at least out of reverence for the watchful eye of the Creator, the avenger of all things.*

>He who began and completed all these things on this island, having placed this monument, asks and implores you,

->TYCHO BRAHE, SON OF OTTO.<-
->HAIL TO YOU WHO READ AND DO THESE THINGS, AND FAREWELL.<-

Outside, in the area of this Stjerneborg enclosure, are stone columns **H** and **I**, arranged on both sides to the East and West, for mounting <term>Ptolemaic Rulers</term> <note>triquetrums; an ancient instrument for measuring altitudes of stars</note> or for supporting smaller portable Armillaries when required. **K, L, N,** and **O** are globes resting on their supports, sometimes placed in the said area so that <term>Sextants</term> may be leaned against them to aim at any position of the stars. **M** is a round stone table intended for the portable Quadrant and other smaller instruments to be placed upon it at will, so they may serve the observations conveniently. The observant and intelligent viewer will notice the rest for themselves.

# EXPLANATION OF THE GROUND PLAN.

**A** is the Vestibule through which one enters the observatory by steps. **B** is the square heated room. **C** is the crypt for the largest Equatorial Armillaries. **D** is for the Revolving Quadrant. **E** is for the Zodiacal Armillaries. **F** is for the large steel Quadrant which has a Geometric Square, also of steel, on its exterior. **G** is for the Four-Cubit Sextant placed upon its revolving globe. **H H** are stone columns placed on the western side. **I I** are other stone columns located toward the East. **K, L, N, T** are globes placed outside for supporting astronomical Sextants. **M** is a round stone table. **O** is a bed, in which I could occasionally rest during observations if, by chance, the clarity of the sky was not continuous due to the intervention of clouds. **Q** is another similar and larger bed for the same use by the Students. **P** is the furnace. **V** is a table. **S** is the beginning of an underground passage which I once intended to lead beneath the rampart and the garden into the Castle and the chemical laboratory <note>original: "laboratorium Pyronomicum," referring to Tycho's "fire-law" or alchemical work</note>. It was marked out, but not finished. The exterior area with its gallery measures seventy feet on each side of its square, facing the four quarters of the world. The diameter of the semicircle intercepted in the middle is twenty-four feet. In two corners of this square area, toward the <term>South-East</term> and <term>South-West</term> <note>original: "Notapelioten & Notolybicum"</note>, there are certain convenient cases. In these, that larger Semicircular instrument (used for taking the distances of stars beyond the capacity of a Quadrant, and whose drawing is not shown among the other instruments but is briefly noted after them), as well as other Sextants and similar mobile tools whose use is not always required, were kept safe and sheltered. Likewise, I had intended to arrange certain other things at the <term>North-East</term> and <term>North-West</term> <note>original: "Borrhapelioten & Borolybicum"</note> corners in due time. Let these things be said briefly about these cryptic structures. If I wished to explain everything individually, a lengthy description would be needed. As for the poems we assigned here and there in these same crypts in golden letters, the same first book of *Letters* displays them, so it is not worth the effort to recall them here.

Let it be enough to have indicated these things regarding the astronomical buildings. Although it was not my intention to include here the island in the famous Danish Sound <note>Hven</note> where we built these things, nevertheless—since I have mentioned the site being elevated and set apart, and since this very island has already been depicted in the fourth volume of the *Theatrum Urbium* <note>Theatre of Cities</note> by the famous Georg Braun, though not accurately enough due to the carelessness of the painter—I have therefore thought it right to present it here a little more exactly.

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes the purpose and layout of Stjerneborg, his specialized underground observatory, emphasizing its protection from wind and its role in isolating students. He provides a key for the architectural drawings and includes the text of a solemn dedication to posterity and the glory of God.</summary>

<keywords>Stjerneborg, Tycho Brahe, astronomical instruments, observatory design, equatorial armillary, sextant, Hven, scientific dedication, subterranean architecture</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific architectural details of the Stjerneborg observatory to a broader topographical description of the entire island of Hven. It provides the geographical context, the layout of the various supporting facilities (mills, workshops, ponds), and Tycho Brahe’s personal reflection on the costs and reasons for his eventual departure from the island.</meta>

# TOPOGRAPHY OF THE ISLAND OF VENUSIA, COMMONLY CALLED HVEN.

## EXPLANATION OF THIS TOPOGRAPHY.

<insert>Decorative initial C</insert>ONstructed within the most famous strait of the illustrious Kingdom of Denmark—which divides Scania <note>modern-day southern Sweden</note> from Zealand—this island looks toward the capital, Copenhagen <note>original: "Haffniam"</note>, to the south-southwest at a distance of three miles. To the west-northwest lies Elsinore <note>original: "Helsingorā"</note>, where the royal custom-house is located, at a distance of two miles (both of these are in Zealand). It is also two miles from Helsingborg, which lies exactly to the north, and a mere one mile from Landskrona to the southeast; these latter two are situated in Scania. The island itself is quite high, as if one were climbing a mountain, yet it is entirely flat on top. Its circumference measures 8,160 large paces. Near its center, where I built the castle <term>Uraniborg</term> <note>The Castle of the Heavens</note>, the elevation of the Pole—or, what is the same thing, the latitude from the Equator—is 55 degrees and 54 1/2 minutes, as we have frequently determined with the greatest accuracy. We have taken its longitude to be 36 degrees and 45 minutes, with due respect to the difference in meridians used by Ptolemy and Copernicus, and with as much diligence as possible. In this square diagram containing the outline of the island, these coordinates are subdivided into seconds of a single degree. The other features seen on the island are to be understood as follows:

**A.** The castle Uraniborg.
**B.** <term>Stjerneborg</term> <note>original: "Stellæburgū"; The Star Castle</note>.
**C.** The farmstead destined for the estate’s management.
**D.** The workshop for artisans, those who build astronomical instruments and other things.
**E.** A windmill.
**F.** A village containing about 40 dwellings for the peasants.
**G.** A mill serving for the manufacture of paper, while simultaneously grinding grain and preparing various skins; a single, very large wheel performs these three tasks either together or separately.
**H.** The church.
**I.** The judicial forum for the peasants.
**K. L. M.** Large fish ponds, of which **L**, assigned to the mill, is of profound depth and built with a dam of incredible size, so that it may serve as a reservoir for the abundance of water needed for the mill. These and the remaining ponds, about 60 in number, contain a great abundance of various kinds of fish; they are easily drained for the most part when needed for the use of the said mill.
**Q.** A marshy meadow, rising with several alder trees.
**T.** Pastures planted in some places with shrubs.
**N.** A small grove of hazel trees, shaded on the northern shore.
**P. Q. R. S.** The remains of four castles that once existed there.

When King Frederick II, of most blessed memory, granted me this island for life and confirmed it with letters on parchment so that I might carry out my astronomical work there with suitable leisure (as we have mentioned before), I expended vast labor and expense upon it. I did this to serve the will of that excellent King and the honor of my Fatherland, not only by constructing the splendid and solid buildings indicated here, but also by collecting water from many places (where nothing of the sort existed before) to establish a mill suitable for making paper there by the seashore. This mill was to supply paper for my printing press, also built there, so that paper would not have to be sought from Germany with great difficulty.

I EXPENDED on all these things more than a <term>ton of gold</term> <note>A "ton of gold" was a technical financial term in the 16th century representing 100,000 Rigsdalers, an enormous sum</note>; for whatever I could gather, whether from Royal grants or my own annual income, I spent for these purposes. I shall say nothing of the immense troubles and labors I sustained there for twenty-one years. From these things and others, any person of heart can easily judge that it was only for the gravest of reasons—especially at this age of fifty and with a large family—that I left such a constant island, and with it my sweetest Fatherland and the many kinsmen and friends joined to me by blood. What things, and how great they were, that drove me to this, I choose to remain silent about here. Meanwhile, I gladly hold excused my most Serene King, Christian IV, my Merciful Lord, who recently succeeded his father of most blessed memory, King Frederick, the founder and promoter of these things. For I do not doubt that if those matters concerning this work (which is perhaps not without honor for the Kingdom) had been proposed to him in a timely and sufficient manner, he—being excellently endowed with a heroic nature, a clear mind, a generous spirit, and an inclination toward the noble arts—would have mercifully and liberally preserved and perpetuated these studies, most worthy of Kings, in his realm. But perhaps it was so destined by the fates, so that the restoration of astronomy might be promulgated more widely and rendered more universal. Indeed, anyone will easily weigh for themselves how seriously and earnestly the restoration of astronomy sits at my heart, since for its sake I have been willing to bravely bear so many labors and expenses, so many disturbances and adversities—to the point that I did not even hesitate to leave my Fatherland and all that is most dear.

->SO GREAT IS OUR LOVE TO UNFOLD THE LAWS OF THE STARS.<-

<summary>This page describes the geography and infrastructure of Hven, detailing Tycho Brahe’s self-sufficient scientific community including mills, workshops, and ponds. It also reflects on his enormous financial investment and his eventual sorrowful departure from the island for the sake of his astronomical mission.</summary>
<keywords>Hven, Uraniborg, Stjerneborg, Frederick II, Christian IV, paper mill, astronomical instruments, Danish geography, exile, scientific funding</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the topographical description of the island of Hven to a technical supplement regarding the design and precision of Tycho Brahe's astronomical instruments. While the previous page established where the observations took place, this page explains the "how"—specifically the mathematical and mechanical innovations that allowed for unprecedented accuracy.</meta>

->SUPPLEMENT ON THE SUBDIVISION AND THE SIGHTS OF THE INSTRUMENTS.<-
<meta>catchword: the other</meta>

# SUPPLEMENT ON THE SUBDIVISION AND THE SIGHTS OF THE INSTRUMENTS.

Since in the previous sections, when I dealt with the Instruments, I had not expressed the <term>transversal subdivision</term> <note>A method of using diagonal lines to divide a scale into very small fractions</note> and the <term>dioptras</term> <note>Sighting devices used to align the instrument with a star</note> with figures—because they were not readily at hand so quickly—it is pleasing to add that very thing here, so that this business may be more rightly understood. Thus, the method of the division having transversal points is such as this figure expresses, in which each ten minutes are marked by small lines into ten equal spaces, distinctly noted with points. In this way, the <term>fiducial line</term> <note>The straight edge of the measuring arm used to read the scale</note> of the ruler, crossing any of these during an observation, brings forth the very minute of the degree that was sought, or some part of it, according as it is discerned to be removed from this or that point.

I learned this method at Leipzig in my youth, being suitable indeed for rectilinear Parallelograms, to which it properly belongs. However, I later applied it quite appropriately to the arcs on my Instruments, just as I hinted ten years ago in our book on the *Comet of the Year 1577*, near the bottom of page 461, with these words: *“For although its demonstration properly belongs to rectilinear Parallelograms, it is nonetheless conveniently applied even to curved lines in such a narrow interval—which differs imperceptibly from a straight line—without any trace of error.”*

In order that I may also add this demonstration here, perhaps on account of certain <term>smatterers</term> <note>original: "sciolos"; those who have a superficial knowledge and are prone to carping</note> who pluck at things they do not sufficiently grasp, let it be held thus: In the assigned figure, **A** is the center of the Instrument, and its semi-diameter is **A.O**. However, a small portion **O.I** is taken in which that division by transversal lines is made in the proportion which is 1 to 48, such as is used for the most part in my Instruments. And since **A.I** is set at 10,000,000,000 parts of the <term>Great Rheticus Canon</term> <note>The massive trigonometric tables published by Georg Joachim Rheticus, used for high-precision calculations</note>, the same **O.I** will be 208,333,333, as it is the forty-eighth part of the Radius. Let the arc **I.E** be 10 minutes and **I.V** be 10 minutes; their sine is 29,088,779 **Y.I**. However, the second sine <note>The versed sine or similar trigonometric value</note> of the same is 42,308 **V.Y**, which, added to **N.V** (which is equal to **O.I**), makes **N.Y** 208,375,641.

Therefore, in the triangle **N.Y.I**, right-angled at **Y**, two sides **N.Y** and **Y.I** are known; wherefore the Base **I.N** is given as 210,396,208, together with the angle **N.I.Y** 82 degrees, 3 minutes, 10 seconds, 47 thirds. When added to **Y.I.A** (89 degrees, 50 minutes), it completes **N.I.A** 171 degrees, 53 minutes, 10 seconds, 47 thirds. But let the Base **I.N** in the right-angled triangle **N.Y.I** be divided into ten equal parts, so that they correspond to one minute, 21,039,621, represented by **I.B**. Presently, in the oblique triangle **B.I.A**, two sides **I.B** and **I.A** (the Radius) are given, together with the angle **B.I.A**, which is the same as the angle **N.I.A** found before: 171 degrees, 53 minutes, 10 seconds, 47 thirds. Wherefore the angle **I.A.B** becomes known as 1 minute, 1 second, 7 thirds. This ought to be only 1 minute, so that it is larger by only a difference of 1 second and 7 thirds—an edge of difference that is truly insensible.

Similarly, if **F.I** is assumed to be nine small parts, they will be 189,316,587. And we shall again have the triangle **F.I.A**, in which two sides are given: **F.I** (just mentioned), together with the Radius **I.A** and the angle **F.I.A** comprised by them, as before. There arises the angle **I.A.F** 9 minutes, 1 second, 6 thirds, which ought to have been exactly 9 minutes, failing in the last minute **F.N** by 1 second and 6 thirds. Furthermore, so that the same thing may be attempted around the middle as we have now done at the extremities, there are found by the same method as before: first, the angle **I.A.H** 5 minutes, 3 seconds, 6 thirds (exceeding by 3 seconds, 6 thirds); second, the angle **N.A.H** 4 minutes, 56 minutes, 55 seconds (falling short by 3 seconds, 5 thirds).

It is clear, therefore, that the greatest difference, whether additive or subtractive, in this practical application comes to a mere fraction over 1/1000th of a minute. The sharpness of human sight can in no way discern this on any instrument whatsoever, and it is even idle in itself. Therefore, they seek a <term>knot in a bulrush</term> <note>A Latin idiom meaning to look for a difficulty where there is none</note> in vain, if any presume to cavil at this sufficiently accurate form of distribution of ours. Moreover, in certain especially larger Instruments where the Radius is longer, we are accustomed to carry out this subdivision by five minutes only, and therefore that difference of which we treat becomes even more insensible; this is what we proposed to show.

***

We have invented this most suitable method for the use of the <term>pinnacidia</term> <note>Sighting plates</note> or Dioptras: that four slits be made in the lower sighting plate (the one closest to the eye) around all its sides, which are precisely analogous to the upper one. These should be equidistant and correspond to its four sides with respect to the fiducial line, as you see represented in the attached figure (as far as could be done on a flat surface). There, **A, B, C, D** designate that front sighting plate very close to the eye of the observer; the other, **E, F, G, H**, which is distant, is at the circumference of the Instrument; **I** marks the ruler, to which these sighting plates are fitly attached at right angles. It will be necessary for the sighting plate **F, G, H, E** to be entirely equal to the other **B, C, D, A**.

Those small plates <note>original: "pinnulae"</note> which are applied to the lower one from three sides are rectilinear on the part where they are closest to it; they can be compressed or relaxed against the sighting plate itself by certain pins, so that they create slits on all sides that are equal, which can also be widened or narrowed as use requires. And that can be provided by a certain peculiar device from the other and inner part of the sighting plate, by a single screw turned around, so that all the slits are relaxed or constricted together equally and with a uniform movement, without labor or loss of time. The fourth slit, which is cut into that part of the sighting plate where it adheres to the ruler, remains thus perpetually; it is represented a little above **B.A**, and another slit equidistant from the plane of the ruler is seen in the upper sighting plate near **F.E**. Although that inner one, no less than the other three aforementioned, can also be appropriately diminished and increased by applying at least a small addition, as needed.

The **USE** is in taking the altitudes of the stars: the Ruler **I** is raised or depressed until the star is seen through the slit **D.A** on the side of the other sighting plate, **A.E**; and at the same instant, just as much of it is seen through the slit **B.C** near the other side **G.F**. For then there is no doubt that a central and exact <term>collimation</term> <note>Alignment of the sight with the star</note> of the star itself has been made. If, however, one desires to obtain the Azimuths <note>Horizontal bearings</note> at the same time, one must attend through another slit near **C.D** to the front side **G.H**, and together through the slit **G.H** at the other...

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes his mathematical "transversal" method for dividing degrees into minutes with extreme precision, proving the error is too small for the human eye to see. He also explains his improved sighting mechanism (dioptra) using adjustable slits and a screw-driven alignment system for accurate observations of stars.</summary>

<keywords>transversal subdivision, dioptra, precision instruments, Rheticus, astronomical measurement, sighting plates, Tycho Brahe, mathematical demonstration, error analysis</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical supplement regarding the design of Tycho Brahe's astronomical instruments, moving from the method of dividing scales with transversal points to the specific design of the sighting vanes (pinnacidia) used for stars and the Sun.</meta>

...the other [marked] F and E, and thus a very prompt observation is established for the stars. In the Sun, however, through the round hole of the upper <term>Pinnacidium</term> <note>A sighting vane or sight-rule used on astronomical instruments to align with a celestial body</note>, the rays let in from the interior part of the lower [vane] fill a certain circle designated there—according to the quantity of light which the Sun produces through that hole—providing what is sought. It should be noted moreover that in certain instruments, that sighting vane which is distant from the eye consists of a cylindrical shape, and thus the matter comes to the same result, except that for the Sun the shadow of the cylinder is to be observed; thus also in <term>armillary spheres</term> <note>Instruments consisting of a framework of rings representing the principal circles of the celestial sphere</note> we apply a certain smooth axis, so that <term>collimation</term> <note>The process of aligning the sight with the star or planet being measured</note> can be made all around it, for both cylinders and smooth axes have this particular advantage: they suffice not just for one but even for two observers. The figure itself, or rather the practice of such instruments, will teach the rest more fully.

Necessity itself taught me this method of observation through parallel slits on the other sighting vane: for stars are perceived with the greatest difficulty through holes in the manner otherwise commonly used—especially in the vane which is furthest from the eye—unless the hole is sufficiently large. And if this is allowed, some small fraction of a degree can be lost, since it is not known whether the alignment is perfectly centered. I truly wonder that this was not noticed by previous **Astronomers**, and this inconvenience provided for otherwise. When a certain distinguished mathematician visited me a few years ago, having come a long distance for the sake of seeing my instruments, and had inspected this way of marking the stars most conveniently through slits so arranged, he leaped for joy, asserting that he had now received the very thing he had breathed for in vain many years before; he congratulated himself that even for this reason alone he had not

->come to **Denmark** in vain. Coming afterwards to **Kassel**, he<-
->applied this to the **Landgrave's** instruments in some way, as also<-
->the method of division by **transversal points**,<-
->just as it is clear in the first volume of the<-
->*Astronomical Letters* from the letters exchanged between<-
->**Wilhelm, Landgrave** [of Hesse] of most praiseworthy memory,<-
->and his mathematician, and myself in its proper<-
->place. And let it suffice to have indicated<-
->these things thus, both concerning the<-
->sighting vanes and the<-
->form of the division.<-

<summary>Tycho Brahe describes his invention of improved sighting vanes using parallel slits for greater precision and recounts how these innovations were adopted by the Landgrave of Hesse-Kassel.</summary>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the technical description of astronomical instruments (sighting vanes and armillary spheres) to a celebratory Latin poem dedicated to Tycho Brahe. This poem serves as a laudatory preface or interlude, praising Brahe's scientific achievements and his patronage by King Frederick II of Denmark.</meta>

->To the truly Illustrious and Noble man<-

# TYCHO BRAHE, LORD
## of KNUDSTRUP, PRINCE OF ASTRONOMERS,
### An Ode in three meters and four stanzas.

---

Once, while the Roman soldier harried the swollen Getae <note>A Thracian tribe</note> with arms, or the Pontic sea, and even the Ganges, and carried his eagles and sharp spears against the Medes:

Wishing the powerful goddess <note>Victory</note> to stand in no place for long, he sought to bind the divine one with a shackle, lest she—visiting the wild Gelonians <note>A Scythian people</note> or the Parthians conspicuous for their arrows—

Might carry herself to the heights in insolent play, with the wheel of Rhamnusis <note>Nemesis, the goddess of retribution, often depicted with a wheel of fortune</note> turned to the bottom. Thus Victory, bound by an iron chain and deprived of her swift wings,

Once cherished the Athenian homes. Who does not know that Tyrian Jove stood bound by a shackle at the purple altars?

Envy, a wasting poison creeping through the innermost marrow, bringing everything to itself and nothing to another, begrudged that a celestial deity could be worshipped by all peoples.

But Denmark, the proud mistress of the Baltic sea, does not claim such fame for herself alone: she did not begrudge you, O TYCHO (though she could have held you with a more rightful bond),

To be given at last to the German shores. By making the name of your fatherland eternal, you, skillful one, have granted it to fly conspicuously through all nations:

For everyone, educated by your guidance, turns his eyes and mind through the wandering stars and the once-inextricable kingdoms of the sky:

And let him mark with a truer path and more certainty what terrifying Comets bring to the world: where the poles are placed, and how the fates vary:

Whether he inhabits the wide shore of Germany, or the coast of France, or the ancient Quadi <note>An early Germanic tribe</note>, Bohemia and the Marcomanni, or the kingdoms of either West <note>Possibly referring to the Spanish and Portuguese empires</note>:

Grateful, he will say he owes his votive songs to you; the youth everywhere devoted to the study of Urania <note>The Muse of Astronomy</note> and Mathematics <term>Mathesis</term> <note>At this time, "Mathesis" referred broadly to the mathematical sciences, including astronomy and astrology</note> will speak of you with an amiable song of praise.

For with you as leader, the free student of the Stagirite <note>Aristotle, who was born in Stagira</note> will judge that many things destined to live forever have been handed down to papers; yet he will also judge that many things were unknown.

Casting off the old cataract <note>Original: *glaucoma*, used here metaphorically for intellectual blindness</note> by which sluggishness protects itself in the decrees of the ancients, as if fascinated by authority.

Nay, even he whom the headlong desire for gain drives to commit a ship to the shipwrecking waves, and to seek the gold-bearing tracts of Guinea:

He whom it compels to go in Portuguese galleys to the ports of Calicut <note>Kozhikode, India</note> rich in spices, and into those of Quinzay <note>A historical name for Hangzhou, China</note> in China and the Japanese:

This man, while he scrutinizes the stars better by your guidance and runs through the sea with more certainty by a path unknown to the ancients, pays back his thanks to you,

Having spoken his deserved gratitude with a grateful murmur. Then he also honors Denmark as your nourisher, nay, your mother: he speaks of you, FREDERICK,

Shining with the long robe of Kings: he sings that you cherished in your kingdom (not the Tirynthian <note>Hercules</note> born of Jove, on whose neck both axes of the world once hung more firmly,

When the son of Iapetus <note>Atlas</note> released his weary neck to a substitute) but him who bridled the unobserved signs and perpetual orbits without a fixed law.

He who, carried above the fiery citadels by the gifts of his genius, saw in which circle Phoebus <note>The Sun</note> and Phoebe <note>The Moon</note> perform their signs.

How the earthly machine provides the center for both of them, and how Venus, following the Cyllenian star <note>Mercury</note>, circles the fire-breathing chariots [of the Sun] in a larger gyre.

How he moderates the orbit of Mars, the chariot of Jupiter, and the slow-moving old man <note>Saturn</note> with ethereal art, like a heart and center.

The Sun is the arbiter of the celestial spheres and king of the world. So that now neither the dogma of Copernicus, nor your cycles, O Ptolemy, may detain us.

There will be those who perhaps decorate the Danish kingdoms with Martian trophies, and are ready to honor the king with both counsel and hand:

But TYCHO, by the sharpness of a divine mind, has—by your art, O FREDERICK—boldly enclosed the long-exiled choirs of Urania and the stars called down from heaven within your kingdoms:

He has led the fame of your name and scepter above the mortal race of men through all the passages of the ethereal boundary.

Who could believe it possible? TYCHO gave to your people an earthly heaven (may it be allowed for me to explain this miracle with a new word); he equaled the mind and empire to Olympus.

And whoever is occupied in the sacred labor of the art of Urania, if he is not stained by the bile of envy, will confess many more things.

<meta>catchword: Not</meta>

<summary>A celebratory Latin poem honoring Tycho Brahe and his patron, King Frederick II. It praises Brahe for revolutionizing astronomy, guiding navigators to the Indies and China, and establishing a new cosmological system that surpasses those of Ptolemy and Copernicus.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Frederick II of Denmark, Urania, Tychonic system, navigation, Aristotle, Copernicus, Ptolemy, astronomy, Latin poetry, maritime exploration</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the celebratory poem dedicated to Tycho Brahe, transitioning from his legacy in Denmark to his arrival in the German lands under the patronage of Holy Roman Emperor Rudolph II. The previous page established Brahe's fame as a "Prince of Astronomers" who made his fatherland eternal through his celestial studies.</meta>

Nor will our ungrateful Germany allow dark oblivion to seize you: for she, too eager to see you, has complained that you were gone for seven periods of three years <note>A "trieterid" is a three-year period; seven of these equal twenty-one years, the approximate duration of Tycho’s residency at his observatory on the island of Hven</note>. Saluting you fondly now that you are finally returned to her according to her prayers, she says: "Let him not be absent, a guest sent to me not without the will of the Gods."

She grieves enough that you, an exile to the world, endured the frosts of the stiffening Baltic; that you were given to the cold Great Bear <note>original: "Trioni," referring to the Septentriones or the Big Dipper, signifying the far North</note> and the stubbornly harsh North Wind <note>Boreas</note> to foster the sacred home of **Urania** <note>The Muse of Astronomy</note>. Be present now with your fertile divine power, and put forth the beam of your more serene countenance through the German cities.

Not even Penelope rejoiced so to see the long-suffering Ulysses, nor the boy Telemachus who first recognized his parent, nor the trembling mothers; as our Germany rejoices that you have come into this, her own kingdom, where there is an illustrious theater for your virtue and a patron who owes much to your genius.

When the kindness of Emperor **RUDOLPH**, joined with Majesty, hears that you have departed from a fatherland that did not sufficiently value such great monuments of its own honor, and that you brought the choir of **Urania** with you, he will strive of his own accord to join this little laurel to his own Scepters and triumphs. He will cherish both you and your arts with a kindly face.

We know how the noble offspring born of the heavens of Austria <note>The Habsburg dynasty</note> joins together both the Muses and iron-clad Mars <note>War</note> in both sets of times: how, having recently crushed the insolent pride of the Turkish leader <note>original: "Threïcii ducis," referring to the Ottoman Sultan</note> with his soldiery, he seeks to be refreshed in the gardens of **Urania** while seeking to ease his labors.

With me, the refined Academy of Vienna will praise the twin Alberts; the father and the son, who, devoted to the stars, carried a mind burning with sacred fire above the heights: from which he took the surname of **ASTRONOMER**. Should I be silent about the lineage of Ernest, or you, Frederick, the long-standing glory of the race and of the German Scepter?

Under this patron, **Johannes Regiomontanus** <note>A famous 15th-century German astronomer and mathematician</note> shone, the glory of **Urania**: whom Fate and fatal envy removed from the earth, taken away by a death too hurried. He gave us triangles; he gave us refined **Mechanics** <term>Mechanicen: the art of building machines and instruments</term>. Indeed, Nuremberg stood amazed at him as a builder, and the Emperor himself marveled, while he beheld a wooden bird <note>A legendary mechanical eagle said to have been built by Regiomontanus to fly and greet the Emperor</note> flying in the middle of the sky. Believe it, posterity! A creation escaped from its enclosures through the void, like the son fleeing with his father <note>An allusion to Daedalus and Icarus</note>.

What can the hands of a Daedalus not do? And that mind "cooked" in the sacred science of Phoebus <note>Apollo, god of the sun and knowledge</term>, into which Prometheus instills a particle of more divine celestial genius? O Tycho, too blessed is the land which will see the miracles of your genius, and your **Pyronomic** arts <term>Pyronomas: "fire-managing," referring here to chemical or alchemical analysis through heat</term>, and your right hand reaching for Medicine in the midst of diseases.

You are indeed the leader of **Urania’s** troop; no less refined in the study of all noble sciences and arts. O happy is he to whom it will be granted to compare the harmony of the terrestrial globe from your lips to the sky! So that here and there there may be stars and their own Planets for both worlds. You, powerful both in the **Chemical** art <note>Tycho was a practitioner of "spagyric" or chemical medicine</note> and in metals, often command the yarn-spinning goddesses <note>The Fates</note> to produce threads for a life cut short, and you often bid them row past the empty boat of Charon <note>The ferryman of the underworld; meaning Tycho's medicine cheats death</note>.

To you, those whom the frosts of Norway and the horrid Great Bear press upon, and the Danish kingdoms, will one day repay thanks to you who deserve it—if indeed any ages will perhaps repay favor to the good, and if envy allows you to be heaped with the honor you merit.

Those enslaved by the groaning of gout will weep for you; everywhere those consumed by wasting disease will complain with tear-drenched faces that you have departed from your ancestral kingdoms. We, however, congratulating you from here, sing a votive melody, and we gaze upon your longed-for face and kiss your hands, desired for so long.

You seven columns of the Imperial crown <note>The seven Prince-electors of the Holy Roman Empire</note>, O leaders and kings, whom celestial ardor cooks with a more noble fire: I call upon you in the name of the sciences, and with me the choir of **Urania**: and the whole crown of the **Hyades** <note>original: "Hiantidum," a poetic term for stars</note>, which must be restored under your protection, requires help.

Do not allow this glory to be snatched away from the borders of Germany by the Italian cities, or the distant British, or the Western Franks <note>The French</note>. From that side, rival France watches you from the corner of her eye; the Italians will want you to have been negligent of praise; and with a steady, watchful eye, Holland, fertile in men, and the furthest Britons take note of you: the more northern Scot marks you, and the bold Iberians <note>Spaniards and Portuguese</note>, swollen with the discovery of the Ocean.

Let each look to what they pant for; I trust to see Germany shining, famous for this praise, above all others through the hospitality shown to **Urania**.

---

-> for the sake of devoted worship and love, made this. <-
